# Arxiv Papers in cs.CV on 2026-11-17
### BATMoS: Budget-Aware Guidance Allocation for Efficient Video Diffusion
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22028v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22028v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 00:39:35+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 00:39:35+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhixu Du, Kuo Yang, Jingwei Sun, Hai "Helen" Li, Yiran Chen
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: The effectiveness of a video diffusion model's inference budget depends on where computation is spent along the denoising trajectory. Across eight equal-budget inference configurations, VBench total scores span 4.80 points, highlighting the importance of explicit allocation design. Our key insight is that guidance evaluations are not equally valuable across diffusion time: retaining the sampling updates while selectively reusing guidance can preserve generation quality with fewer denoiser evaluations. We introduce BATMoS (Budget Aware Temporal Mixture of Strategies), which routes between fresh classifier-free guidance and cached guidance using a single pretrained denoiser. A magnitude-based temporal rule assigns guidance reuse to low-magnitude blocks and reserves fresh guidance for the remaining blocks, with a single integer controlling the computation budget. The resulting schedule requires no additional router training or per-budget threshold search. Evaluated on CogVideoX-2B with 4,720 videos per configuration across all sixteen official VBench dimensions, BATMoS achieves the highest total score among the eight allocation policies and comes within 0.10 points of full classifier-free guidance using 20\% fewer branch evaluations. BATMoS also composes with feature reuse, reducing computation from 80 to 62 effective branch evaluations with a 0.11-point decrease in total score. These findings make temporal guidance allocation a practical mechanism for extracting more generation quality from a fixed inference budget.



### RiDEx: Reward-Informed Directional Exploration for Diffusion Reinforcement Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22052v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22052v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 01:27:10+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 01:27:10+00:00
- **Authors**: Song Yan, Chenfeng Wang, Wei Zhai, Xingchen Wang, Tao Zhang, Yawen Shao, Shi Pan, Min Li, Zheng-Jun Zha
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Reward-based diffusion post-training updates model parameters from task rewards. While existing approaches improve sampling and policy optimization, they still rely largely on isotropic random perturbations in a high-dimensional latent space for exploration. Such variation often spends the perturbation budget on changes with little influence on the target reward, making exploration inefficient. We study where diffusion models should explore and how to learn from that exploration. We introduce Reward-Informed Directional Exploration (RiDEx), which concentrates local exploration in reward-relevant subspaces of the denoising velocity field. Conditional residuals or differentiable reward gradients propose the subspace, and equal-and-opposite perturbations at the same generation state measure its terminal reward response. The reward difference supplies signed local supervision in the explored coordinates, while all generated images contribute to global DiffusionNFT learning. RiDEx thereby couples exploration-space selection with feedback about the changes it produces. On Stable Diffusion 3.5 Medium, RiDEx attains the highest peak rewards among the evaluated methods on four alignment and preference tasks. Its gradient-direction instantiation achieves 15x and 29x target-attainment speedups over DiffusionNFT on HPSv2 and PickScore, measured in H200 GPU hours. Controlled experiments confirm that reward-aligned directions elicit stronger responses at equal perturbation magnitude, and that applying their measured credit improves curve-average reward across all four tasks.



### FM-Mamba: An Efficient Native 2D Selective State Space Model with Stability-Aware Discretization
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22061v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **eess.SY**, cs.AI, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22061v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 01:40:53+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 01:40:53+00:00
- **Authors**: Shengyuan Hu, Yiyong Sun, Weihang Shan, Yongzhe Chang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: When applied to two-dimensional features such as images, Mamba-based methods either flatten the feature map into 1D sequences and fuse multiple scans, which breaks spatial adjacency and multiplies computation, or recurse directly on the 2D grid but rescale the recurrence with a fixed input-independent factor and aggregate only the predecessors on a single anti-diagonal, leaving the discrete step unconstrained by 2D stability theory. We propose \textbf{FM-Mamba}, a native 2D selective state space model built on the general Fornasini--Marchesini (FM) model. Its coupled recurrence evolves along both spatial axes; cross-axis coupling induces a discrete diagonal-predecessor term $\tilde A_0$, and two independent input-dependent step sizes $Δ_1(i,j)$, $Δ_2(i,j)$ govern propagation along the two axes, so that the coefficients adapt per position and per direction. A normalized ratio $r_k=a_{0,k}/(a_{1,k}a_{2,k})$ yields a closed-form piecewise bound on the two step sizes; when a configuration falls outside the stable interval the steps are projected onto this bound, and the criterion is re-verified per position after training, which suppresses systematic local amplification without global damping. The recurrence is executed by a dual-diagonal wavefront scan: each update gathers information from two anti-diagonals, and the input enters the current state through a single projection, so that neither training nor inference flattens the feature map. Code is available at https://github.com/BitSsm/FM-Mamba-Runtime



### Data-Efficient Algal Bloom Segmentation in Freshwater Bodies Using a Three-Stage Transfer Learning Pipeline with Attention-Enhanced Decoding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22064v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22064v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 01:41:47+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 01:41:47+00:00
- **Authors**: Albert Ansah Benson, Mingqiang Han, Rahul Harsha Cheppally, Treman Singh Kaloya, Menard Soni, Sudan Baral
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 5 figures, 6 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Harmful algal blooms threaten water quality and public health, yet automated segmentation of bloom regions in freshwater imagery is limited by scarce pixel-level annotations. We present a transfer-learning pipeline that adapts a Swin Transformer U-Net with SCSE decoder attention from ImageNet-1K to water-body segmentation (RIWA, LaRS) and then to algal blooms, together with a model-assisted annotation protocol that expands the labelled algae corpus from 145 to 655 images. In a single-seed ablation on a 41-image validation split (checkpoint S2-A4 and its variants), corpus expansion is the dominant factor (+11 mIoU points); water-body pretraining helps only on the expanded corpus (+1.1 points over ImageNet initialization); SCSE attention adds 0.3 points; and a learnable positional encoding lowers mIoU by 0.6 points and is omitted. Adding 476 images, 188 of them algae-free, reduces false-positive area on non-water scenes from 22.6% to 4.9%. Against nine baselines on a 256-image test split, a separately trained checkpoint of the same architecture (S2-A13) reaches mIoU 0.919 and the highest algae IoU (0.902); a near-duplicate audit indicates train/test overlap in this split, so these scores are likely optimistic.



### Segmentation-Guided Direct Capacity Estimation of Rooftop Photovoltaics from Aerial Imagery
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22076v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22076v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 01:56:30+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 01:56:30+00:00
- **Authors**: Guibin Chen, Emma Fan, Yuzhang Lin
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Distribution planners need installation-level inventories of rooftop photovoltaic (PV) capacity, but interconnection records may be incomplete or spatially aggregated. Conventional image-based estimators segment PV arrays, convert the mask to physical area, and multiply by an estimated power density, coupling capacity prediction to mask-derived area. We propose Direct Head, a segmentation-guided architecture that retains U-Net segmentation and soft-mask pooling while directly regressing nonnegative capacity from PV-region features and area descriptors. We evaluate three independent runs on 7,590 French rooftop installations under the original split and a department-based holdout. Relative to a parameter-matched area-density neural model, Direct Head reduces mean absolute error by 8.7% and 12.5%, and symmetric mean absolute percentage error by 14.0% and 20.7%, respectively. Comparisons with conventional estimators, controlled architecture variants, auxiliary-loss ablations, and representative cases show that segmentation provides useful spatial evidence without requiring capacity to be determined by an area-density product.



### CRA: Continuous Risk Assessment for Safety-critical Visual Navigation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22080v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV, eess.SY
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22080v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 02:02:06+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 02:02:06+00:00
- **Authors**: Lihao Zheng, Ying Wu, Chen Jiang, Randy Freeman
- **Comment**: Project page: https://isaac-restaurant-cra.cra-demos.workers.dev
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Traditional safety-critical control methods often rely on predefined constraints that separate states into safe and unsafe regions. While recent studies have extended geometric safety toward semantic safety, real-world safety is rarely purely binary. The risk associated with an object or human can vary continuously with its physical properties, like motion, and interaction context; for example, a person carrying a heavy object or a hot object moving at high speed may require a different safety response from an ordinary static obstacle. In this paper, we propose a vision-based continuous risk assessment framework for safety-critical visual navigation. Our method uses RGB and LiDAR observations to estimate four risk factors (kinetic, potential, contact-severity, and thermal) and combines them into a continuous risk score for each assessed object or person. These estimates are integrated into a Poisson safety function with a Laplace guidance field as a safety filter, enabling online monitoring and intervention in open-world robot navigation. We demonstrate the proposed method in a simulated restaurant scene.



### When Lower Harm Misleads: Auditing Concept Corrections
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22084v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22084v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 02:10:17+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 02:10:17+00:00
- **Authors**: Tianyou Lai, Wentao Yue, Hongji Li, Chaoyuan Hao, Qilei Li
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: An expert who corrects a concept prediction, such as a BI-RADS descriptor in breast ultrasound, expects the diagnosis to improve. Objectives that reduce the harm of such corrections are judged by harm and accuracy, yet harm also falls when a model responds less to every correction or starts from a worse uncorrected prediction, the reference for harm. We propose a correction audit, a reproducible evaluation protocol that separates these explanations: it splits each correction into harm and useful gain on identical events, separates the starting prediction from the correction response, and compares objectives with amplitude baselines matched in source harm. On three breast-ultrasound sources, worst-case correction training, evaluated from the mean-case model's start, lowers harm but gives up more useful gain in all 21 source comparisons pooled over six seeds, raising the corrected negative log-likelihood (NLL); from the same start, selecting between the objectives by harm picks the less helpful model on all three sources. On CUB, a monotonic harm penalty at its reported weight lowers path-level harm in all 80 concept sets while raising the NLL in all 80, almost entirely before any correction. At a fixed start, rescaling both responses to the same source harm reproduces the penalty's mean harm, useful gain and NLL on single-concept corrections within a prespecified margin of 0.0005 nat, although the two responses point in different directions. Lower harm therefore signals better corrections only when it is read together with useful gain, absolute risk and the start it is measured from.



### When AUC Meets Generative Reconstruction: A Unified Principle for Balanced Visual Representation Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22094v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22094v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 02:24:09+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 02:24:09+00:00
- **Authors**: Boyu Han, Qianqian Xu, Shilong Bao, Zhiyong Yang, Qingming Huang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) require visual encoders that extract fine-grained visual features while constructing semantically disentangled and discriminative representations. Conventional methodologies encounter a fundamental trade-off: contrastive learning enhances inter-class discrimination by suppressing intra-class variability, whereas generative reconstruction emphasizes perceptual fidelity but lacks explicit comparative supervision. The naive aggregation of these objectives via composite loss functions often induces optimization conflicts and instability due to gradient interference within the shared latent space. To resolve this, we introduce a unified representation-learning paradigm that reformulates generative reconstruction as a discriminative ranking problem within the generative output manifold. Our approach maintains perceptual reconstruction as a supervisory signal and integrates a ranking-based discriminative constraint by systematically ordering matched and mismatched reconstruction outputs. This formulation embeds discriminative information seamlessly into the generative process, alleviating the risk of loss competition. Specifically, we propose AUC Generative Reconstruction (AUCGR), which optimizes the area under the ROC curve (AUC) by ensuring higher scores for matched pairs than for mismatched pairs, and Partial AUC Generative Reconstruction (pAUCGR), which addresses supervision bias by focusing on mismatched comparisons within reliable feature subspaces. The proposed objectives are designed to be plug-and-play, requiring no architectural modifications to existing generative reconstruction frameworks. Theoretical analysis establishes rigorous connections to AUC/pAUC optimization and provides generalization guarantees. Extensive experiments validate that our method advances both discriminative accuracy and fine-grained perceptual representation.



### CueSplat: Learning to Schedule Sparse Deformation for Dynamic Gaussian Splatting on Edge Devices
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22095v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22095v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 02:24:13+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 02:24:13+00:00
- **Authors**: Qingyuan Tang, He Zhu, Shixuan Sun, Anbang Wu, Jingwen Leng, Minyi Guo, Yu Feng
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 6 figures. Main paper only
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Deformation-based 4D Gaussian splatting (4DGS) enables dynamic scene rendering. However, evaluating deformation networks for all Gaussians at every frame is compute-intensive on edge devices. We find that Gaussian trajectories are often locally predictable, yet prediction reliability varies across Gaussians and time. Thus, we propose CueSplat, a post-training framework that exploits local temporal regularity while keeping the pretrained backbone frozen and retaining its Gaussian set. Specifically, CueSplat predicts Gaussian attributes from recent history and uses a lightweight learned router to select exact deformation updates within a scene-specific budget calibrated offline. Selection accounts for prediction reliability and rendering contribution. Selected indices are reused across nearby frames to reduce routing overhead, while selected Gaussians still receive exact updates each frame. To control accumulated prediction error, we use drift signals to schedule three consecutive full-deformation frames that rebuild the prediction history. Evaluated across four mainstream 4DGS algorithms, CueSplat achieves up to $6.74\times$ and $8.15\times$ speedups on Jetson AGX Orin and Orin NX, respectively, with a negligible quality drop (at most $0.16$ dB in dataset-average PSNR).



### Diagnosing and Correcting Skin-Tone Color Casts in Ultra-Lightweight Photo Retouching Networks
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22106v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22106v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 02:41:58+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 02:41:58+00:00
- **Authors**: Choulhyouc Lee
- **Comment**: Accepted to ACCV 2026. 32 pages including supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Color casts are expected to come from the scene or camera, and a retouching model removes them. We report a case where the model leaves one: an ultra-lightweight retouching network renders human skin greener than the original because it under-restores the skin chroma that a degradation removes and, even on clean inputs, adds a small hue shift of its own. A common paired-data-free recipe, synthetic self-supervision (degrade high-quality images, learn to restore them), silently produces this cast while whole-frame PSNR/SSIM stay essentially unchanged. In a representative 6,825-parameter histogram-conditioned network, the cast appears in 52 of 70 portraits under a ground-truth-anchored, white-balance-free protocol. Both a histogram-conditioned and a CNN-conditioned network develop the cast under synthetic self-supervision, yet both are near-neutral in-domain when trained on MIT-Adobe FiveK expert pairs. Image-level objectives miss it: a global-mean green loss worsens the cast (52/70 to 57/70). With zero added parameters, our per-pixel, skin-masked, asymmetric green-excess loss reduces the cast to 1/70 across stratified skin tones (68.1% to 2.2% over the 70,000-image FFHQ corpus; 64.4% to 0.8% on held-out CelebA-HQ photos). In-domain FiveK PSNR/SSIM are unchanged (+0.13 dB, +0.004), at the cost of a small whole-frame CIEDE2000 increase (8.77 to 9.69) and a magenta over-correction where skin is nearly neutral (skin Delta a* -1.5 to +4.4 and CIEDE2000 5.6 to 6.4 on clean inputs): the loss targets the signed green-magenta axis, not total skin-color fidelity. The same loss suppresses the cast across three ultra-light architectures. A whole-frame green metric correlates with skin-region CIEDE2000 at the method level (Spearman rho = 0.89). The final network exports to a 35 KB TFLite model.



### RetiDual-Select: Reliability-Aware Specialist-Generalist Routing for Retinal OCT under Image-Quality Shift
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22117v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22117v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 02:59:12+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 02:59:12+00:00
- **Authors**: Zihao Han, Philippe De Wilde
- **Comment**: 4 figures, 4 tables, 42 references. Submitted to the IEEE for possible publication. Main article only; the separate Supplementary Information is not included
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Adaptive fusion can repair a static ensemble's errors, but can also overturn correct decisions. RetiDual-Select combines retinal specialist RETFound with the image encoder of the medical vision-language model MedSigLIP, separating candidate fusion, routing safety and predictive review. Calibrated product-of-experts (PoE) fusion is the static reference, a small gate proposes an alternative for each image, a validation-frozen harm guard decides whether that candidate may replace the reference, and a separate score prioritizes review. Static PoE reached the highest or joint-highest clean accuracy on all three datasets. At a clean 2% accepted-error target the review policy accepted 94.3% of Kermany-4 images at 99.89% selective accuracy, and 92.4% of OCT-C8 at 98.65%. Both branches were correct on 95.0% and 91.8% of clean images; only 4.4% and 6.2% had exactly one correct branch. Across 15 prespecified synthetic conditions, adaptive gains were concentrated in severe contrast reduction, where this routing opportunity widened roughly ninefold; percentile rescaling largely removed the effect, linking it to compressed dynamic range. In post-hoc validation-only analyses, gain was higher when the gate followed the more reliable branch, and a frozen guard cut the worst-condition OCT-C8 loss from 1.39 to 0.36 percentage points. Adaptive routing was useful when image quality created a reliability opportunity and the gate followed it; otherwise the system retained Static PoE or deferred for review. The findings are bounded by the synthetic perturbations evaluated and do not establish robustness to natural acquisition or scanner shift.



### FrameSeg3D: Learned Local Frames for Segmenting Small Parts on High-Resolution 3D Scans
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22124v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.GR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22124v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 03:05:28+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 03:05:28+00:00
- **Authors**: Xinwen Zhang, Tongyan Wang, Huawei Lin
- **Comment**: 31 pages, 6 figures, 16 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Segmenting small parts on high-resolution 3D scans, such as nails on hand scans and teeth on intraoral scans, poses three challenges: 1) each part covers a tiny fraction of the scan, 2) its boundary is only a subtle change of height and curvature, and 3) it faces its own direction. Existing methods fall into two groups, and both fall short. The first group processes the whole scan at once and sees each part too coarsely. Point and mesh networks must shrink the scan to fit in memory, so a small part keeps only a few of its points. Multi-view methods render images of the whole object, so each part fills only a few pixels and a tilted part is seen from the side. The second group first finds each part and then segments it up close. But these methods find the parts with knowledge about one kind of part, for example the shape of the dental arch for teeth. Such knowledge does not carry over to other kinds of parts. We find that accuracy is limited by how a part is seen, from which direction and at what scale, not by how many points land on it. We therefore present FrameSeg3D. It first predicts a local frame for every part, its center and the direction it faces, from a low-resolution view of the whole scan. It then segments the part in views around its frame normal at the scanner's resolution, so covering the whole scan no longer costs resolution. Because every part arrives centered, facing the camera, and at a fixed scale, one segmentation network can serve different kinds of parts and learn from few labeled masks. We test FrameSeg3D on two kinds of parts, nails and teeth. It outperforms state-of-the-art methods on hand scans (mean Dice 0.970 on nails) and on the Teeth3DS+ test fold (per-tooth IoU 96.5), with the most accurate tooth boundaries (boundary IoU 70.5).



### Text-Guided Modality Correction for Open-Vocabulary Object Detection
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22126v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22126v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 03:07:08+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 03:07:08+00:00
- **Authors**: Yu Quan, Dong Zhang, Ping Gong, Peng Huang, JinHui Tang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Open-vocabulary object detection (OVD) is essential for detecting objects beyond the categories seen during training. Establishing vision-language associations is crucial for recognizing unseen categories, while integrating complementary visual features across levels supports detection at different scales. However, existing methods often provide insufficient textual guidance for object regions, rely on limited cross-modal alignment strategies, and underuse complementary features across levels, limiting their ability to detect unseen categories and objects of varying scales. To address these limitations, we propose a Text-Guided Modality Correction Network (TMCNet) that incorporates textual guidance into region feature extraction, cross-modal alignment, and multi-scale decoding to strengthen region-category semantic correspondence and promote complementary feature fusion across levels. First, a text-guided modality encoder incorporates textual semantics into queries to guide regional feature aggregation, reducing semantic ambiguity in object representations. Second, a cross-modal alignment module strengthens the correspondence between region representations and category semantics through interactions between visual and textual features. Finally, a progressive text correction decoder combines a top-down pathway with lateral connections to progressively fuse text-guided deep semantic features with shallow visual features at the corresponding scales, preserving both semantic information and spatial detail for objects of different sizes. Experiments on two public datasets, COCO-OVD and LVIS-OVD, demonstrate that TMCNet achieves strong performance in open-vocabulary object detection using simple text queries as guidance. Extending TMCNet to open-vocabulary segmentation also yields performance gains, demonstrating its cross-task applicability and extensibility.



### LoCaR: Localized Canvas Retrieval With Frozen Vision-Language Model
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22131v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22131v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 03:15:02+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 03:15:02+00:00
- **Authors**: Rahim Hossain, Dipanwita Saha, Ashirbad Mishra, Xinxin Shu, Svetlana Strunjas, Binbin Li
- **Comment**: Zero-shot retrieval , Local Retrieval
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Whole image embeddings can under represent small objects and local attributes that distinguish relevant images for localized queries. We present LoCaR, a training free framework for localized text to image retrieval with frozen vision language models. LoCaR constructs and utilizes independent proposals (SAM2) focusing on the object so as to get a more localized representation that independently encodes each region using the frozen visual encoder. We further study paired-support adaptation as an optional residual that uses matched text region exemplars without updating the underlying encoders. We also introduce LOCFG, a Visual Genome derived benchmark designed to evaluate localized retrieval for smaller and more challenging referents. Across several vision language backbones, our results show that improving the region representation itself is the main source of gain.



### DailyBrick: Language-Guided Synthesis of Brick Assembly Instructions
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22132v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22132v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 03:17:14+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 03:17:14+00:00
- **Authors**: Hang Zhang, Qijian Tian, Yuan Xie, Xuhong Wang, Xin Tan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Generating a LEGO model from a user request is more than a plausible 3D shape generation. LEGO models require physical support per brick, and each brick should be placed unblocked. Current methods address this issue by training an expert large language model. We reduce the problem of generating extremely long context on all brick connections to that of writing a program that builds LEGO models like a human, considering each intermediate step could be executed. We introduce DailyBrick , a multi-stage agentic system that produces a LEGO model together with a brick-by-brick assembly instruction for human building and robot assembly. We design a multi-stage harness with roles to help LLMs generate more effectively. A versioned construction SDK compiles multi-level decisions, from semantic subparts to individual bricks, into canonical operations. An external Meta-Reviewer agent replays and inspects the generated package, verifies the model and its construction process, identifies failures, traces them to the responsible stage, and routes them to bounded repairs or to editable candidates for later skill or tool updates. Extensive experiments demonstrate that DailyBrick generates LEGO models based on simple user requests better than recent agentic systems, and show its utility in downstream applications such as robot manipulations on model assembly from instructions and brick sets (https://SpatialAgent.github.io/DailyBrick/).



### CEVA: Complementary Evidence Verification via Frame-Level Reorganization for Short-Video Fake News Detection
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22141v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22141v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 03:27:36+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 03:27:36+00:00
- **Authors**: Wenshen Yang, Junfeng Hao, Jing Li, Decheng Liu,  Yangkunfei, Shuyu Jiang, Haizhou Wang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Short-video platforms are now a common way for the public to follow unfolding events. Their rapid, visually persuasive format also makes fake news easier to spread. Unlike text-image posts, short-video news interleaves multiple modalities and changing scenes, making it difficult to identify which frames verify a claim. Existing detectors either pool sampled frames homogeneously or select them by text-frame similarity. Both overlook that aligned frames may repeat a misleading claim, whereas less dominant context may challenge it. We present CEVA (Complementary Evidence Verification Approach), to our knowledge the first short-video fake news detection framework to explicitly organize frames by their evidential roles before multimodal fusion. Its Dual-Branch Complementary Reorganization (DBCR) module separates explicit-alignment frames from candidate complementary-context frames, and cross-modal fusion with bidirectional contrastive regularization operates on the reorganized evidence. On FakeSV and FakeTT, CEVA achieves the best overall results among the compared methods. Under fixed frame budgets and backbones, DBCR outperforms uniform, random, and similarity-based selection and improves two structurally different detectors. Together, these results establish pre-fusion evidence organization as an effective and transferable component of short-video fake news detection.



### EgoHandWAM: Probing the Role of Hand Motion in Egocentric World Action Modeling
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22151v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22151v1)

- **Authors**: Kailin Wang, Haoxiang Jie, Yaoyuan Yan, Zhiyou Heng, Zhaosong Li
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: How much does hand motion tell us about what a first-person camera will see next? EgoHandWAM forecasts three-dimensional wrist and fingertip trajectories and a future visual feature from the current image and recent hand states. We train on 751,065 EgoDex windows using a frozen DINOv2 encoder. On held-out development sessions, the action-only model reduces two-second wrist endpoint error from 60.26 to 56.48~mm relative to a stationary forecast. It still underestimates long reaches. Adding future-feature supervision gives nearly identical trajectory accuracy. With the recorded hand history, the visual head reduces smooth L1 error by 1.32\% relative to copying the current feature. Shuffling histories across sessions erases this gain. In a separate four-task probe, adding recorded future displacements to observed context and future tracking-validity masks reduces visual-feature error by 3.93\%. Masks alone give little improvement. A trajectory predicted from the same observations adds little. On two simulated RoboTwin tasks, substituting aligned human examples for an auxiliary batch of robot examples gives no consistent increase in task success across three training seeds.



### Unsupervised Multi-modal Medical Image Registration via Invertible Translation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22152v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22152v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 03:34:39+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 03:34:39+00:00
- **Authors**: Mengjie Guo, Jinming Duan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In medical imaging, the alignment of multi-modal images plays a critical role in providing comprehensive information for image-guided therapies. Despite its importance, multi-modal image registration poses significant challenges due to the complex and often unknown spatial relationships between different image modalities. To address this, we introduce a novel unsupervised translation-based multi-modal registration method, termed Invertible Neural Network-based Registration (INNReg). INNReg consists of an image-to-image translation network that converts multi-modal images into mono-modal counterparts and a registration network that uses the translated mono-modal images to align the multi-modal images. Specifically, to ensure the preservation of geometric consistency after image translation, we introduce an Invertible Neural Network (INN) that leverages a dynamic depthwise convolution-based local attention mechanism. Additionally, we design a novel barrier loss function based on Normalized Mutual Information to impose constraints on the registration network, which enhances the registration accuracy. The superior performance of INNReg is demonstrated through experiments on two public multi-modal medical image datasets, including MRI T1/T2 and MRI/CT pairs. The code is available at https://github.com/MeggieGuo/INNReg



### Centroid Spectral Coupling: Implicit Alignment and Representation Diversity in Self-Supervised Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22158v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG, stat.ML
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22158v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 03:41:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 03:41:50+00:00
- **Authors**: M. Hadi Sepanj, Benyamin Ghojogh, Saed Moradi, Paul Fieguth
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Self-Supervised Learning (SSL) aims to align augmented views while preserving representation diversity and preventing collapse. Existing non-contrastive methods often address these goals using multiple weighted loss terms. We introduce Centroid Spectral Coupling (CSC), a single-term SSL objective that implicitly couples view alignment, representation diversity, and collapse resistance. CSC constructs centroids from independently normalized augmented-view pairs and applies a ridge-based spectral objective to their uncentered second-moment matrix. Preserving centroid magnitudes encodes view agreement while encouraging balanced representation energy across spectral directions. We establish an exact decomposition into nonnegative alignment and spectral-imbalance components, characterize the geometry of global optima, and derive finite-loss guarantees for alignment and diversity. We also show that complete constant-output collapse is a strict saddle in normalized representation space. Experiments on CIFAR-10, CIFAR-100, and Imagenette show that CSC achieves the highest linear Top-1 accuracy among the evaluated non-contrastive methods on all three benchmarks, and the highest kNN Top-1 accuracy on both CIFAR datasets. A ridge-parameter sensitivity study reveals an empirical alignment--diversity trade-off: increasing $κ$ strengthens view alignment but reduces representation effective rank. These results show that a unified spectral objective yields competitive visual representations without explicit contrastive negatives or separately weighted alignment and diversity penalties.



### HandRSI: Recursive Self-Improvement for Hand Pose Estimation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22164v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22164v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 03:55:44+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 03:55:44+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhekai Wang, Luqing Luo, Wenhao Yao, Junjie Lu, Huanan Zhu, Feifei Gao, Yuhua Jiang
- **Comment**: Technical report. 25 pages, 17 figures, 18 tables. Code: https://github.com/GinKenneth/Hand_RSI
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Egocentric 3D hand pose estimators still miss hands at the image border, misplace occluded hands and jitter. Such failures are usually fixed by hand-written inference refinements and by fine-tuning, two interdependent steps that are tuned separately. We present HandRSI, a recursive self-improvement framework for a hand pose system that consists of a video estimator and its executable inference harness. In each round, coding agents refine the harness, and a change is accepted only if it improves a composite of all ten benchmark metrics on held-out data without significantly regressing any of them. The accepted harness then guides the estimator through selective distillation, which supervises hand presence and temporal consistency on frames without ground truth, while ground truth supervises pose. Since the updated estimator may change which refinements remain useful, the harness is re-validated on it. Starting from the released ACE-Ego-Hand model, five rounds reduce MPJPE-p by 35% on HOT3D and 11% on ARCTIC and roughly halve jitter. On HOT3D, HandRSI's MPJPE-p is 16% lower than that of ACE-Ego-Hand fine-tuned on the same ground truth. Under a unified protocol, it outperforms nine re-run published methods on all ten metrics of both benchmarks. Hypervolume selection on the accuracy-speed front yields Lite, Medium and Heavy systems running at 20.7 to 41.1 FPS. This report gives the full method, every stage of every round and the complete protocol. Code, evaluator, harnesses and weights are public.



### Not All Evidence Is Admissible: Evidence Governance for Tool-Augmented Medical Agents
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22169v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22169v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 04:01:28+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 04:01:28+00:00
- **Authors**: Runxun Zhang, Onofrio Antonio Catalano, Jinqiao Wang, Jingwei Wei
- **Comment**: 11 pages
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Tool-augmented medical agents treat whatever their tools return as evidence. We show that this default is costly: medical tools fail silently, and a frozen reasoner cannot ignore what enters its context, so many cases it would have answered correctly are flipped to wrong once tool outputs are added. We call this evidence hijacking. $\mathcal{V}$-information explains the phenomenon: the reasoner has lost the option to ignore, so a tool output can carry negative usable information, and moving the decision of what to keep outside the reasoner restores that option. The theory further shows that part of any filtering gain comes merely from letting the reasoner see less, and only selection by content lets it see better. Building on this, we recast tool-augmented reasoning as evidence governance, which separates deciding what counts as evidence from the reasoner that answers, and propose context-independent evidence governance (CIEG), the first medical agent framework that rules, after acquisition, on whether each individual tool output may enter as evidence. Every tool output is written as a card that can be checked against the raw input; an independent checker that sees only the input and the cards decides which cards become evidence; and the reasoner answers from the admitted cards alone, with no training anywhere. On medical question answering, fundus glaucoma screening, and chest X-ray pneumonia classification, it achieves the highest accuracy among models of comparable scale, and significantly outperforms state-of-the-art tool-using agents and multi-agent systems built on the same backbone at lower cost.



### CueClash: Frontier Vision-Language Models Judge 3-D Relations Close to the Picture Plane
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22180v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22180v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 04:17:35+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 04:17:35+00:00
- **Authors**: Soham Dan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: A vision-language model (VLM) can judge 3-D relations from the picture or from the ground layout. In photographs the two usually agree. We separate them with CueClash, rendered scenes in which the 2-D cue for each of five relations is right on exactly half the statements. The shortcut gap is accuracy when the cue is right minus accuracy when it is wrong. For Claude Opus 5.5, GPT-5.5 and Gemini 3.6 Flash at low reasoning effort it is +18 to +37 percentage points. It is +7 to +29 without two relations whose cue-wrong items are confounded with borderline cases or occlusion. Given ground coordinates and each relation's definition, they answer 99 to 100% correctly. Pixel measurements with camera depths remove most of the gap for objects fitting through openings. The failure lies in the step from the picture to the layout. The maps that the models draw of the scene from above mostly keep the depth order but, like the picture, shorten depth. GPT-5.5's maps stay close to a copy of the picture. Opus 5.5's and Gemini's maps undo about 0.6 of this shortening. Gemini's then shrink all depths by a further uniform factor. Opus 5.5 follows the picture mainly on fits, and its maps correct depth about as much as human observers of real scenes do. The three models also lose accuracy on CV-Bench photographs where a reliable 2-D cue misleads. In two open models the layout information reaches the language model, but their maps lose much of it.



### SRED: Sparse Regional Evidence Distillation Across Magnifications in Whole-Slide Imaging
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22189v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22189v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 04:22:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 04:22:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhiyuan Yang, Jiahao Cheng, Vincent Quoc-Huy Trinh, Mahdi S. Hosseini
- **Comment**: CVPR 2027 submission
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In computational pathology, high-magnification whole-slide image analysis preserves tissue detail but requires encoding many patches, whereas lower magnification reduces computation by covering a higher field of view per patch while sacrificing detail. Existing cross-magnification distillation matches features that often encode detail unreachable by low-magnification input, sometimes requiring slide-level labels that limit scalability. To address those issues, we introduce Sparse Regional Evidence Distillation (SRED), which transfers regional event occurrences that encode reachable high-level interactions between details without slide-level labels. We use a sparse autoencoder (SAE) to learn an event dictionary from 20x teacher features. Logical OR pooling records whether each event is detected in any spatially aligned patch, retaining both localized and shared evidence within the corresponding 5x region. A pretrained 5x encoder is fine-tuned to predict these regional targets without slide-level labels. Visual analysis identifies dictionary components responsive to recognizable histological patterns, supporting target interpretation. Compared with native 5x features, SRED improves average macro-F1 by 1.1 points across seven classification and grading cohorts and average C-index by 1.7 points across five survival cohorts. Runtime matches native 5x, with an average 11.58x speedup over 20x.



### MIRAGE: Motion Identity Reduction via Autoregressive Generative Encoding for Privacy-Preserving Skeleton-based Motion Data
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22200v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22200v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 04:39:03+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 04:39:03+00:00
- **Authors**: Thomas Carr, Xujun Che, Depeng Xu, Shuhan Yuan, Aidong Lu
- **Comment**: 14 pages, 3 figures, 12 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Skeleton motion identifies people through body proportions and movement style, and skeleton streams are increasingly passed to third-party analytics that run action classifiers trained on raw data. A prior adversarial anonymizer adds a per-frame residual in the raw coordinate space but sees one frame at a time; motion retargeting needs a target skeleton and complete sequences, which rules out online use. We present MIRAGE, a target-free, causal anonymizer that adds an identity-reducing residual to each incoming frame in the raw coordinate space, computed by attention over past frames. One causal, optionally windowed mask governs every attention in a rotary-position encoder-decoder, and key/value caches let the trained function run frame by frame; on all 56,578 NTU 60 sequences, batched and streaming outputs differ by at most 7.2e-6 m. Reported results use an unbounded window, so per-stream state grows with stream length. We evaluate against a passive adversary that applies raw-trained identity models and an adaptive adversary that trains on the anonymizer's output, treating the adaptive adversary as the headline, on NTU RGB+D 60 over five generator seeds, alongside the per-frame residual anonymizer of Moon et al. trained identically. When consumers retrain on the released data, MIRAGE keeps 83.9% action recognition (raw 89.1%; Moon et al. 88.7%) while a retrained identity classifier reaches 43.4% re-identification (RI) over 40 subjects, against 69.4% for Moon et al. and 75.4% on raw data. The strongest adaptive attacker still reaches 49.3% (chance 2.5%), so MIRAGE reduces identity leakage; it does not anonymize. Against raw-trained (frozen) classifiers, MIRAGE lowers RI below the strongest retargeting baseline (10.36% vs. 22.09%), but frozen action recognition falls to 37.04% (vs. 75.82%), so at full residual scale it is not a drop-in replacement for raw input to existing frozen models.



### ARG-Avatar: Animation-Rendering Guidance for Efficient Feedforward Avatar Reconstruction
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22207v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.GR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22207v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 04:45:07+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 04:45:07+00:00
- **Authors**: Jianjin Xu, Soham Dasgupta, Fernando De la Torre
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Reconstructing animatable 3D human avatars from images is important for applications such as gaming and augmented reality. Feedforward methods make avatar reconstruction accessible by reducing inference time to a few seconds, but recent approaches rely heavily on scaling model capacity, training data, and computation. We ask whether such scaling is necessary. To this end, we introduce ARG-Avatar, an efficient feedforward framework with only 68M trainable parameters that achieves SOTA reconstruction quality. Our key insight is that 3D animation and rendering already provide strong geometric correspondences that existing methods largely attempt to learn from data. In light of this, we design the Foreground Avatar Coordinate RoPE attention (FACRoPE) and Intermediate Token Rendering (ITR) pipeline to guide avatar tokens to attend to their corresponding patches. Experiments across multiple datasets show that ARG-Avatar consistently outperforms prior methods when using two or more input images. Compared with LHM++, ARG-Avatar uses approximately 9$\times$ fewer trainable parameters, 4.8$\times$ fewer training sequences, and 14$\times$ less training compute, while running 6$\times$ faster and achieving a 2.7 dB PSNR improvement on the out-of-distribution NeuMan benchmark.



### Joint-ID: Transformer-based Joint Image Enhancement and Depth Estimation for Underwater Environments
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22212v1
- **DOI**: 10.1109/JSEN.2023.3338730
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22212v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 04:49:09+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 04:49:09+00:00
- **Authors**: Geonmo Yang, Gilhwan Kang, Juhui Lee, Younggun Cho
- **Comment**: 11 pages. (c) 2023 IEEE. Personal use of this material is permitted. Permission from IEEE must be obtained for all other uses, in any current or future media, including reprinting/republishing this material for advertising or promotional purposes, creating new collective works, for resale or redistribution to servers or lists, or reuse of any copyrighted component of this work in other works
- **Journal**: IEEE Sensors Journal, vol. 24, no. 3, pp. 3113-3122, 2024
- **Summary**: Underwater imaging is a challenging task due to factors such as scattering, absorption, and turbulence, which degrade image quality and limit visibility. In this paper, we propose a novel approach for enhancing underwater images that leverages the benefits of joint learning for simultaneous image enhancement and depth estimation. We introduce Joint-ID, a transformer-based neural network that can obtain high-perceptual image quality and depth information from raw underwater images. Our approach formulates a multi-modal objective function that addresses invalid depth, lack of sharpness, and image degradation based on color and local texture. We design an end-to-end training pipeline that enables joint restoration and depth estimation in a shared hierarchical feature space. Additionally, we propose a synthetic dataset with various distortions and scene depths for multi-task learning. We evaluate Joint-ID on synthetic and standard datasets, as well as real underwater images with diverse spectra and harsh turbidity, demonstrating its effectiveness for underwater image enhancement and depth estimation. Furthermore, we demonstrate the ability of Joint-ID to perform feature matching and saliency detection for visually guided underwater robots. Our proposed method has the potential to improve the visual perception of underwater environments and benefit applications such as oceanography and underwater robotics. Supplements are available at: https://sites.google.com/view/joint-id/home



### ExplicitIR: Explicit Spatial Policy Fields for All-in-One Image Restoration
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22222v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22222v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 05:06:06+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 05:06:06+00:00
- **Authors**: Dezhi Zhang, Shangrong Yang, Yudong Liang, Wenjian Wang
- **Comment**: 13 pages, 10 figures, 4 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: All-in-one image restoration aims to recover clean images blindly from inputs with various degradation types and levels using a single model. However, the adaptation policy in most existing methods remains implicitly encoded in feature representations, leaving the restoration decision neither explicitly represented nor independently controllable. Since real-world degradations vary spatially, applying uniform updates across the entire image leads to over-smoothing of clean areas and under-correction of localized degradations. To address these limitations, we present ExplicitIR, which represents the restoration policy as two explicit per-pixel fields: Budget, which determines where and how strongly to update, and Style, which selects between narrow and wide context windows. Specifically, ExplicitIR incorporates a Spatial Policy Estimator (SPE) to predict these fields, explicitly representing the restoration policy and capturing spatially-varying degradations, which are then applied by Policy-Adaptive Decoder Blocks (PADB) through adaptive fusion of narrow- and wide-window responses, enabling independent control and precise local modulation. This design enables spatially-adaptive restoration in a single forward pass, without requiring task labels, prompts, or test-time degradation estimators. Extensive experiments on three-task and five-task benchmarks demonstrate that ExplicitIR achieves performance competitive with the strongest all-in-one baselines.



### TRIDENT: Efficient Triple-Task Learning of Dehazing, Depth, and Uncertainty Estimation for Underwater 3-D Robot Visual Perception
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22224v1
- **DOI**: 10.1109/JSEN.2024.3488495
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22224v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 05:08:06+00:00
