- **Authors**: Renjun Cai, Antonio Javier Gallego, Félix Fuentes-Hurtado
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We propose a self-configuring framework that extends the nnU-Net workflow to both segmentation and classification in 3D medical imaging. Building on nnU-Net's self-configuring method, the framework reuses representations learned by a segmentation model for image-level classification. Its encoder features and predicted segmentation probabilities are used by a lightweight classifier. Two information-sharing strategies are investigated. In the frozen-backbone branch, features are augmented with region-of-interest information, predicted foreground volume, and segmentation confidence. In the adaptation branch, the final encoder stage is partially fine-tuned for classification, with an auxiliary segmentation loss to preserve segmentation-related information. Additional five-fold experiments showed that more complex configurations did not consistently improve classification performance. On the public validation leaderboard, our selected framework achieved an Overall score of 0.8056. In the official FLARE 2026 Task 2 final evaluation, our framework ranked first overall, achieving the highest DSC, NSD, and AUROC across all four testing tasks, including two hidden testing tasks. Local inference measurements also remained below the challenge limits of $60 \mathrm{s}$ per case and $4 \mathrm{GB}$ GPU memory. These results demonstrate the practical value of reusing segmentation representations for classification while maintaining a simple and efficient framework.



### CoBoVLA: Decoupling Language from Action with Discrete Codes for Robust and Interpretable Robot Manipulation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22395v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22395v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 08:43:29+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 08:43:29+00:00
- **Authors**: Mehul Arora, Dhruv Agarwal, Ambuj Agrawal
- **Comment**: 16 pages, 3 figures, 4 tables, and supplemental material included
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision-Language-Action (VLA) models achieve high success rates on the LIBERO benchmark but remain sensitive to paraphrasing. Existing VLAs ranging from 0.6B to 7.5B parameters experience a 22-52 point performance drop on LIBERO-Para while SmolVLA exhibits an even larger decline, from 83\% to 8\%. Because most VLAs jointly learn language understanding and action generation, it is difficult to determine whether a failure originates from misunderstanding an instruction or executing it incorrectly. To address these challenges, we introduce CoBoVLA, or CodeBookVLA, a 250M-parameter VLA that decouples language understanding, planning, and control through learned discrete interpretable codes. A text encoder maps the instructions to shared step codes, which a visual planner then converts into action code sequences executed by a language-independent actor. This design improves robustness to instruction paraphrasing while allowing failures to be traced to individual stages. It also enables efficient real-time control on resource-constrained hardware by encoding instructions only once and using compact discrete representations to keep each component lightweight. CoBoVLA achieves 91.4\% success on LIBERO-Goal, compared with 82.8\% for SmolVLA, under a matched protocol, with 17 ms per control step latency. On LIBERO-Para, it achieves 81.1\% across 4,092 unseen paraphrases and 78.5\% on matched episodes, compared with 36.5\% for SmolVLA LoRA fine-tuned on paraphrases. Without two-step demonstrations, CoBoVLA completes 48.8\% of 160 free-form two-step tasks. Finally, success reaches 91\% when instructions are correctly encoded but falls to 27.2\% otherwise, identifying language understanding as the main source of remaining failures. Together, these results demonstrate that decoupling language understanding and control can improve instruction robustness while making VLA failures easier to diagnose.



### SignAligner: Dual-Granularity Span Generation for Sign Language Alignment
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22409v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.CL
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22409v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 08:47:09+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 08:47:09+00:00
- **Authors**: Jongbhin Woo, Youngjoon Jang, Joon Son Chung
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 5 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Large sign-language corpora benefit from temporal labels at two scales: subtitle spans for subtitle alignment and word spans for sign spotting. Sign spottings already locate words in time at corpus scale, but training subtitle alignment jointly on subtitle and word spans remains underexplored. We present SignAligner, a prompt-conditioned visual-language span generator that maps a sign-language video and a subtitle-sentence or target-word query to a textual start/end frame interval. It is first trained jointly on subtitle spans and word spans from sign spottings, then refined with overlap-based rewards while retaining auxiliary word examples. On the subtitle test set of the British Broadcasting Corporation-Oxford British Sign Language (BOBSL) corpus, the refined model reaches 66.68 F1@0.50 (F1 with a temporal intersection-over-union threshold of one half), the best among compared systems without external sign-video resources. This overlap-reward refinement outperforms cross-entropy continuation from the same jointly trained model. Joint training with word spans improves subtitle alignment in a pair of our generators matched in code, labels and maximum update budget. The advantage remains after both models of the pair are refined with overlap rewards. For word queries, the refined model follows the queried word, not a fixed position in the video: when the word's sign lies away from the video centre, it is far more accurate than predictions that use position alone. Together, these findings support one shared span generator refined with overlap rewards, aligning subtitles and producing word spans for human review.



### SpatialMind: Adaptive Multi-Level Geometric Fusion for Spatial Reasoning in Multimodal Large Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22415v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.CG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22415v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 08:51:37+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 08:51:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Shibin Zhao, Hao Tan, Fan Yang, Haixin Wang, Feilong Chen, Yidong Ma, Chaoyang Zhao, Ming Tang, Jinqiao Wang
- **Comment**: Includes supplementary material. Code available at https://github.com/zhaosb-star/SpatialMind
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) demonstrate promising capabilities in 2D visual understanding, yet remain limited in 3D spatial reasoning. Recent studies explore integrating 3D reconstruction models into MLLMs. However, these approaches either use a single geometric granularity across all tasks or adopt a fixed multi-level fusion scheme, regardless of task-specific reasoning requirements. Through systematic experiments on multi-level geometric fusion, we find that spatial tasks at different cognitive levels (e.g., distance estimation vs. route planning) require geometric representations of different granularities, revealing that fixed fusion cannot accommodate diverse spatial reasoning requirements. To bridge this gap, we propose SpatialMind, a task-adaptive framework for dynamically integrating multi-level geometric representations. SpatialMind employs GeoFus, a multi-layer geometric fusion module built around a gating network jointly conditioned on textual instructions, visual semantics, and geometric representations. This network adaptively modulates the contributions of shallow, intermediate, and deep geometric features across the layers of the LLM decoder. Furthermore, we introduce a negative-entropy regularization loss to mitigate gate collapse. Gate visualizations indicate that GeoFus adapts geometric granularity to task requirements. Experiments demonstrate that SpatialMind achieves competitive performance across spatial benchmarks, attaining a state-of-the-art score of 72.3 on VSI-Bench. The source code is publicly available at https://github.com/zhaosb-star/SpatialMind.



### CutAgent: Creative-Gene-Driven Planning and Experience-Guided Self-Refinement for High-CTR E-commerce Advertising Video Creation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22418v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22418v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 08:54:39+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 08:54:39+00:00
- **Authors**: Xuyue Huang, Guanbo Huang, Jiasong Feng, Run Ling, Ao Ma, Xi Zhao, Yaoyu Li, Jie Gao, Ke Cao, Wei Feng, Zhanjie Zhang, Hao Wang, Jinghe Hu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: E-commerce advertising video creation requires both high-level creative planning and reliable multi-modal production. However, existing methods often map product attributes directly to scripts or editing decisions without explicitly modeling advertising strategies, narrative structures, and production styles. They also have limited ability to reuse past production failures. To address these limitations, we propose $\textbf{CutAgent}$, a dual-agent framework that formulates advertising video creation as a Plan--Execute--Self-Evolve process. CutAgent comprises two collaborative agents, $\textbf{GeneAgent}$ and $\textbf{VideoAgent}$, connected by a structured creative gene that bridges product understanding and video production. GeneAgent maps multi-modal product inputs---textual attributes together with product images---to creative genes that specify narrative units and production rules. To train GeneAgent, we construct $\textbf{CutAgent-70K}$ with approximately 70K samples of product attributes/images, reverse-extracted creative genes, cleaned clip pools, and real-world CTR, and apply high-CTR supervised fine-tuning followed by CTR-guided GRPO. Guided by the generated gene, VideoAgent retrieves category-then-gene failure--correction experiences from a long-term memory as constraints for shot-script planning, then coordinates material retrieval, narration, subtitles, music, and timeline rendering. Independently, a reflection loop diagnoses quality defects, revises deficient production decisions, and writes verified corrections back to memory for later retrieval. Experiments on $\textbf{CutAgent-Eval}$ show that CutAgent outperforms representative baselines in e-commerce advertising video creation, achieving the highest online CTR of $\textbf{2.21\%}$.



### Do Diffusion Models Learn to Generalize Basic Visual Skills?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22427v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22427v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:01:19+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:01:19+00:00
- **Authors**: Amish Sethi, Boya Zeng, Wenhao Chai, Zhuang Liu
- **Comment**: NeurIPS 2026. Project page: https://amishsethi.github.io/visual-generative-lab/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Diffusion models generate high-quality images, but how well they generalize is hard to measure. Current evaluations rely on human preference and prompt-following benchmarks for text-to-image models and on distribution-level metrics such as Fréchet Inception Distance (FID) and Inception Score (IS) for class-conditional models, but these cannot tell whether models learn basic visual skills or largely match training patterns. To address this gap, we introduce the Visual Generative Lab (VGL), a controlled experimental framework for studying how diffusion models learn and generalize basic visual skills, including size, position, rotation, count, color, shape, and their compositions. By generating synthetic training data for each skill from explicit rules, VGL allows precise measurement of generalization with task-specific, rule-based metrics. Training models from scratch on these data reveals three patterns in this controlled setting: (1) with our default design, extrapolation is limited: size and position degrade within a few pixels of the training range, count reaches only about 50% accuracy on out-of-range counts, and rotation extrapolation accuracy varies widely across seeds; (2) for compositional generalization, the number of distinct basic visual skill combinations matters more than dataset size; (3) skills mostly fail independently, except position. Code and data-generation scripts: https://github.com/AmishSethi/visual-generative-lab.



### When Neural Collapse Fails to Route: Routing Drift in Continual Learning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22429v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22429v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:02:57+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:02:57+00:00
- **Authors**: Nicolas Larue
- **Comment**: Accepted at BMVC 2026. 36 pages, including supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Progressive neural-collapse classifiers can learn strong task-local decision boundaries while failing the global Class-IL decision. We identify the failure as routing drift: under an expanding equiangular tight frame (ETF) classifier, a held-out sample can remain separable within its true task block, yet receive its largest global score from another block. This produces high Task-IL accuracy but low Class-IL accuracy, and small replay buffers can mask the problem because stored samples are optimized repeatedly and often remain well routed. We formalize route accuracy directly from the global class argmax and use it to separate within-task discrimination from cross-task score comparability. This decomposition suggests a classifier-level correction: preserve the global ETF frame, but add class-local reliability evidence when transported class measures deviate from the neural-collapse ideal. We develop Reliability-Calibrated Routing (RCR), a lightweight post-hoc classifier that composes standardized ETF alignment with radial plausibility around transported, NC-shrunk class representatives. Across three datasets and two fixed replay-buffer budgets, this NC-consistent correction gives the best Class-IL FAA on five of six settings and reduces Class-IL forgetting on all six. The largest gains occur in harder routing regimes: up to $+6.75$ FAA over the reported reference, and up to $+8.03$ FAA and $+8.62$ final route accuracy in paired evaluations, while preserving Task-IL performance. These results position routing drift as a measurable failure mode of ETF-based continual learning and show that reliability-calibrated class evidence can repair part of the lost global routing without retraining the representation. Code and evaluation artifacts are available at https://towzeur.github.io/RCR/.



### Where Demand Meets Supply: Spatial Elastic Consensus for Low-Bandwidth Collaborative Perception
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22439v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22439v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:11:58+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:11:58+00:00
- **Authors**: Linshan Yan
- **Comment**: 10 pages
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Occlusions and limited sensor range make ego-only 3D perception insufficient for safe driving. Collaborative perception addresses this by enabling connected vehicles to share complementary observations, yet it remains constrained by tight vehicular communication budgets. While spatial selection is more flexible than fixed-rate feature compression, existing selectors remain largely sender-driven and waste scarce bandwidth on ego-visible targets. Furthermore, applying convolutions after masking spreads isolated tokens into their neighborhoods, silently violating low-bandwidth constraints. To address this, we propose Spatial Elastic Consensus (SEC), a bandwidth-adaptive framework centered on bilateral demand-supply consensus. Instead of unilateral token selection, the ego vehicle formulates a content-aware demand prior while each collaborator evaluates its local supply. This single-round consensus allocates the token budget to critical ego-missed evidence rather than ego-visible redundancy, without transmitting dense maps. To guarantee exact budget compliance, an anti-dilation refine-before-mask pipeline refines features prior to discrete token selection, preventing receptive-field leakage. A lightweight deformable sampler then aggregates these sparse messages with learned local offsets, compensating for the spatial context lost to token selection and mitigating inter-agent pose drift. Evaluated at a stringent 64KiB budget, SEC consistently achieves the best AP@0.7 on the simulated OPV2V and V2XSet datasets and the real-world DAIR-V2X dataset. These results demonstrate that combining bilateral consensus with budget-exact transmission is key to efficient low-bandwidth collaborative perception.



### The shape of individual differences in judgment: shared axes, personal weights, and when their spread crosses the consensus
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22440v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **q-bio.NC**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22440v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:12:19+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:12:19+00:00
- **Authors**: Hiroshi Higashi
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: People differ in how they judge faces, artworks, photographs and jokes. Previous work measured the size of these differences; we ask what shape they take. We wrote three candidate shapes as nested generative models (same-sign reweighting of shared dimensions, a linear transform that allows reversal, and stimulus-specific offsets) and compared how well they predict people unseen in training, using ten public datasets (about 20,000 raters). The models recovered a person's judgment space from 10-20 judgments and predicted judgments of unseen stimuli. For faces, reweighting was sufficient, and weights opposing the population were not statistically detected. For artworks, weights spread continuously along the shared abstract-figurative direction, and 28 percent of raters fell on the opposite side from the population. This tail was much smaller when a different rater sample judged the same artworks. Individual differences thus differ in shape as well as size, and the shape depends on domain and raters.



### As Student Rollouts Continue, Visual Backdoors Keep Growing
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22446v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22446v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:15:01+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:15:01+00:00
- **Authors**: Zane Fu, Yanzhao Zheng, Jinze Liu, Ziting Qin, Qianshan Wei, Jihuai Zhu, Yipeng Yu, Baohua Dong, Hangcheng Zhu, Ruohui Huang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Conventional knowledge distillation can facilitate the propagation of backdoors from teacher models to student models. However, how this risk manifests in On-Policy Distillation (OPD) remains insufficiently studied. OPD has recently emerged as an important post-training paradigm for Vision-Language Models (VLMs). Unlike traditional offline distillation, OPD does not require teacher-generated responses. Instead, it uses the teacher's probability distributions to supervise response trajectories generated online by the student. Since the initial student model typically generates benign responses and receives no explicit supervision from malicious target responses, OPD appears to offer some inherent resistance to backdoor propagation.   In this paper, we challenge this intuition and reveal a more subtle risk of visual backdoor propagation in OPD. Through experiments across multiple model families and datasets, together with theoretical analysis, we identify three main findings. First, unlike the relatively gradual propagation observed in traditional offline distillation, backdoors rarely emerge in OPD at low poisoning ratios. However, the attack success rate rapidly approaches 100% once a critical threshold is exceeded. Second, backdoor propagation does not depend on explicit malicious responses. Instead, it is driven by the continuous accumulation of anomalous probability mass assigned by the teacher. Finally, based on these findings, we propose a novel trigger design that enables visual backdoor propagation with zero explicit trigger injection. These findings further reveal an overlooked mechanism of backdoor propagation in OPD and highlight its potential security risks.



### MedImgAnnotator: batch wise medical image annotation and preprocessing tool
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22450v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, eess.IV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22450v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:17:28+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:17:28+00:00
- **Authors**: Ewunate Assaye Kassaw, Amit Mehndiratta, Peush Sahni, Anup Singh
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: The curation of large cohort datasets for developing deep learning models demands annotating or editing over the AI-predicted labels. However, openly available annotation tools are time-consuming, as they involve complex navigational steps and do not support batch processing. This study aimed to develop a simple, fast, semiautomated batch-annotation tool named MedImgAnnotator. The tool was created by integrating editing tools, image mask pair matching logic, preprocessing functions, object detection tool, and a plugin management system. Its performance was evaluated in terms of active annotation time and ease of use by the developer and 12 users using open-source MR and CT scans. Additionally, the tool had been used by the MICCAI 2026 CARE Challenge liver segmentation best performance winner to annotate liver masks from 6412 MRI volumes. The evaluation results showed that MedImgAnnotator reduced active annotation time from 206.5+/-32.87 (average active annotation time of openly available tools) seconds to 59 seconds. The Wilcoxon signed-rank test of 12 users evaluation results recorded P values of 0.032 and 0.008 for the active annotation time and higher ease of use rating metrics, respectively, indicating that the current tool significantly reduces annotation time and enhances ease of use. Moreover, the tool utilization for annotating different regions of interest from different modality scans and body regions shows its usability across modalities and areas of interest. MedImgAnnotator, which significantly reduces active annotation time for large imaging cohorts, is available as an executable file and as a Python script. Access link: https://ewunateassaye.github.io/developed-tools/medimgannotator.html



### Sample-Aligned Decoders for Latent Generative Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22458v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22458v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:21:58+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:21:58+00:00
- **Authors**: Yu Lei, Yi Li, Hongcheng Gao, Yipeng Wei, Liang Li, Li Su, Qingming Huang
- **Comment**: Project page: https://torpedo2003.github.io/SAD/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Latent generative models synthesize images by decoding generated latents, yet their decoder is trained only to reconstruct encoder latents and never sees the latents it decodes at test time, so the residual errors of the generator are rendered directly into the final image. Existing remedies retrain the generator, add cost at every sampling step, or make the decoder robust to perturbed real latents, assuming that generator errors resemble such perturbations. We find that they do not. Generated latents lie on the encoder latent manifold as closely as real ones, yet follow a different distribution. The decoder should therefore be adapted to the distribution of generated latents, which raises two challenges: (1) unpaired supervision, since a generated latent has no ground-truth image, and (2) latent-space drift, since a decoder freed from paired targets forgets the latent space it must decode. We propose Sample-Aligned Decoders (SAD), a post-training framework that fine-tunes only the decoder of a frozen generator at no inference cost. Generated-Sample Distribution Alignment matches decoded generated latents to unpaired real images in a frozen foundation-model feature space, and Encoder Anchor Preservation keeps the decoder close to the original one on encoder latents. Our analysis shows that the alignment is effective only on generated latents, where it corrects each sample in proportion to how much more often the generator produces such latents than the encoder; on encoder latents it can only work against the anchor. SAD improves four state-of-the-art ImageNet generators across three tokenizers, e.g., FID-50K from 1.15 to 0.98 for REPA-E, complements discriminator guidance, and extends to text-to-image generation. Our code and models are available at https://torpedo2003.github.io/SAD/.



### MAVE: Reconstructing Editable 3D Blockouts from Film Shots
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22464v2
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22464v2)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:25:47+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 18:35:59+00:00
- **Authors**: Yifan Chang, Jian Yang, Jiangnan Shao, Xiaofeng Wang, Zhaoxin Fan, Renjing Xu, Ming Lu, Xingang Wang, Zheng Zhu
- **Comment**: Project page: https://yfchang-cv.github.io/MAVE/. Code: https://github.com/yfChang-cv/MAVE
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Animated 3D blockouts support previsualization and provide structural conditions for video generation, but manually reconstructing them from reference videos is costly. This limits both their use by creators and the production of paired training data. We introduce MAVEBench, a benchmark of 50 film shots and 416 keyframes for evaluating camera motion, spatial layout, and character blocking. We propose MAVE, a video-to-blockout framework combining Metric observation, Agentic modeling, and evidence-based VErification. It first recovers cameras, human motion, and geometric evidence in a shared metric coordinate system. An agent then constructs an editable environment under constraints from these observations, while programmatic checks report inconsistencies to guide refinement. The outputs are editable Blender scenes and frame-aligned blockout videos. On MAVEBench, MAVE improves overall scores by 27.5-41.1 points across three agents over their corresponding vanilla baselines. With DeepSeek-1017, it achieves a score of 73.2 at an average API cost of only USD 0.054 per shot, outperforming the strongest vanilla baseline while reducing total reconstruction time by 53%. We also construct MAVEData-3K, a dataset of 3,000 blockout-video pairs for model training, and will release it publicly. Creators can change viewpoints and character paths, then render the edited scenes to condition video generation. Code is available at https://github.com/yfChang-cv/MAVE.



### Disagree to Prune: Reallocating Visual Tokens Where a Small VLM Dissents
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22472v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22472v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:30:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:30:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Yanchao Li, Jiaqing Xie, Ben Gao, Yusu Zhang, Beilun Wang, Yanbo Wang, Xiaohua Xu, Zhongyu Huang, Tianfan Fu, Nanqing Dong
- **Comment**: 45 pages, 8 in the main text. Code and data: https://github.com/LiZaiyuan0619/PRUDENT
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Visual token pruning speeds up large vision-language models (VLMs) by keeping only a part of their visual tokens. Most existing methods improve the ranking of the tokens and then answer every question once with the same budget. With strong rankings, pruning hurts only a few questions, and signals computed on the kept tokens see little of what it removed. We find that, on most of these questions, a small VLM given the full image disagrees with the pruned answer, since it still sees the missing evidence. We propose PRUDENT, which first answers with about half the budget and spends the saved tokens on re-answering the disagreeing questions at twice the budget. We show when such re-answering pays and why the small VLM should read the full image. On 18 benchmarks, PRUDENT outperforms eight existing pruning methods at the same average budget of visual tokens. Its lead widens as the budget tightens, and holds across five InternVL sizes. PRUDENT also has the highest average in two more model families. The disagreement of the small VLM flags most pruning failures of all eight methods, and re-answering on it also improves the two strongest with their own rankings. Other checkers give similar gains, and the extra tokens go where the first pass hurts the answer. These results suggest that identifying which pruned answers are trustworthy provides an additional lever for visual token pruning beyond the ranking of tokens. Code and data are available at https://github.com/LiZaiyuan0619/PRUDENT.



### LeViT-UNet++: Towards Balanced Fine-Grained Details and Context for Efficient Medical Image Segmentation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22482v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.MM
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22482v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:39:42+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:39:42+00:00
- **Authors**: Guoping Xu, Jinling Zhu, Liuyue Li
- **Comment**: 24 pages, 5 figure
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Efficient medical image segmentation is essential for clinical applications requiring fast and accurate delineation, such as surgical navigation, radiation therapy, and real-time image-guided intervention. Despite substantial advances in convolutional neural networks and Transformers, attaining high accuracy under limited computational resources remains difficult, because reducing model complexity often sacrifices fine-grained structural details and global context modeling, yielding an unfavorable accuracy-efficiency trade-off. To address this, we propose LeViT-UNet++, an efficient encoder-decoder architecture that better balances local detail preservation and global context modeling. Specifically, we introduce a Haar Wavelet Multi-Scale Downsampling (HWMD) module that decomposes feature representations into low- and high-frequency components to extract multi-scale structural information, thereby mitigating detail loss during downsampling. We further develop a depthwise convolution-based decoder that integrates multi-scale global representations from Transformer blocks with local spatial features, enabling progressive feature refinement while reducing computational overhead. Evaluated on four benchmarks (Synapse, ACDC, LA2018, and MSD Heart) under both 2D and 3D settings, LeViT-UNet++ achieves competitive accuracy with a compact parameter footprint and fast inference. On Synapse, for instance, it attains a DSC of 76.57% with only 8.025M parameters and 241.72 FPS, confirming a favorable accuracy-efficiency balance. These results demonstrate the effectiveness of combining wavelet-based multi-scale feature extraction with lightweight local-global feature fusion for efficient medical segmentation. The source code is available at https://github.com/apple1986/levit_unet_pp.



### Fast and Strong Image Protection against Diffusion Personalization
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22494v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.CR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22494v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:49:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:49:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Jiwon Park, Jihye Kang, Namhyuk Ahn
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Personalized text-to-image diffusion models can reproduce a person from a handful of photographs. Image protection perturbs each image before publication so that fine-tuning on it fails, but it optimizes every perturbation from scratch, so its cost grows with the number of images. We present the protective diffusion model (PDM), which is trained once and samples the perturbation of a new image over many denoising steps. PDM distills an iterative teacher and is then post-trained with the target residual projection (TRP) loss as a reward, a stronger objective than existing ones. PDM outperforms nine baselines on faces and artworks and nearly matches its teacher at a tenth of the cost.



### Motion-Aware Reward Modeling for Instruction-Guided Video Editing
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22495v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22495v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 09:51:25+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 09:51:25+00:00
- **Authors**: Li Li, Xiangbo Gao, Bo Ni, Samyadeep Basu, Viet Dac Lai, Jing Shi, Seunghyun Yoon, Gang Wu, Yu Wang, Thien Huu Nguyen, Tyler Derr, Franck Dernoncourt, Zhengzhong Tu, Yue Zhao
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Reward models for instruction-guided video editing can favor temporal consistency at the expense of motion. We study this failure, termed \emph{motion collapse}, using controlled motion-suppression variants of real edits. On VE-Bench, the evaluated composite reward prefers a motion-suppressed candidate in $97\%$ of cases. Learned video rewards and a VLM judge also struggle with mild suppression, while latent optimization and reward-guided fine-tuning exploit this weakness to increase reward as motion declines. A linear probe of our base reward backbone performs near chance on mild suppression, motivating an explicit inter-frame dynamics input. We propose a motion-aware reward model that combines an RGB frame-difference stream with preference training against motion-suppressed and fabricated-motion negatives. A distillation anchor preserves alignment with human quality judgments. On the VE-Bench motion ladder, our model reduces the rate of selecting motion-suppressed candidates to $0\%$ while achieving a Spearman correlation of $0.57$ with human quality ratings. It also detects unseen suppression variants and preserves motion during reward-guided fine-tuning on two editing pipelines. These results support explicitly modeling motion in rewards for video editing.



### Efficient Self-Supervised Adaptation of 3D Abdominal Vision-Language Model for Institution-Specific HCC Classification via Full Fine-Tuning and PEFT
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22504v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22504v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:00:30+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 10:00:30+00:00
- **Authors**: Febryan Putra Kartika, Cheng-Yu Ma, Ying-Jia Lin, Chi-Tung Cheng, Kuan-Fu Chen
- **Comment**: 25 pages, 12 figures, 7 tables, including appendices. Corrected author revision of the MIDL 2026 paper. Revised preprocessing yields lower HCC classification performance; the main findings on temperature initialization and better VerSe performance retention with PEFT remain supported
- **Journal**: Proceedings of the 9th International Conference on Medical Imaging with Deep Learning, PMLR 315:3245-3269, 2026
- **Summary**: Medical vision-language models (VLMs) capture cross-modal relationships between image and text, yet their adaptation to institution-specific clinical tasks remains underexplored. In this study, we fine-tuned a pretrained 3D medical VLM for hepatocellular carcinoma (HCC) classification using paired abdominal CT scans and radiology reports from a different institution with acquisition characteristics that differ from the model's original pretraining corpus. We compared two adaptation strategies: full fine-tuning and parameter-efficient fine-tuning (PEFT), motivated by the common use of PEFT to reduce computational cost and enable adaptation under limited-data constraints. Both approaches achieve strong downstream HCC classification performance despite the cross-institutional domain shift, with PEFT reaching an AUC of 0.79 and F1 of 0.70, and full fine-tuning achieving an AUC of 0.82 and F1 of 0.71. Although these results remain below the performance reported by supervised HCC classification approaches, our method achieves improvements over the pretrained model through CT-report contrastive learning without requiring lesion-level annotations or HCC classification labels as training targets. Full fine-tuning converges rapidly but overfits within a few epochs, whereas PEFT (ConvLoRA for the image encoder and LoRA for the text encoder) attains comparable performance while updating approximately 1% of the model parameters, although requiring more training steps. We also examine the role of contrastive temperature, observing that temperature initialization significantly affects classification performance. This study demonstrates that a 3D medical VLM can be efficiently adapted to institution-specific HCC classification using self-supervised CT-report contrastive learning, while highlighting the practical trade-offs between full fine-tuning and parameter-efficient fine-tuning.



### EfficientVPR: Toward Efficient Visual Place Recognition via Scene-Aware Prompt Tuning and Adaptive Feature Enhancement
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22506v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22506v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:01:56+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 10:01:56+00:00
- **Authors**: Wenjing Tang, Chuanguang Yang, Zhulin An, Libo Huang, Yongjun Xu
- **Comment**: 18 pages, 11 figures, accepted at CVPR 2026
- **Journal**: Proceedings of the IEEE/CVF Conference on Computer Vision and Pattern Recognition (CVPR), 2026, pp. 33738-33748
- **Summary**: Visual place recognition (VPR) faces critical challenges in handling extreme environmental variations while meeting the computational constraints of practical applications. Current methods predominantly address these challenges by either scaling up model capacity or employing computationally intensive reranking stages, creating a significant efficiency bottleneck. To overcome this limitation, we propose EfficientVPR, a lightweight one-stage framework that achieves speed-accuracy trade-offs through two key innovations: i) a scene-aware visual prompt tuning method which adapts pretrained features with less parameters while dynamically adjusting to sample-specific characteristics, and ii) an instance-dependent key local feature enhancement module that further reinforces discriminative regions. Comprehensive evaluations on Pitts250k, MSLS, Eynsham, AmsterTime and SVOX demonstrate that our method establishes a new SOTA for DINOv2-small models by outperforming all same-scale competitors, and delivers a $73\times$ speedup with 60\% lower-dimensional features while maintaining competitive (within 2.5% average R@1 gap) against the SOTA DINOv2-large-based two-stage method. Our code will be available at https://github.com/WiniTang/EfficientVPR.



### Reliability-Driven Feature Correction for Hyperspectral and LiDAR Classification
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22508v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22508v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:03:36+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 10:03:36+00:00
- **Authors**: Lianming Zhang, Xiaolu Wang, Qiya Song, Jinyang Liu, Renwei Dian, Xudong Kang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Hyperspectral image (HSI) and LiDAR data provide complementary spectral and elevation information for land-cover classification. However, the spatially varying class-discriminative reliability of modality features is rarely modeled explicitly, allowing weak or ambiguous features to adversely affect cross-modal interaction and fusion. To address this issue, we propose ReDF, a reliability-driven framework that quantifies the reliability of each modality's features at each spatial location through the strength of class-related evidence they provide, and uses it to guide feature correction and fusion. Specifically, a Reliability-Guided Feature Correction module introduces a class prototype bank for each modality and converts feature-prototype similarities into class evidence for reliability estimation. The estimated reliabilities are used to construct a reliability-weighted shared representation and to guide bidirectional cross-modal feature correction based on neighborhood agreement, refining modality features with weak class evidence through information from the other modality. Building on these outputs, a Reliability-Guided Feature Reweighting module conditions a low-rank modulation on the estimated reliabilities and the cross-modal disagreement, and applies it to the corrected modality-specific descriptors to adaptively rescale each modality's contribution to the fused representation used for final classification. Experiments on three public HSI-LiDAR datasets show higher mean overall accuracy and Kappa than the compared methods.



### ATS: Anti-Aliased Triangle Splatting with Exact Per-Primitive Integrals
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22512v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.GR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22512v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:06:31+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 10:06:31+00:00
- **Authors**: Brent Zoomers, Joni Vanherck, Leander Winters, Jeroen Put, Lode Jorissen, Nick Michiels
- **Comment**: 17 pages, 7 figures, 9 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Triangle Splatting represents scenes using semi-transparent triangles, but it evaluates each triangle at the pixel center and culls any triangle with an inradius smaller than one pixel. The former causes severe aliasing, while the latter culls most of the scene at lower resolutions. We show that the integral of the Triangle Splatting window over a pixel admits an exact closed-form solution. The angle bisectors split the triangle into three sub-triangles, in each of which the window is a power of the distance to one edge. Each piece therefore has a closed-form antiderivative, and Green's theorem reduces the integral and its gradients to one term per side of the sub-triangle clipped against the pixel. The integral over the whole triangle, which we show has a closed form, then allows conservative culling, removing only triangles that cannot meet the renderer's alpha cutoff at any pixel. This exact formulation anti-aliases existing models without retraining, exhibits less temporal aliasing than 2x2 supersampling, and enables multi-scale training, matching or outperforming supersampled training at reduced resolutions.



### From Perception to Simulation: Unveiling and Empowering Mental Spatial Dynamics in MLLMs
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22541v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.CL
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22541v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:31:56+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 10:31:56+00:00
- **Authors**: Jiachun Li, Zhuoran Jin, Kang Liu, Jun Zhao
- **Comment**: Findings of EMNLP 2026
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Spatial reasoning is a fundamental component of world understanding for Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs). While existing research has advanced the perception of spatial relations in static scenes, the domain of dynamic spatial simulation remains relatively unexplored. To bridge this gap, we introduce MentalGrid, a benchmark focused on spatial reasoning tasks that require models to simulate evolving spatial states without intermediate visual feedback. Extensive evaluation on our benchmark reveals a significant deficiency in the spatial simulation capabilities of existing models. Specifically, we find that performance is constrained by perceptual limitations in simple simulation scenarios and hindered by inadequate multi-step reasoning as complexity increases. To address these challenges, we propose a three-stage curriculum learning framework utilizing 16k specialized spatial reasoning samples. This approach yields SpatialWalker, which achieves a 33.8% average improvement over its base model, surpassing proprietary models like Gemini-3-Flash in spatial simulation. Furthermore, SpatialWalker demonstrates robust generalization, maintaining a 4.9% average gain on out-of-domain benchmarks, demonstrating the pivotal role of spatial simulation as a fundamental building block for broader spatial intelligence.



### JointGV: Structure-Aware Correction of Visual World Models with Parallel Graph Dynamics for Long-Horizon Modeling
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22543v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.AI, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22543v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:33:21+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 10:33:21+00:00
- **Authors**: Tian Lyu, Yichen Jiang, Ziyuan Cheng, Yunda Yan, Xiangyu Pan, Junjie Jiang, Jialin Yu, Boyu Ning, Pietro Liò, Fan Mo
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Visual world models predict future visual states conditioned on actions, but recursive errors in entity poses and spatial relations limit their long-horizon prediction for robot planning and control. We ask whether a separately predicted relational state can correct this drift without restructuring the visual model. This is challenging because graph nodes and visual states share neither elements nor a feature space. We propose Joint Graph--Visual Dynamics Correction (JointGV), a plug-in based on a cross-granularity dynamics hypothesis: despite their different representations, graph-node and visual-state changes describe the same physical transition. JointGV rolls out a task-conditioned scene graph alongside the visual world model, couples their predicted changes, and combines the resulting graph-motion context with graph-predicted node positions to compute a bounded residual. Graph-learning and correction gradients do not propagate into the original visual dynamics. We attach JointGV to DINO-WM (spatial tokens) and DreamerV3 (a global latent state), evaluating both on DMC Cheetah Run and Walker Walk across three seeds and extending the evaluation to contact-rich manipulation with DMC Finger Turn Hard. Across 32-step rollouts, JointGV improves structural and visual-prediction accuracy, reducing joint-position error by 11--28\%. For spatial tokens, learned graph--visual change coupling gives lower error than broadcasting the same mean graph-motion context to every token, particularly when non-adjacent parts appear close together in the image. On real-robot inverted-pendulum sequences, JointGV reduces DINO-WM's 32-step pole-angle error from $12.56^\circ$ to $6.69^\circ$ and improves DreamerV3 and LeWorldModel as well. In simulated inverted-pendulum control, an actor trained with JointGV-corrected rollouts completes 46 of 50 episodes, compared with none using the original rollouts.



### Fast Training-Free Low-Light Video Enhancement with a Frozen Image Diffusion Prior
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22551v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22551v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:42:24+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 10:42:24+00:00
- **Authors**: Hon Ming Yam, Chun Pong Lau
- **Comment**: 15 pages, 12 tables, 4 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Pretrained image diffusion models can enhance low-light video without low-light training data and without retraining for a new camera. ZVRD, the only published training-free method for low-light video, is too slow to use: our implementation needs over a minute per $256 \times 256$ frame. We present a causal, training-free pipeline that adds two temporal components to a frozen MR. Illuminate image prior. Temporal input fusion recursively averages motion-compensated low-light frames before diffusion inversion. Late-start offset propagation runs the full sampling trajectory only on key frames. Each non-key frame inverts the low-noise part of its own trajectory and adds an enhancement offset warped from the latest key frame. At ZVRD's own resolution the pipeline is $261\times$ faster than ZVRD and scores better on all five metrics we report. On 13 held-out SDSD clips at $1280 \times 720$, it improves on framewise eight-step processing by 0.55 dB in PSNR and 0.034 in LPIPS and halves the warping error, in 10% less time. Measurements inside the model show why, and a linear-Gaussian model of inversion explains them. Fusion removes frame-to-frame noise before inversion amplifies it. A flow error perturbs the transported offset ten times less than it perturbs a transported state. The perceptual gain does not transfer to low resolution: at $346 \times 260$, LPIPS is worse by 0.028. At 0.65 frames per second, the pipeline is an offline method.



### Strong Fused Performance Can Mask Branch Collapse in a Dual-View Heart-Sound Classifier: A Three-Part Diagnostic Protocol
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22556v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22556v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:44:04+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 10:44:04+00:00
- **Authors**: Safayet Khan, Hasan Zohirul Islam, Md. Tauhid Bin Iqbal
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Fusion models are usually judged by their fused score, a practice that assumes that every branch has learned something useful. We show that this assumption can fail silently in dual-view heart-sound classification. In AudioFuse, which fuses a spectrogram transformer with a waveform CNN, the spectrogram branch is unused despite strong fused performance when both branches are trained from scratch. We introduce a three-part diagnostic for branch collapse: whether deleting a branch changes the fused score, whether a linear probe on its features beats an untrained copy of the same branch, and whether the branch can learn its view when trained alone. Deleting the branch has negligible effect, and probing its features shows little improvement over an identically initialised, untrained copy. Yet the same architecture trained alone exceeds 0.9 AUC, so its view is learnable, while the jointly trained model is near chance when it must rely on this branch. Update statistics and an initialisation experiment point to an early optimisation imbalance. Moving a strong artificial label cue from one input to the other can switch which branch the model relies on, even though the branch architectures stay the same. We observe branch collapse on PhysioNet, but the full diagnostic is not met on ICBHI or CirCor. Fused performance alone therefore cannot establish that every branch learned a meaningful representation, and we recommend reporting branch deletion and an untrained-control probe alongside it.



### Plausible Videos Can Lead to Poor Decisions: Diagnosing and CURE-ing World Models for Planning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22561v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22561v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:46:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 10:46:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Raphael C. Kim, Michele Santacatterina, Jingsen Zhu, Ramin Zabih
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Action-conditioned world models are increasingly used as world models for planning, yet a world model that makes plausible, realistic-looking videos that are consistent with the training data can produce poor decisions. We address two key questions: (1) when can we trust a world model for planning, and (2) what should we do when we cannot? We introduce CURE (Certify, Upweight, Rollout, Explore), a certificate-guided technique that assesses whether a world model can be trusted for planning and determines what to do next: whether to upweight the training data to debias the model; roll out more samples at test time; or explore by collecting new data where support (how well the training data cover the planner's decisions) is missing. We deploy CURE across multiple domains, spanning simulated physics based on PhyWorld, MuJoCo arm control, and Language-Table pushing in simulation and on real videos. The decisions our certificate trusts have lower regret than those it does not (e.g., 0.06 vs. 0.38 on Language-Table sim and 0.07 vs. 0.38 on MuJoCo contact), whereas video perceptual quality does not identify good decisions. By focusing on decision quality rather than perceptual quality, CURE lowers regret over the best pessimistic decision rule by 10-25% across support levels, and over world-action models, search and policy learning by 41-64%, given the same data.



### CADMAGEDDON: High-Fidelity CAD Reconstruction with Local Inference

- **Summary**: CAD practitioners often work with proprietary parts that they are unwilling to share with external services. For individuals and small companies, bringing this work in-house also requires computational resources they can afford. We introduce CADMAGEDDON, an open-source agent that reconstructs editable CAD from meshes and can run locally on single-GPU systems and current-generation laptops. The method decomposes this complex task into a chain of simple visual decisions about what to construct and what to correct. Geometric tools support precise construction, allowing the agent to focus on visual reasoning. Using the mid-sized Qwen3.8-27B model, CADMAGEDDON outperforms prior approaches, including systems based on frontier models, in reconstruction quality on all the main benchmarks. CADMAGEDDON integrates with FreeCAD, bringing its reconstructions into practitioners' existing CAD workflows. Code: https://github.com/kulibinai/cadmageddon



### Lost in Read-out: Locating the Image Quality Gap in Vision-Language Models with a Closed-Form Edit
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22564v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22564v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:49:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 10:49:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhuangzi Li, Shilv Cai, Jian Jin, Zhiyi Pan, Haihong Xiao, Weisi Lin
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision-language models (VLMs) are widely used as zero-shot image quality assessors, yet their good-vs-poor answers agree only moderately with human opinion. We ask whether these models fail to see quality or fail to say it. Layer-wise probes on five VLMs point to the second: quality is linearly decodable from the vision encoder and still from the last language layer, far better than the answer reveals, and about a hundred labels recover most of it. The loss lies mainly in the final read-out, which partly reads something else: relative to human opinion, it over-rewards colorfulness and under-rewards sharpness on all five models. We test this by intervention: if the read-out is at fault, a closed-form edit of a single last-layer neuron, fitted to many prompts at once, should close the gap and no more. Both hold. Under prompts never used for fitting, the edit recovers 86--100\% of what a ridge head on the last-layer state, fitted under the same prompts, finds beyond the answer on KonIQ. Where that gap is small, it gains little, too little for our transfer criterion on the two largest models; where the head itself is below the answer, it lowers the score; and it works only when written from the middle of the language model on. Unlike unconstrained fine-tuning, the edit leaves perception question answering intact. It does not carry over to yes-or-no or rating questions, and it reverses the good--poor contrast after sentiment-laden sentences. Code will be made available.



### MillBill: A Machining Agent with Geometric Feedback
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22565v2
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22565v2)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 10:52:45+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 18:33:02+00:00
- **Authors**: Gennadii Savrasov, Artem Slavin, Soslan Kabisov, Andrey Kuznetsov, Anton Konushin, Dmitrii Zhemchuzhnikov
- **Comment**: 18 pages, 8 figures. Code: https://github.com/kulibinai/millbill
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Turning a CAD model into a machining program requires both process knowledge and geometric precision. Language models can generate such workflows by writing geometry and toolpath code for each part, but this makes numerical execution part of the model's task. We introduce MillBill, an agent that separates machining decisions from their geometric execution and verification. Given a boundary-representation part, stock, and material, a locally hosted, mid-sized open model proposes operations and cutting parameters for indexed three-axis machining. A geometric harness generates toolpaths, simulates material removal, and returns feedback for plan refinement. Setup planning accounts for what the available cutters can physically reach, rather than visibility alone. We evaluate the resulting programs by the fraction of the target surface area within the specified tolerance after simulated machining. With Qwen3.8-27B, MillBill outperforms frontier-model approaches that construct their own geometry and toolpath workflows on the evaluated machining tasks. Code: https://github.com/kulibinai/millbill



### Semantic-Guided Geometric Decoding for Token-Efficient Visual Grounding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22577v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22577v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 11:02:17+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 11:02:17+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhaojie Chen, Junyan Yang, Le Yang, Qi Dang, Yucheng Huang, Qianyi Che, Zhaoqiang Chen, Xinqi Zhu, Qiang Zhao
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 4 figures, 5 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal large language models (MLLMs) provide a unified interface for language-conditioned visual perception. In pixel-to-sequence grounding, autoregressive coordinate generation requires multiple sequential decoding steps per box, increasing inference cost with the number of instances. Pixel-to-embedding methods reduce this overhead but lack explicit spatial encoding when using a shared special token. We introduce SemGeo, a token-efficient visual grounding framework that represents each instance with a single grid token for coarse localization. A geometric decoder reuses visual features and hidden states to recover precise discrete box coordinates in parallel. To train proposal generation and box localization together, we introduce Gaussian soft labels that encode coordinate distances during supervised fine-tuning (SFT). Reinforcement post-training further combines box-set rewards with coordinate supervision on model-generated proposals. This stage trains the language model and geometric decoder on their own predictions to mitigate the mismatch between teacher-forced training and inference. Across eight benchmarks spanning object detection, referring object detection, and document layout grounding, SemGeo achieves the highest F1@mIou among compared MLLMs on six benchmarks. It also delivers $2.07\times$ and $1.25\times$ the mean box-generation throughput of representative grounding baselines under vLLM and Transformers, respectively, while achieving higher accuracy in both comparisons.



### PACT: Plan-Action Consistency Training by Preserving Correct Plans and Repairing Actions in GUI Agents
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22591v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22591v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 11:14:59+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 11:14:59+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhuohan Cai, Zhangxuan Gu, Runze Li, Beitong Zhou, Ming Wen, Hongbo Yu, Shuheng Shen, Changhua Meng
- **Comment**: 21 pages, 11 figures, including supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: GUI agents can formulate a correct immediate plan in their reasoning yet emit an action that fails to execute it, creating an execution gap. This recurring mismatch undermines reliable task completion, as a single execution error can derail a multi-step workflow. In outcome-supervised reinforcement learning, broadcasting a negative response-level advantage across all generated tokens risks penalizing valid planning together with erroneous execution. To address this credit-assignment conflict, we introduce PACT (Plan-Action Consistency Training), a training framework that combines reference-aligned plan protection and plan-aligned action repair without altering the original scalar action reward. Using annotated reference actions to identify execution gaps, PACT protects the verified plan by masking negative policy-gradient contributions across its token span. Keeping the generated reasoning unchanged as context, it then favors the reference action over the emitted erroneous action through a preference surrogate with stop-gradient pair weights. Compared with the outcome-only baseline, PACT raises AndroidWorld task success from 60.34% to 68.97% (+8.63 pp), while improving rule-based plan-action consistency on AndroidControl High from 83.71% to 89.74% (+6.03 pp). Adapting PACT to desktop agents also improves task success from 40.92% to 44.17% (+3.25 pp) on the full 369-task OSWorld benchmark. Together, these results establish PACT as an effective credit-assignment framework for bridging execution gaps and improving task completion across mobile and desktop GUI environments.



### Distilling World Action Models for Efficient Bimanual Manipulation via Anchored Path Matching
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22592v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22592v1)

- **Authors**: Jialong Liu, Junjie He, Yandu Chen, Bin Zhou, Heng Su, Peijun Tang, Jianan Wang, Changhao Chen
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: World Action Models (WAMs) jointly generate future video and robot actions through iterative denoising. Few-step distillation reduces the number of model evaluations, but its coarse updates can move the student away from the teacher's trajectory. We propose Anchored Path Matching (APM), which supervises endpoint and short-transition predictions from states reached by the student. From each state, we continue the teacher to zero noise and use its endpoint and first intermediate state as targets. Modality-routed adapters learn to match both targets from the same starting state. When the two gradients conflict, anchor-priority updates preserve the endpoint gradient and remove the opposing component of the local gradient within each modality. One checkpoint supports 1-, 2-, and 4-step joint generation. The sampling budget can be chosen at deployment without retraining or teacher queries. On RoboTwin 2.0, we train only on Clean demonstrations and evaluate directly on the domain-randomized Random setting. At one step, APM improves closed-loop success over continued base distillation at matched inference budgets by 2.0 percentage points on Clean and 1.4 on Random. Video peak signal-to-noise ratio (PSNR) rises from 30.169 to 32.317 dB, with gains in structural similarity (SSIM) and learned perceptual image patch similarity (LPIPS). On two real-robot tasks, APM improves success over the one-step teacher and reduces control-cycle latency by $21.3\times$ relative to the eight-step teacher.Project page: https://jialonglong.github.io/Anchored-Path-Matching/.



### Propose with the Gradient, Accept with the Loss: One Hedged Attack for Pixel-Sparse Robustness
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22596v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.CR, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22596v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 11:16:57+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 11:16:57+00:00
- **Authors**: Hon Ming Yan, Chun Pong Lau
- **Comment**: 22 pages, 13 tables, 4 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: A pixel-sparse adversary may change a few pixels of an image to any value. Robustness to such attacks is currently measured with an ensemble, sparse-AutoAttack (sAA), because its members fail in different places: sparse projected gradient descent (sPGD) trusts a first-order model that a full pixel change breaks, and sparse random search (Sparse-RS) ignores the gradient even where it is informative. We show that both attacks are local searches, each a proposal distribution plus an acceptance rule, and ask what such a search can learn from the gradient. A Taylor bound says what to measure, and measurements at the states the search visits give five rules. The sign of the gradient is its most reliable use at a single pixel and sets a new pixel's value. Its ranking of candidate pixels should be followed loosely, and its ranking of the pixels to remove, which is often more reliable, can be followed more closely. A pixel should not be proposed twice before a move is accepted, and acceptance should be decided by the loss alone. These rules define Gradient-guided Random Search (GRS), which runs once and costs one forward and one backward pass per step. On 13 CIFAR-10 configurations and 5 ImageNet models, GRS matches or beats the best single member of sAA and is within one point of sAA itself or stronger on 17 of 18 settings, and it is significantly stronger than sAA on 8. It uses 3 instead of up to 7 forward-pass units, and on CIFAR-10 it runs 3.7 to 5.0 times faster than the members of sAA run in full.



### PlaceTally: A Test-Time Memory Model for Constructing Place Graphs
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22608v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22608v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 11:23:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 11:23:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Alibay Osmanli, Shaogang Gong
- **Comment**: 12 pages, 3 figures, 5 tables. Code, data and supplementary material: https://alibayosmanli.github.io/placetally
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Counting distinct objects in a video is hard when the camera returns to places it has seen. A vision-language model that reads the whole video answers with a few small numbers, and one that reads it in parts counts an object again in every part that shows it. Object trackers need the category before the video is read, or a fixed vocabulary. We observe that a memory for counting does not need to know what it is looking for, only where it has been: objects rarely leave a room between visits, so parts of a video grouped by place share almost no objects, and a place can be recognised before any question is asked. Building on this, we introduce PlaceTally, a question-blind test-time memory model that constructs a place graph for each video. Each video fragment joins the place of its best earlier match or opens a new place, because a strong match is rarely wrong while a true return often matches weakly. A frozen vision-language model counts each place once from at most 128 of its frames, so every read has a bounded input and the memory grows with the number of places; the graph is built 15 times faster than real time. On RevisitCount, our benchmark of real room re-recordings, it scores 62.5% mean relative accuracy on test rooms and 61.2% on held-out rooms, against 51.2% and 44.3% for the same model reading the whole video. It stays level as rooms return more or less often, while a fixed correction of summed visits moves from 38.5% to 61.4%.



### Sub-Meter Canopy Height Synthesis from Sentinel-2 via Anchored Residual Flow
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22636v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22636v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 11:52:54+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 11:52:54+00:00
- **Authors**: Kiarie Ndegwa, Tony Chang, Andreas Gros, Vincent A. Landau, David D. Diaz, Luke Zachmann, Nathan E. Rutenbeck, Guy C Bayes
- **Comment**: 30 pages, including appendices
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Canopy height models (CHMs) - per-pixel maps of vegetation top height - are essential for forest carbon accounting, wildfire fuel mapping, and tree-level ecology, yet sub-meter CHMs require airborne LiDAR refreshed on multi-year national cycles. We produce 0.5 m CHMs from free 10 m Sentinel-2 imagery, a 20$\times$ cross-modal super-resolution task where optical reflectance must predict geometric height it does not directly observe. Motivated by the perception-distortion trade-off, we derive two operating points with shared conditioning. A deterministic regressor (Anchor) targets low distortion; a conditional flow matching model, trained on the Anchor's residual in a learned CHM latent space (AR-flow), targets perceptual fidelity. Trained on 197k LiDAR-paired tiles spanning 69 ecoregions across the contiguous United States and evaluated on 2,530 held-out tiles against eight baselines, the Anchor achieves 2.83 m MAE using only free satellite input. The AR-flow reduces sliced Wasserstein distance from the Anchor's 1.13 to 0.208, at a higher MAE of 3.68 m. Crown-scale statistics improve, but strict individual-tree detection remains weak: generated sub-pixel detail represents plausible structure rather than measured individual trees. Quarterly continental refresh becomes tractable, letting satellite monitoring inform timing and extent for more expensive data acquisitions.



### OVET-Consensus: Rank-Preserving Counterfactual Event Verification for Video Moment Grounding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22637v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22637v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 11:52:55+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 11:52:55+00:00
- **Authors**: Alibay Osmanli, Shaogang Gong
- **Comment**: 22 pages, 2 figures, 17 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Modern video moment retrieval systems often propose windows that overlap the target event, yet still select the wrong one. We study this scorer bottleneck on QVHighlights with a fixed QD-DETR-derived temporal expert: the parent-aware temporal expert reaches 64.71 R1@0.5, while diagnostic oracles over the same candidate family reach 75.68 R1@0.5 within the parent-reranked pool and 94.97 R1@0.5 over the full 100-candidate evidence pool. We introduce OVET-Consensus, a rank-preserving counterfactual event verification layer that keeps the temporal expert's ranked list as the default and permits only high-confidence rank-1 overrides from a policy calibrated on held-out training videos. The verifier uses parent-aware interval evidence, structured Qwen3-VL event judgments, VideoLLaMA3 pairwise temporal signals, and dense temporal evidence, but never lets those signals freely reorder the top-K list. On QVHighlights validation, OVET-Consensus improves the parent-aware temporal expert from 64.71 to 65.35 R1@0.5 with a positive paired-bootstrap interval, while mAP is largely unchanged, from 42.67 to 42.74; it makes 20 overrides, with 15 helpful corrections and 3 harmful corrections. The results point to a practical constraint on verifier design: semantic compatibility is useful evidence, but temporal grounding still requires deciding where the event starts, changes, and ends inside a visually compatible scene.



### REVEAL: Reconstructing Video Amodal Content via Language and Flow Guidance
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22645v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22645v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:00:15+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:00:15+00:00
- **Authors**: Minh Tran, Winston Bounsavy, Ngan Le
- **Comment**: Accepted to Transactions on Machine Learning Research (TMLR)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Amodal perception enables humans to perceive entire objects even when parts are occluded, a remarkable cognitive skill that artificial intelligence struggles to replicate. Recent diffusion-based methods have extended amodal completion from images to videos, yet they lack auxiliary priors to guide the reconstruction of heavily occluded content and to maintain temporal consistency over long sequences. We present REVEAL (REconstructing VidEo Amodal content via Language and flow guidance), a unified diffusion-based framework that integrates complementary motion and semantic priors for video amodal completion. A user-provided text query serves a dual role: it drives open-vocabulary video segmentation to obtain visible masks, and it provides semantic guidance for texture reconstruction. For amodal segmentation, we introduce optical flow guidance: by warping visible masks from previous frames to the current frame, the flow-warped mask propagates visible content into occluded regions and approximates the object's current shape, providing a strong shape prior even under simultaneous deformation and occlusion. For texture completion, text guidance constrains the reconstruction of occluded appearance while providing a stable semantic reference that helps maintain visual coherence throughout the reconstruction. We also contribute LAVAT, a new benchmark featuring long video sequences paired with text descriptions, enabling evaluation of text-guided video amodal completion under heavy occlusion. Extensive experiments demonstrate that REVEAL achieves state-of-the-art performance on existing benchmarks while maintaining high-quality temporal consistency over extended sequences. The project page is available at: https://uark-aicv.github.io/REVEAL/.



### Asymmetric Optical Image Watermarking
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23350v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CR**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23350v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:04:14+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:04:14+00:00
- **Authors**: Guang Yang
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 4 figures, 5 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Watermark detectors held by a provider cannot be checked by third parties, and a party that can detect a mark can usually also forge it. We study a two-layer image watermark in which the Fourier transform at the core of the pipeline is performed by a coherent optical 4F unit. Layer 1 carries an Ed25519 signature bound to the image content in Fourier magnitudes, so anyone with the public key verifies offline. Layer 2 is a key-defined carrier written by a secret phase hologram and read by a matched-filter correlator. The optics is a scalar-diffraction simulation with finite modulator bits, pixel aperture, pupil, defocus, misalignment, and sensor noise. No hardware is built. All criteria were fixed in a hashed specification before any optical result. The simulated optical signer reproduces the digital pipeline (field error 1.7e-15, bit agreement 99.997%), verifies at least 99.5% of 877 images under each of 8 post-sensor settings, one unattacked, and accepts none of 40,670 unmarked images. The optical carrier is key-selective: the matched key detects it on 99.8% of the unattacked images, and detection falls to 0% under a scale error of 1% and to 0% under a mask shift of 1 bin. Security rests on the signature and the content binding, for which we separate what is provable from what is measured.



### Must You See Fakes to Catch Fakes? Language Says What to Look For, Real Images What to Ignore
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22650v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22650v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:07:26+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:07:26+00:00
- **Authors**: Tianchi Xu, Yuxuan Zhou, Yuheng Zhang, Yanan Guan, Tao Dai, Shu-Tao Xia
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Must a detector see generated images in order to catch them? Detection of AI-generated images has long faced two problems. The cues a detector learns from the few generators in its training set carry traces specific to those generators and transfer poorly to new ones, which limits generalization. The real and generated training images also differ in format and content, so the detector takes these differences as evidence of authenticity, and its decisions change with factors that have nothing to do with authenticity, which harms robustness as well as generalization. Both problems stem from training on generated images. A detector never trained on them can largely avoid both at the source. Vision foundation models have already learned the concept of "AI-generated" during pretraining, and text can point to it directly. Building on this, we propose SaySee, a training-free detector: Say builds a generation direction from sets of paired descriptions that differ only in authenticity and scores an image by the projection of its embedding onto it, and See uses real images to correct the score offsets caused by format, image type and content. On 24 evaluation sets that span standard benchmarks, in-the-wild data and the latest generators, SaySee reaches a mean AUC of 98.2% with MoE-ViE, 4.2 points above the best existing detector. When format is decoupled from authenticity, it remains the best of all methods. Code will be released.



### OmniTongue: A Comprehensive MLLM Benchmark for Automated Tongue Diagnosis in Traditional Chinese Medicine
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22652v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22652v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:10:17+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:10:17+00:00
- **Authors**: Liuyi Xu, Yun Guo, Shusheng Li, Xuewen Zhang, Yining Qian, An-Yang Lu
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 2 figures, 3 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have excelled in Western medical imaging, yet their potential in Traditional Chinese Medicine (TCM) tongue diagnosis remains systematically unevaluated. Tongue diagnosis requires sophisticated global and local perception, serving as a rigorous touchstone for assessing the visual understanding depth of MLLMs. However, existing datasets are limited by single dimensions, coarse granularity, and ambiguous labels, hindering the evaluation of authentic clinical capabilities. To bridge this gap, we introduce OmniTongue, the first comprehensive, fine-grained Visual Question Answering benchmark for TCM tongue diagnosis. This benchmark integrates 1,351 expert-validated images and 21,616 instruction pairs. Its core design features 16 clinical dimensions for multidimensional evaluation, region-specific annotations for pathological localization, and 66 fine-grained labels for precise diagnostic representation. Extensive evaluations of existing MLLMs reveal significant performance gaps, demonstrating that medical-specific models lack domain advantages, while universally poor Macro-F1 scores expose a reliance on statistical shortcuts rather than genuine understanding of tongue pathology. These findings not only highlight the current limitations of MLLMs in interpreting regional medical features but also underscore OmniTongue's significance for developing robust automated tongue diagnosis models. The code and dataset will be made publicly available in a future release.



### Unified Residual Translation for Paired Image Restoration
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22655v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22655v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:12:52+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:12:52+00:00
- **Authors**: Joyanta Jyoti Mondal, Mridul Banik, Ibne Farabi Shihab, Md. Shifatul Ahsan Apurba
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 2 figures
