- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Paired image restoration spans changes in imaging contrast, sensor modality, and spatial resolution. These settings differ in their input channels and target grids, raising the question of how much of the prediction pipeline must be specialized to each task. We present the Unified Residual Translator (URT), which separates fixed channel and scale mapping from learned residual prediction. A deterministic reference uses channel averaging, replication, or bicubic interpolation to match the target dimensions, while a NAFNet encoder-decoder predicts an additive correction from the complete input. Separately trained instances share the prediction rule, $\ell_1$ objective, and optimizer across MRI contrast synthesis, visible-to-infrared translation, SAR-to-optical translation, and real-camera super-resolution. Across six task and scale settings, URT achieves the highest PSNR and SSIM among the evaluated baselines on IXI, SEN1-2, and RealSR $\times3$ and $\times4$. It reaches 30.774dB on IXI and 13.478dB on SEN1-2, with luminance PSNR of 33.596, 28.742, and 27.342dB at the three RealSR scales. On LLVIP, it ranks second in PSNR and SSIM. A study of four spectral alternatives identifies complementary benefits of fitted references and bandwise residual gains. Together, these results support explicit dimensional alignment and learned residual correction as a common formulation for heterogeneous paired imaging tasks.



### SpatialCast: Reading the 3D World via Cast Shadows
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22657v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22657v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:15:08+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:15:08+00:00
- **Authors**: Enhan Li, Jinghao Zhou, Jiaze Zhang, Hongyang Du
- **Comment**: 10 pages (main paper), 7 pages (supplementary material), 5 figures and 6 tables in the main paper
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: As multimodal large language models (MLLMs) advance toward understanding and interacting with the physical world, spatial reasoning becomes essential for understanding scene structure and anticipating the effects of physical changes. Existing spatial benchmarks have made substantial progress in evaluating spatial relations, memory, and viewpoint reasoning, but less systematically assess how models connect physical scene conditions with visual observations. We introduce SpatialCast, a benchmark that uses cast shadows to evaluate this ability. Cast shadows provide observable evidence of a 3D scene. Their image appearance depends on object geometry and pose, illumination, and viewpoint. We examine this relationship through three complementary tasks that assess scene-projection consistency, predict intervention-induced changes, and infer the physical changes behind visual transitions. The benchmark contains 21,000 synthetic questions with separate scene and geometry test partitions, complemented by 200 SpatialCast-Real questions from 20 photographed scenes. Evaluations show that closed-source MLLMs outperform general-purpose open-weight MLLMs and MLLMs designed for spatial reasoning, which remain near chance on the synthetic benchmark, while even strong closed-source models struggle with shadow-length and light-height judgments. We also use SpatialCast training data for GRPO post-training, improving open-weight MLLM performance on the benchmark. Further evaluations show improvements on several external spatial benchmarks, suggesting transfer of the learned capabilities. These experiments support SpatialCast as a controlled resource for diagnosing spatial reasoning capabilities and studying their learning and generalization.



### VSpark: Speculative Decoding for Autoregressive Vision-Language-Action Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22666v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22666v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:22:38+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:22:38+00:00
- **Authors**: Suho Yoo, Youngjoon Jang, Hyebin Cho, Taeyeop Lee, Jaewook Jeong, Joon Son Chung
- **Comment**: Preprint
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Our aim is to accelerate autoregressive vision-language-action models (VLAs), which generate robot actions one token at a time. Speculative decoding makes this possible: a lightweight draft model proposes several tokens at once, and the VLA verifies them in a single pass, accepting only those that match its own predictions, so decoding remains lossless. The speedup, however, is limited in robot control, where many different tokens encode nearly the same motion and a draft close to the correct action is still rejected. To address this, our approach, VSpark, is built upon three design choices: (i) rollouts collected with calibrated action noise, which expose the draft to a wider range of robot states; (ii) feature alignment, which trains the draft to match the VLA's hidden features rather than its output tokens alone; and (iii) continuation reuse, which keeps the predictions the VLA has already computed along rejected drafts as candidates for the next verification step, instead of discarding them. On four VLAs with different action tokenizers and four manipulation benchmarks, VSpark achieves 1.28x to 2.10x speedup while keeping the VLA's decoding lossless.



### Let Samples Sing in Chorus: Mitigating Spurious Correlations via Cross-view Consensus
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22668v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22668v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:23:15+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:23:15+00:00
- **Authors**: Weiwei Li, Junzhuo Liu, Tong Chu, Hengfu Yu, Yinjie Lei, Wen Li
- **Comment**: Under Review
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Deep models often rely on spurious correlations that fail on underrepresented groups. Existing methods either correct human-specified bias attributes or infer unknown biases from the learned representation space. However, annotated attributes may differ from the shortcuts actually used by a model, while a single representation provides incomplete and potentially noisy evidence. Our key insight is that a bias obscured in one feature space may be clearer in another. In this work, we propose CHORD (Cross-view Harmonic Outlier Reversal for Debiasing), a multi-view framework that allows samples to "sing in chorus" across diverse representation spaces to achieve robust cross-view consensus. CHORD aggregates aggregates within-class partitions in each view, which is a proxy for specifying candidate biases, to debias the target representation and classifier without bias supervision. Experiments on seven benchmarks across vision, language, speech, and medical imaging show consistent improvements in worst-group accuracy. Code and checkpoints are available at https://github.com/davelee-uestc/chord_debiasing_public .



### Beyond the Camera: A Tri-Modal Driving World Model for Future RGB, Thermal, and LiDAR Forecasting in Adverse Weather
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22669v2
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22669v2)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:24:09+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 11:00:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Faysal Mehrab Chowdhury, Md. Mahfuzur Rahman, Ahmed Rafi Hasan, Kishor Datta Gupta, Mohd Ariful Haque, Savannah Shannon
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Driving world models forecast what a vehicle's sensors will record next. Most forecast camera video and some add LiDAR, but thermal cameras, which still see in darkness and some adverse weather, are rarely a forecast target, and to our knowledge no world model forecasts all three sensors. We study joint forecasting of RGB, thermal, and LiDAR on real adverse-weather sequences of the DSERT-RoLL dataset. We post-train a pretrained video world model for each sensor and represent LiDAR as a depth video aligned with the RGB camera, with densification and encoding chosen by what the model's video tokenizer can reconstruct. A tri-sensor model sees all three sensors as one video. We score LiDAR in meters against measured returns and compare against persistence baselines passed through the same tokenizer as the model. With this matching, an apparent loss of the thermal model to persistence in fog disappears. On all 58 validation sequences in six weather conditions, post-trained RGB and thermal forecasts beat compression-matched persistence. Joint training lowers the LiDAR error at 2s by 2.1m at a small cost in camera fidelity, and only the tri-sensor model's LiDAR forecasts beat persistence in mean error. Blanking one sensor degrades the forecasts of the others more in snow than in fog.



### FVScore: Specification-Driven Neuro-Symbolic Video Evaluation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22673v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22673v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:25:33+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:25:33+00:00
- **Authors**: Do Xuan Long, Long T. Le, Yiwen Song, Min-Yen Kan, Tomas Pfister, Yale Song
- **Comment**: Project page: https://dxlong2000.github.io/FVSCORE/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We introduce Faithfulness Video Score (FVSCORE), a neuro-symbolic framework for specification-driven video generation evaluation. Existing evaluators rely on holistic MLLM judgments or vision-language metrics, yet aligning their scores with human judgments and tracing them to specific temporal violations remain open challenges. FVSCORE addresses these limitations by compiling each video into explicit propositions capturing prompt requirements and implicit propositions capturing unstated physical, temporal, and cross-modal constraints. It grounds each proposition using specialized perceptual tools and evaluates the resulting confidence traces with soft temporal operators inspired by Linear Temporal Logic. A Significance-Aware Calibration step then aligns verification with human perception by retaining only perceptually significant violations before producing a timestamp- and evidence-grounded report. We conduct extensive experiments across public and complex multi-event benchmarks with the strongest video models to date, demonstrating that FVSCORE outperforms holistic and agent-based evaluators in human correlation while providing highly accurate and interpretable feedback.



### Classification of Tissues on MALDI-MSI Image Modality for Pancreatic Cancer Studies
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22680v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22680v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:32:43+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:32:43+00:00
- **Authors**: Abubakar Qahir, Jenny Benois-Pineau, Victoria Bourgeais, Fabien Baldacci, Sandrine Dabernat, Nicolas Desbenoit, Mélanie Moreau, Mehdi Boubaddi, Marion Marty
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Mass Spectrometry Imaging (MSI) spatially maps molecular distributions in tissue sections. Despite the availability of preprocessing tools for MSI data, building end-to-end workflows suitable for deep learning remains challenging. We have developed a fully automated pipeline for transformation of raw Matrix-Assisted Laser Desorption/Ionization Mass Spectrometry imaging (MALDI-MSI) data into deep-learning-ready image patches for binary pancreatic tissue classification (tumoral vs non-tumoral) and built individual models for recognition of tumoral regions in spectral images. Images resulting from the proposed pipeline and from reference interactive workflow are very close in terms of image quality metric mean squared error (MSE). The proposed pipeline computational time is of 20 min in average compared to a full working day of the reference interactive workflow. The models achieved 0.94 accuracy on high-resolution spectra and a mean accuracy of 0.59 on lower-resolution spectra.



### CascadeJEPA: Enhancing 3D Geometric Representations via Spatially Coupled Cascaded Prediction
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22686v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22686v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:36:05+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:36:05+00:00
- **Authors**: Kailai Zhou, Ziqi Chen, Xiaoning Xu, Yuefeng Wu, Zixuan Fu, Zijun Lin, Zhihao Li, Youwei Pang, Xiaoqi Zhao, Xun Cao, Bihan Wen
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Joint-Embedding Predictive Architectures (JEPAs) have emerged as a promising paradigm for predictive visual representation learning. However, our analysis of I-JEPA reveals a growing discrepancy between semantic and geometric learning: improved semantic recognition during pretraining is accompanied by degraded 3D geometric representations. We hypothesize that this partly stems from predicting highly abstract latent representations with incomplete spatial context, favoring semantic regularities over fine-grained geometric information. To address this issue, we introduce CascadeJEPA, a spatially coupled cascaded prediction framework that promotes richer patch-level interactions through full-grid context encoding and cross-attention between prediction stages, while largely preserving the original I-JEPA architecture. Extensive experiments demonstrate substantial improvements over I-JEPA across multiple 3D geometric tasks while maintaining strong semantic recognition performance. Furthermore, its advantages extend to future feature forecasting and world model planning, highlighting the potential of enhanced geometric representations to support predictive world modeling.



### TANGO: Real-World Unregistered RGB-Guided Hyperspectral Super-Resolution with Two-Camera Acquisitions
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22699v2
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22699v2)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:46:01+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 10:02:27+00:00
- **Authors**: Matteo Kolyszko, Marco Buzzelli, Simone Bianco, Raimondo Schettini
- **Comment**: 18 pages, 11 Figures, 10 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Unregistered hyperspectral image super-resolution aims to recover a high-resolution HSI from a low-resolution hyperspectral observation and an unregistered high-resolution RGB reference. Although recent methods have begun to address the geometric misalignment between the two modalities, their evaluation is still constrained by the lack of a large-scale public benchmark based on real two-camera RGB-HSI acquisitions with a high-resolution HSI reference. To address this gap, we introduce DUET, the first large-scale public dataset for unregistered RGB-guided hyperspectral super-resolution, comprising 403 indoor and outdoor scenes captured with two separate RGB and hyperspectral cameras. We further propose TANGO (Two-camera AligNment with Gated Occlusions), which first compensates for the dominant cross-camera displacement through end-to-end global alignment and then refines the remaining inconsistencies with a coarse-to-fine local feature alignment. A learned gating mechanism further attenuates the RGB guidance where the two views cannot be consistently matched. Experimental results show that TANGO achieves state-of-the-art performance on both DUET and REAL across all evaluated scale factors, outperforming the strongest competitor on DUET by up to 2.29 dB in PSNR while requiring 2.5$\times$ fewer FLOPs. The code and dataset will be available at https://github.com/FagiolottoConSombrero/TANGO-DUET



### Social Signal Bench: Evidence-Weighted Evaluation of Co-occurring Social Signals in Video and Audio
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22704v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22704v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:49:04+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:49:04+00:00
- **Authors**: Siddharth Ravi, Anders R. Bargum, Hugo Anselme, Patrizio Cugia di Sant'Orsola, Alberto Cardaci, Flora Kincso Parragi, Vincent Borowiec, Line H. Clemmensen
- **Comment**: 25 pages, including appendix
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recognizing social signals such as interest and hesitation requires interpreting context-dependent behaviors that may co-occur in conversation. Existing affective computing benchmarks rarely evaluate such co-occurring signals, and conventional multi-label F1 overlooks semantic relationships and annotator disagreement. We introduce Social Signal Bench, a 47-hour densely annotated benchmark for the evaluation of social signal recognition and engagement, comprising two datasets: Social Signal Bench Video (25 hours), covering ten social signals in audiovisual recordings, and Social Signal Bench Audio (22 hours), covering six signals in audio-only recordings. Each dataset pairs a public training set of online conversational video, of which 10 hours are expert-annotated and the remainder pseudo-labeled, with a private expert-labeled test set spanning four conversational domains. We also propose Evidence-Weighted F1 (EW-F1), which weights reference labels by annotator evidence and assigns graded credit to semantically related predictions through optimal per-clip matching. Controlled perturbations demonstrate that EW-F1 distinguishes semantically related errors from unrelated ones, unlike conventional F1. Under a shared evaluation protocol, 7B open models post-trained on Social Signal Bench outperform general-purpose frontier models on video and match them on audio.



### Know When to Zoom: How Small Is Too Small for Vision-Language Models?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22709v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22709v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:50:14+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:50:14+00:00
- **Authors**: Siddharth Vohra, Min Xu
- **Comment**: Accepted to the SENSE workshop at WACV 2027. 10 pages, 5 figures, 3 tables. Code, data and a plain-language walkthrough: https://siddvoh.com/know-when-to-zoom/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Online vision-language models often miss small details in large images, because the service shrinks the image, or rejects it, before the model ever sees it. A car 20 pixels wide in an 8000 by 6000 drone photo reaches Claude about 6 pixels wide, and OpenAI's default rejects the photo outright. We measure how much detail survives for seven models from four vendors, using an eye exam of printed codes and 601 questions about small objects in real photos. The smallest text a model reads half the time grows from 6 pixels or less on a small image to a predicted 10 to 13 pixels on a 7680 by 4320 photo at default settings. Zooming wins much of the detail back. Splitting each photo into up to nine tiles raises accuracy by 11.1 to 14.5 percentage points for Claude and Gemini, and by avoiding rejections it lifts GPT-6.1 Sol from 50.7% to 83.7%. A two-step zoom, in which the model first names the region it needs, matches or beats tiles on Gemini and comes within 2.5 points of them on Claude, with about 40% of their image tokens. When a detail is lost, Claude and Gemini both say they cannot tell about half the time, but they fail differently in the rest: Gemini gives a confident wrong answer in 43% to 48% of these cases, Claude in only 5% to 9%. A check based on detail size, run before the call, misses most of the gain from tiles on Claude and Gemini, but Claude's own reply is a reliable trigger: tiling only after an unsure reply comes within 1.4 points of always tiling with 62% to 70% of the image tokens. We turn these findings into five rules for when an agent should zoom, and release the eye exam, every model answer and code at https://siddvoh.com/know-when-to-zoom.



### PCRG: Training-Free Pruning and Caching of Reference Tokens for Video Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22717v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22717v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:56:13+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:56:13+00:00
- **Authors**: Siyuan Wang, Haoran Li, Yi Yue, Tak L. Fung, Quan Chen
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 5 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Reference-to-video diffusion transformers pack the variational autoencoder (VAE) latents of all references and the generated tokens into one full-attention sequence, in which reference tokens make up about half. Because attention cost grows quadratically with sequence length, these tokens take up an even larger share of the attention computation, yet most of them are static, smooth or irrelevant to the output. To accelerate reference-conditioned generation, we propose pruning and caching of reference tokens for video generation (PCRG), a training-free, plug-and-play method that uses two signals the model already computes to assign each reference token to one of three tiers, namely pruned, cached or kept. The first is a low-level redundancy r from VAE latents, since a token whose latent barely changes over time or across its neighbors adds little new content. The second is a high-level importance g from the vision-language-model (VLM) conditioner, where the attention that the prompt and any first or last frames pay to a token tells whether the output needs to read it. Tokens that are not redundant are kept, and redundant tokens are cached if important and pruned otherwise. Pruned tokens leave the sequence before the first step with all rotary position embedding positions unchanged. Cached tokens have their keys and values computed once and reused, and kept tokens are processed as usual. On MiniMax-H3, already distilled to 8 timesteps and quantized to int8, PCRG is 1.57x faster end to end on our V2V-Bench and 1.49x faster on the public VACE-Benchmark, while the quality of VBench drops by only 0.24% and 0.17%, respectively. PCRG shortens the sequence rather than changing how attention is computed or which steps are run, so it also stacks on step caching and sparse attention. On V2V-Bench it reaches 1.86x with TeaCache, 1.82x with Spark at nearly unchanged fidelity and 2.15x with Veda.



### Practice in Text, Act in Pixels: Correcting Where Perception Fails
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22722v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22722v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:58:00+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:58:00+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhongling Xu, Hairong Wang
- **Comment**: 25 pages, 8 figures, 19 tables. Code: https://github.com/Carlxuzhl/practice-in-text ; aligned reader: https://huggingface.co/xuzhl/practice-in-text-aligned-reader
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: GUI agents can learn cheaply from a text view of an interface and act on screenshots through a vision-language screen reader. Yet on a simulated mobile platform with a low-cost reader, policies that complete most tasks in text fail on many of the same tasks on screenshots. We ask where a small budget of real screenshots is best spent to close this gap. We find that the reader's misreads are systematic and recur on the same interface elements. Noise matched to the reader's error rates, a natural sim-to-real remedy, misses this structure, and in a pre-specified comparison it did not beat training the policy directly on screenshots. We therefore correct the misreads at their source with reader alignment, which fine-tunes the reader on screenshots paired with their text views. The reader learns to output each screen's text view, as in text practice, and the policy stays unchanged. Spent on the reader, the screenshot budget gives 80.9% full-task success on new task instances, against 55.6% when spent on training the policy. Given the same labeled screenshots, adapting the reader also beats adapting the policy, and a single aligned reader improves every text-trained policy we tried without retraining any of them.



### ConserveEdit: Source-Anchored Transactions for Drift-Bounded Iterative Image Editing
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22724v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22724v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 12:58:37+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 12:58:37+00:00
- **Authors**: Shuyang Jiang, Fucheng Deng, Yuchuan Luo, Zhenyu Wu
- **Comment**: 28 pages, 15 figures, 16 tables; includes supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Instruction-driven image editors produce convincing local edits in one pass, but inside an iterative refinement loop every accepted output becomes the next input, so unintended changes outside the requested region accumulate. We present ConserveEdit, an editor-agnostic controller that treats each refinement step as a transaction against a contract compiled once from the source image. The contract separates what may change (the authorized region of each target, with a bounded halo for shadows and reflections) from what must survive (protected content with explicit tolerances). Candidates from a frozen editor are composed through a source-anchored mask and committed only if target progress, drift on protected content, and image quality pass as separate tests; otherwise the step is rolled back and repaired. For any editor, this keeps every pixel outside the authorized region bit-exact and bounds drift independently of the number of iterations, whereas no fixed scalar trade-off reproduces the separate tests. On all 1053 MagicBrush test edits with a frozen Qwen-Image-Edit, masking is necessary but not sufficient. A direct call already alters the rest of the image (12.1 dB PSNR outside the mask); composed through the source-anchored mask, it stays bit-exact there and its DINO similarity to the ground truth rises from 0.67 to 0.85, matching ConserveEdit, so this gain comes from the mask. Next to automatic masks, however, masked direct editing changes a protected face or text on 48% of turns, and masked scalar selection avoids this mostly by abstaining (23% constrained success). Because its drift test names the violated entity, ConserveEdit shrinks the support instead (2% violations, 53% constrained success); in a simulator with known ground truth it raises constrained success from 55.0% to 61.8%. Automatic grounding remains the main bottleneck. Code and logs will be released.



### SlopeGuard: Towards Generalizable AI-Generated Image Detection via Radial Spectral Slope Guidance
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22727v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22727v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 13:00:56+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 13:00:56+00:00
- **Authors**: Cheng Fu, Yiheng Li, Yang Yang, Wenzheng Liu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: AI-generated image detectors often overfit to generator-specific artifacts, hindering generalization to unseen synthesis pipelines. Existing frequency-based methods usually learn high-dimensional spectral patterns that may preserve generator-dependent variations. We explore Radial Spectral Slope, a compact descriptor of global spectral decay that captures transferable spectral trends while suppressing fine-grained variations. Our analysis shows that real and generated images exhibit systematic but generator-dependent slope differences, indicating that slope provides useful forensic evidence but is insufficient as a standalone decision criterion. This motivates combining spectral slope with semantic representations for more robust detection. Based on this insight, we propose SlopeGuard, which couples slope-aware data processing with slope guided feature learning to construct informative patch-based views, integrate Radial Spectral Slope with semantic features, and adapt prior preservation according to spectral atypicality. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SlopeGuard achieves state-of-the-art mean accuracies of 97.5% on AIGCDetectBench, 97.8% on GenImage, and 85.1% on AIGIBench, while maintaining competitive performance under blur, cropping, JPEG compression, and additive noise.



### LoopZip: Visual Token Compression for Efficient Looped Vision-Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22728v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22728v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 13:01:13+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 13:01:13+00:00
- **Authors**: Weizhong Huang, Zhicheng Liu, Yan Zhang, Fei Chao, Xiawu Zheng, Liujuan Cao
- **Comment**: 11 pages, 10 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Looped vision-language models (VLMs) deepen computation by reusing shared layers, approaching larger models' capabilities with fewer parameters and easing the storage and deployment burden of stacking independent layers. However, repeatedly processing long visual sequences still incurs substantial compute and GPU memory costs. Visual token compression reduces these costs by shortening sequences. Existing methods mainly select original tokens directly or combine selection with aggregation. In looped models, the former may overlook changing token importance across calls and discard information needed later; the latter lack joint consideration of output changes across calls, limiting information preservation. We propose LoopZip, a training-free visual token compression algorithm addressing both issues. Visual aggregation builds a compact candidate pool before the looped backbone, and cross-invocation selection averages instruction attention from the first two existing calls of the same shared attention layer to retain tokens needed by both states. Weight-tied value correction then jointly fits shared visual-value adjustments to both pre-selection layer outputs, reducing output deviations across states. To validate LoopZip's effectiveness, we conduct extensive experiments covering two open-source looped VLMs, eleven benchmarks, and three pruning ratios. On LoopVL-1B, LoopZip retains 97.16% of the original model's performance at 50% pruning ratio and exceeds the strongest baseline by 5.36% at 87.5% pruning ratio. Across Hugging Face Transformers, vLLM, and SGLang, LoopVL-1B with LoopZip reduces peak allocated GPU memory by 58.36%-58.98% and achieves 2.01-2.47x end-to-end speedups over the original model.



### Evolve in Panorama, Render in View: Panoramic World State Evolution for Video World Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22733v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22733v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 13:03:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 13:03:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Longtao Jiang, Mingfei Han, Jianmin Bao, Tingjun Dai, Jinjie Wei, Zhiran Zhang, Xin Tao, Zhihui Li, Salman Khan, Xiaojun Chang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Video world models should maintain a world that keeps evolving as the viewpoint changes. Yet when the camera turns away and returns, current models often freeze the unobserved scene, lose objects, or change their appearance, because content outside the view has no representation that keeps changing. We present PanoEvo, which uses a Panoramic Evolver to evolve the world state in the latent space of a panoramic video diffusion model, where every viewing direction is present at every time step, so the scene evolves whether or not it is observed. Existing panoramic video models are not trained to evolve a scene from a single image or the objects within it, so we build PanoEvo-Data, an event-centric dataset from real 360° videos, and train the Panoramic Evolver coarse-to-fine as an image-to-panoramic-video model. Since each view covers only a small part of the low-resolution panorama, directly projected views are blurry and often show low-quality details or structural errors. A Perspective Renderer, built on a perspective video diffusion model, reads this state via latent-space projection and direction-restricted cross-attention to render sharp, high-quality views. PanoEvo achieves the best overall score among 23 publicly evaluated models on WRBench and the highest total score on STEVO-Bench. Code, models, and data will be released.



### RefCloth: Transferable Adversarial Tablecloths for Vision-Language-Action Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22736v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22736v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 13:06:53+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 13:06:53+00:00
- **Authors**: Shaozhen Liu, Zhiwei Li, Jiayang Sun, Shuangjun Du, Yi Zhong, Shenrui Ma, Yaozu Liu, Qi Li, Zhenan Sun
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Closed-loop vision-language-action (VLA) policies rely continuously on multi-view cameras, yet existing visual attacks typically hijack sensor streams with 2D patches or alter target object meshes that require retraining when manipulation objects change. We introduce RefCloth, a framework that optimizes one persistent adversarial tablecloth on a frozen source policy and deploys it zero-shot across unseen tasks and target models without modifying task objects or cameras. Throughout manipulation, static third-person and moving wrist views observe this shared world-bound texture under evolving occlusions. To generalize across shifting surface views while bridging the gap between differentiable and native renderers, RefCloth combines bounded texture parameterization with shared surface UV transformations and a same-path clean reference that removes static rendering offsets at initialization. Across four LIBERO suites in native simulation, one frozen texture transfers from OpenVLA-OFT to $π_0$ and $π_{0.5}$, reducing success relative to an unoptimized cloth by 62.50 and 43.75 percentage points on average and far exceeding amplitude- and variation-matched random controls. Dual-view audits and target closed-loop camera interventions show that moving wrist exposure drives over twice the standalone drop of the main view with strong synergy, while ablations confirm the roles of UV diversification and path calibration.



### GaussianMamba: Geometric and Relational State Space Scanning of 3D Gaussian Primitives
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22742v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22742v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 13:11:52+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 13:11:52+00:00
- **Authors**: A. Enes Doruk, Ozgur Erkent
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: 3D semantic occupancy prediction requires representing complex spatial structures and modeling contextual relationships across a scene. 3D Gaussian primitives provide a sparse and adaptive representation of scene geometry and semantics, while Mamba offers efficient sequence modeling for capturing dependencies among primitives. However, Gaussian primitives are irregular and unordered, making sequence construction challenging, as conventional spatial ordering may overlook their anisotropic geometry and feature-semantic relationships. In this paper, we present GaussianMamba, a dual-branch Mamba framework that explicitly incorporates Gaussian-specific geometric and feature-semantic relationships into complementary scanning strategies. The first branch refines a locality-preserving traversal using anisotropic Gaussian support derived from primitive scale and orientation. The second branch reorders spatially organized groups according to feature-semantic affinity and adjusts local features using geometry-weighted semantic differences. The resulting sequences are processed by separate Mamba modules, and their features are fused through a support-aware mechanism that incorporates local feature discrepancies arising from the distinct scanning paths. Experiments on nuScenes-SurroundOcc and SSCBench-KITTI-360 demonstrate consistent improvements over GaussianFormer and GaussianFormer-2 in both IoU and mIoU.



### Teaching What to Look For: Privileged Perception Questions for Agentic On-Policy Distillation in Video Captioning
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22755v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22755v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 13:23:54+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 13:23:54+00:00
- **Authors**: Yifan Xu, Ruopeng Gao, Zhixiao Wang, Jiafu Tang, Yichun Yang, Zihao Wang, Yuanxing Zhang, Limin Wang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Detailed video captioning requires comprehensive and faithful descriptions of objects, actions, and temporal events. Fixed-frame sampling, however, can miss brief motions, small objects, and other fine-grained evidence, motivating captioning agents that actively seek relevant moments and inspect local regions. Training such behavior is challenging because, unlike video question answering, generic captioning instructions do not specify what visual evidence should be acquired. We propose Perception-Question On-Policy Distillation (PQ-OPD), which uses training-only Perception Questions (PQs) to guide active video perception. PQs specify what should be inspected without revealing the corresponding answers, encouraging the teacher to explore the video more comprehensively and focus on relevant visual evidence. Along trajectories generated by the student, on-policy distillation transfers this guidance to the student's reasoning, tool calls, and captions; no PQs are required at inference. Across three detailed-captioning and two caption-to-QA benchmarks, PQ-OPD consistently improves the corresponding untrained agents across teacher-student scales and input-frame budgets. Under our evaluation protocol, PQ-OPD achieves the strongest primary-metric results among the compared open-source models on DREAM-1K, CaReBench, ShortVidBench, MotionBench, and VidCapBench-AE. Our ablations, together with analysis of temporal coverage and answer leakage, further indicates that the student internalizes the training-time guidance as a more effective video exploration policy.



### ForceBody: Force-Paired Parametric Body Motion with Torque Uncertainty
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22769v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22769v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 13:35:10+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 13:35:10+00:00
- **Authors**: Joonwoo Kwon, Yufei Zhang, Xiaoming Liu, Zijun Cui
- **Comment**: Accepted at NeurIPS 2026 Evaluations & Datasets Track
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Forces and torques drive human motion, central to biomechanics, robotics, and physics-aware animation, yet they are often absent from learning-based motion pipelines for body reconstruction and generation. Some motion datasets provide force annotations, but the paired motion sequences are incompatible with the parametric body models used in modern learning pipelines. In addition, existing datasets typically obtain joint torques through inverse dynamics, which is inherently sensitive to the quality of the input kinematics; however, they do not quantify the reliability of these torque estimates. We close this gap with ForceBody, the first dataset to bring ground truth force supervision into a parametric body representation, shipped with per-sample torque uncertainty. ForceBody pairs the SKEL body model with ground reaction forces and inverse-dynamics joint torques across 10,386 motion trials (9.7M frames, 26.9 hours) from 140 subjects. Each torque label is paired with a per-frame, per-joint uncertainty annotation obtained by Monte Carlo sampling through the inverse dynamics pipeline. We benchmark six architectures (MLP, Conv1D, LSTM, GRU, Mamba2, Transformer) on ground reaction force and joint torque prediction from SKEL motion. As one example use of the released uncertainty, finetuning a Transformer with per-sample uncertainty weighting reduces the MAE of joint torque estimation by 5%. Code and data are at https://github.com/DominoAI-Lab/ForceBody-NeurIPS-ED-2026.



### Streaming Generative Video Compression with Embedding Alignment and In-Context Propagation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23352v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **eess.IV**, cs.CV, cs.MM
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23352v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 13:38:33+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 13:38:33+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhitao Wang, Hengyu Man, Han Liu, Wenrui Li, Debin Zhao, Xiaopeng Fan
- **Comment**: 15 pages, 18 figures, 5 tables, including supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Video compression at ultra-low bitrates presents substantial challenges in preserving perceptual quality. Although pretrained video diffusion transformers (DiTs) provide powerful generative priors, existing generative video compression (GVC) methods built on them typically rely on computationally expensive VAE decoders to reconstruct video frames. Moreover, jointly refining multiple frames to exploit the inherent spatiotemporal modeling capabilities of video DiTs introduces additional buffering delay. To address these challenges, we propose SGVC, a streaming generative video compression framework that adapts a pretrained latent video DiT into a causal pixel-space refiner. Specifically, SGVC projects decoded features from a contextual pixel codec directly into the DiT input embedding space. To bridge the representation gap between codec-derived embeddings and pretrained DiT embeddings, we introduce Embedding Alignment, which aligns the projected embeddings with input embeddings extracted from the original frames during training. The refined DiT hidden states are then mapped directly to the pixel domain through a lightweight reconstruction path, bypassing the pretrained VAE decoder. To further exploit the spatiotemporal priors of the video DiT while maintaining causal decoding, we introduce In-Context Propagation, which reuses layer-wise K/V states across frames to exploit historical generative context, improving temporal consistency without requiring clip-wise buffering. Extensive experiments demonstrate that SGVC achieves competitive rate-perception performance, with average BD-rate savings of 71.97% over S$^2$VC in terms of DISTS. Project Page: https://chigland.github.io/SGVC/



### NexSight: Verifiable Tasks for Fine-Grained Agentic Long-Video Understanding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22790v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22790v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 13:52:28+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 13:52:28+00:00
- **Authors**: Tianci Xie, Li Chen, Pinxue Guo, Rui Zheng
- **Comment**: 25 pages, including references and supplementary material
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Fine-grained questions about long videos ask how many times an action occurs, in what order events unfold, or exactly when a state changes. Answering them can require finding brief moments scattered across hours of footage. Agentic models that decide when and where to look promise to find these moments, yet this ability is hard both to measure and to learn. Sparse supporting evidence may establish that an action occurred without establishing its total count; verifying timeline-wide claims requires checking for missed occurrences and double counting. Answer-only supervision, in turn, does not explicitly supervise the search for evidence. We present NexSight, which builds verifiable tasks to both evaluate and train agentic long-video understanding. Its agentic pipeline, SightForge, accepts a task only if a verifier blind to the draft answer can reconstruct it from evidence densely reacquired from the raw video and counterexample checks fail to refute it. This yields SightLine, 310 evidence-traceable tasks from 108 long videos, evaluated through SightLoop, a shared multiscale harness supporting temporal exploration, local inspection, evidence integration, and revisiting while recording returned observations. On disjoint videos, solver trajectories on verified tasks become step-level supervision for observation decisions. The strongest model reaches only 54.52% on SightLine, yet 79.35% when given the annotated evidence. With under 6K examples, Sight-35B nearly doubles its base model's interactive accuracy (21.29% to 42.26%), outperforms the 397B model of its family, begins using explicit spatial inspection after post-training, which its base model never uses, and gains over 8 points on two external long-video benchmarks.



### Visual Harness Scales General VLMs to Medical Images
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22815v2
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22815v2)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:04:55+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 03:49:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Shaowen Wan, Hanqi Jiang, Zihao Wu, Junhao Chen, Weihang You, Ruiyu Yan, Bowen Chen, Tianming Liu, Lin Zhao
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We show that an appropriate visual harness can substantially improve the performance of general vision-language models (VLMs) across diverse medical imaging tasks. We introduce VERA, a medical visual harness that equips general VLMs with interactive visual tools and systematic diagnostic evidence management. With VERA, general VLMs interactively explore medical images and progressively acquire diagnostic evidence, which is preserved in a lossless memory for subsequent retrieval and use. After submitting an initial answer, the VLM receives an assessment of potential omissions and the reliability of identified findings. This assessment guides further image inspection and measurement, followed by answer revision in an iterative agentic loop until sufficient evidence is obtained. Across 11 tasks and four imaging modalities, VERA improves performance on all tasks with Qwen3.8-27B, with notable gains in spleen measurement (63.3% to 98.3%), renal search (57.5% to 71.2%), and pulmonary nodule detection (0.0% to 28.2%). High-capability API-based VLMs achieve even higher performance with VERA. For example, GPT-6.1 Sol improves pulmonary nodule detection from 10.8% to 69.2% with VERA, demonstrating the complementary benefits of stronger model capabilities and an effective medical visual harness. Our results highlight the potential of visual harnesses as a new paradigm for scaling general VLMs to medical imaging tasks.



### Pixel is All You Need
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22820v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22820v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:06:40+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:06:40+00:00
- **Authors**: Jie Chu, Haoting Shi, Fucheng Zhang, Jiawei Liu, Junwei Liao, Ying Wen, Muning Wen
- **Comment**: 20 pages, 6 figures, 7 tables, including references and appendices
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Text, images, audio, touch, and even robot actions can, in principle, all be rendered onto a single canvas, suggesting an ambitious goal of unifying all modalities within vision. Language provides the natural first test. To subsume it, a visual model must reason directly over text in pixels. Across a wide range of image, video, and unified multimodal generators, however, we find that their visual generation modules cannot perform symbolic reasoning over text on the canvas. They succeed only when an underlying text module or prompt rewriter already supplies the answer, effectively reducing the generator to a mere renderer, a limitation that post-training does little to resolve. To test whether this failure stems from pixels themselves or from current training paradigms, we train a family of purely pixel-space generative models from scratch without any text pathway. With images as their sole input and output, these models read questions, write intermediate steps, and produce answers directly on the canvas. Remarkably, they exhibit hallmark behaviors of large language models. Performance improves predictably with scale following a power law. Writing intermediate steps frame by frame substantially outperforms single-frame answering, echoing chain-of-thought, while reinforcement-learning post-training further strengthens reasoning. Moreover, sharing one canvas allows the same model to reason across modalities, a capability that text-only language models lack. To the best of our knowledge, this is the first systematic study of these phenomena in purely pixel-space generative models free of text tokens.



### A tiny object detection dataset for high-altitude open-water search and rescue
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22825v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22825v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:08:15+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:08:15+00:00
- **Authors**: Hui Xu, Chenfei Hou, Henglai Wei, Hao Wang, Yidong Jin, Xiulin Niu, Haoyang Zhang, Hui Zhang, Tianshi Xu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Large aerial platforms often operate above 1.5 km to improve the efficiency of open water search and rescue (OW-SAR). However, long imaging distances, wide fields of view, and limited sensor resolution reduce objects to a few pixels, increasing their susceptibility to environmental interference and making detection harder than for conventional small objects. To our knowledge, existing datasets focus on low-altitude scenarios and do not support automatic detection at 1.5-2.0 km. We therefore present TinyWOD, a tiny object detection dataset for high-altitude OW-SAR using lifebuoys as detection targets. It comprises 11,359 manually annotated images and 19,505 annotated instances spanning diverse environments, lighting conditions, water colors, and surface ripples. Compared with existing datasets, TinyWOD contains much smaller objects and a substantially higher proportion of tiny instances; its average equivalent side length (ESL) is 12.7 pixels, with 76.96% of in stances having ESLs below 15 pixels, reflecting actual high-altitude water-rescue imaging conditions. We define APtiny, a tiny object detection metric, and benchmark mainstream and state-of-the-art networks using this and conventional metrics. All evaluated networks effectively detect tiny waterborne objects, demonstrating TinyWOD's reusability and utility. We expect this dataset to support research on tiny object detection in high-altitude OW-SAR.



### Kolibri: Color Finds the Place, Refinement Sets the Pose for RGB-D Localization in Colored Point Clouds
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22829v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22829v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:14:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:14:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Leonel Aguilar, Bartu Tuncay, Christoph Hoelscher
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: An agent or robot in a scanned building must locate its camera in rooms that no model saw during training. Point cloud registration methods can align each RGB-D frame with the building's colored point cloud, accurately but at a high cost. We observe that geometric refinement brings nearby initial poses to the same final pose, so the learned component only needs to find the place. Kolibri does this with a graph network of 55,712 parameters that describes 10 cm cubes of points, called cells, by their color, local shape and neighbors. With the same refinement, it matches GeDi on 96 unseen ScanNet rooms (81.4% against 80.3% within 5 cm and 5°) and on a held-out test of 10 further rooms, in 82 ms per frame on an RTX 2080 Ti. The saved time allows for search without a room label. In one point cloud of 100 rooms, Kolibri loses 1.3 points against knowing the room, while retrieving the room first loses 56.0. In a hospital emergency department scene from a real scan, it localizes 86.5% of the frames rendered from the scan in 384 ms each, where GeDi needs 91 minutes per frame on cubes of the scan for a similar recall. The method needs colors that agree between the frame and the point cloud. On gray frames and across cameras with different color responses, point cloud registration methods keep the lead.



### Technical Report of Mobile Manipulator Robot for Industrial Environments
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22852v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV, eess.SY
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22852v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:34:18+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:34:18+00:00
- **Authors**: Aria Dadnavi, Matin Jamshidi, Kian Khatibi, Shahriar Karimi Makhsous, Saina Najafi Shabestari, Seyed Sobhan Hosseini Hajibekandeh, Zahra Mahaam, Reyhane Mohasseli, Ghazal Ghoreishi, Amirhossein Gholizadeh Behbahani, Mohammad Hossein Moaiyeri
- **Comment**: 25 pages, 22 figures, 4 tables. Technical report of Team Auriga's 2025 mobile manipulator (@Work) platform (Robotics and Intelligent Automation Laboratory, Shahid Beheshti University)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: This paper presents the design and implementation of Auriga Robotics' mobile manipulator robot, developed at the Robotics and Intelligent Automation Laboratory, Department of Electrical Engineering, Shahid Beheshti University. Hereafter, this platform is referred to as the @Work Robot. The robot targets industrial tasks, with an emphasis on improving efficiency in repetitive or hazardous work environments. It uses a 4-wheel Mecanum drive for omnidirectional movement and a 5-degree-of-freedom manipulator arm with a 3D-printed gripper for object handling and navigation. The electronics consist of custom PCB boards built around ESP32 microcontrollers, together with an NVIDIA Jetson Orin NX that handles high-level processing and real-time system control. On the software side, Hector SLAM provides mapping and localization, A* handles path planning, and YOLO performs real-time object detection. These modules draw on fused sensor data to deliver robust navigation and reliable collision avoidance. Overall, the system shows effective hardware-software integration, with consistent performance in autonomous navigation, object handling, and task execution in industrial-like settings.



### Keeping Parts Honest: Isolated Part Graphs for Faithful Vision Transformer Explanations
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22853v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22853v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:34:32+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:34:32+00:00
- **Authors**: Bernard Tomczyk, Gail Collyer-Hoar, Bryan Williams
- **Comment**: 12 pages, 6 figures, 6 tables. Supplementary material will be added in a later version
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Part and prototype-based explanations for Vision Transformers (ViTs) present a decision as evidence from individual image regions. However, self-attention mixes information across patches, so part-level features absorb context from the whole image and consequently fail to faithfully reflect the decision. We show that existing interpretable ViT classifiers depend heavily on this leakage: when each explained region is encoded in isolation, their accuracy collapses, and retraining them under isolation recovers only part of the lost accuracy. We propose a classifier that represents each image as a graph of isolated parts. Each part is encoded only from its own patches by a ViT whose last blocks are fine-tuned under isolation, preventing evidence leakage from elsewhere in the image. Parts are discovered without annotation and refined hierarchically through their sub-parts, making them more consistent and stable across images than those of competing methods. Each edge is computed from part pairs, and recovers evidence that attention previously shared across the object without affecting the parts. Matching the image graph to a learned prototype graph per class makes the prediction a direct combination of node and edge similarities, each computed only from the patches it is credited to, so every explanation is faithful by construction. Across 11 fine-grained and general-object benchmarks, our method exceeds the accuracy of existing explainable methods that rely on leakage, while providing explanations that reflect what the model actually uses.



### CaFe: Causally Faithful Conditional Traffic Scenario Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22855v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CR**, cs.CV, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22855v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:35:52+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:35:52+00:00
- **Authors**: Steven Yu Tung, Shubham Vaijanath Phoolari, Christoph Lauer, Abhinav Valada, Steven Peters
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Language-conditioned traffic simulation enables scalable test scenarios steered by natural language. The goal is to control selected agents while surrounding traffic reacts to their observed behaviour. For example, a vehicle instructed to cut in late should cause the following vehicle to brake only when the manoeuvre becomes observable. Yet simulators can leak private instructions across agents, causing surrounding vehicles to react before the instructed behaviour becomes observable. Such premature reactions defuse desired events and alter the ensuing closed-loop scenario. Existing benchmarks measure what agents do, not what they could have known, leaving this failure unmeasured. We formalise it as intent leakage and address it through evaluation, data and modelling. Our paired intervention test holds an instructed agent's executed trajectory fixed while varying its private instruction, isolating leakage-induced changes in surrounding agents. We also construct WOMD-PACT, an automatically generated, verifiable dataset of 531,099 WOMD scenes with timed behaviour tags with reaction episodes identified through a counterfactual ghost test and grounded private instructions. Finally, we introduce CaFe, a dual-stream diffusion-forcing simulator fine-tuned in closed loop that restricts inter-agent interactions to public beliefs, preserving legitimate anticipation without leaking private intent. Our evaluation reveals that existing conditioning designs displace 23-83% of nearby uninstructed agents through private-intent leakage, even when the instructed agent's executed motion is held fixed. CaFe eliminates measurable leakage while maintaining comparable WOSAC realism (0.763), demonstrating that causal faithfulness can be enforced without sacrificing measured simulation realism.



### Propose & Verify: Fast Reflection Symmetry Detection via Harmonic Fields
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22856v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22856v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:37:11+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:37:11+00:00
- **Authors**: Yishai Lavi, Shai Avidan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Detecting multiple reflection symmetries in 3D shapes requires aggregating geometric evidence over a large space of candidate planes, making traditional voting approaches computationally expensive. We introduce the Fourier Accelerated Symmetry Transform (FAST), a training-free framework that combines geometric pair voting with efficient spectral computation. Exploiting the fact that global reflection planes pass through the shape centroid, we reduce the search to plane orientations, while geometric consistency filtering and independent witnesses suppress unreliable pair hypotheses. Our key insight is to represent these votes as a signal over plane orientations and efficiently compute a continuous, smoothed symmetry field in the spherical-harmonic domain. Specifically, we exploit the Funk-Hecke theorem, the spherical counterpart of the Fourier convolution theorem for rotationally symmetric kernels, to transform Gaussian smoothing into multiplication of harmonic coefficients. This enables efficient field evaluation, from which symmetry candidates are extracted, refined, and geometrically verified. Across two benchmarks, including a new benchmark of highly symmetric shapes, FAST achieves the highest clean multi-plane detection $F_1$ scores among evaluated methods (0.861 and 0.947), with GPU inference below 4 ms per shape: over $100\times$ faster than the next-fastest evaluated multi-plane GPU baseline. FAST also demonstrates strong robustness to geometric noise and competitive single-plane detection, achieving 94.9% success on clean single-plane shapes.



### Beyond Co-Generation: Rethinking Jointness in Joint Audio-Video Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22857v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, eess.AS
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22857v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:37:31+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:37:31+00:00
- **Authors**: Jihoo Jung, Ji-Hoon Kim, Suyeon Lee, Gia Thinh Pham, Joon Son Chung
- **Comment**: Preprint
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Our aim is to advance the understanding of genuine audio-visual coordination in joint text-to-audio-video (T2AV) generation, with particular emphasis on semantic consistency. Evaluating audio-visual consistency in T2AV generation is nontrivial, as detailed text prompts obscure whether such consistency stems from genuine cross-modal coordination or merely from independent text following. To isolate the former, we introduce intrinsic jointness: the ability of audio and video to produce the same realization for details left underspecified by the prompt. Using semantically underspecified prompts (e.g., "musical instrument playing" rather than "electric guitar playing"), we investigate four recent models and reveal their limited intrinsic jointness. To address this limitation, we propose JointREPA, a simple representation alignment-based post-training method to facilitate cross-modal coordination. Since existing benchmarks use semantically detailed prompts, we introduce UNSAIDBench, a benchmark designed to evaluate intrinsic jointness under semantic underspecification. Experiments across two distinct models and three benchmarks, including UNSAIDBench, demonstrate that JointREPA consistently improves semantic consistency.



### LOCUS: Causal Support Windows for Attribute Control in Autoregressive Image Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22867v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22867v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:45:42+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:45:42+00:00
- **Authors**: Zijun Huang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Unified autoregressive (AR) image generators struggle to satisfy multiple attribute constraints in a single prompt. Although these attributes are decodable from image-token latents, editing those latents at test time remains difficult. Adding a fixed attribute direction to the prefix can reinforce a requested attribute but rarely overrides a conflicting one, such as turning a car red when the prompt specifies blue. We investigate how the effect of an intervention depends on where it acts in the image-token sequence. We introduce the causal support profile, which measures, for each attribute, how often patching image-token states within a given window flips the rendered attribute. Structural attributes (count and position) are most responsive in early, narrow windows, while appearance attributes (color, size, and material) respond over later, broader windows. A budget-matched comparison of early and middle windows shows that the effect of window location depends on the target attribute, and two-dimensional support maps show that the responsive regions track spatial content. Based on these profiles, we propose LOCUS (Latent Optimization in Causal Support windows), which diagnoses which attribute failed and routes test-time latent optimization to its corresponding support window. With the generator, optimizer, reward, verifier, and token budget held fixed, support-routed windows outperform late windows by 23.6 percentage points in GenEval++ macro accuracy. With the generator frozen, LOCUS raises Janus-Pro's GenEval++ accuracy from 24.6% to 46.8% overall and from 19.5% to 40.5% on multi-attribute tags. On GenEval, LOCUS repairs 48.1% of failed generations in one rollout, compared with 37.4% for prefix optimization, and needs 14% fewer rounds on average to succeed.



### Harnessing Image Diffusion Prior for Photo-Realistic Video Restoration
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22874v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22874v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:49:02+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:49:02+00:00
- **Authors**: Fanghua Yu, Hongyu An, Jinfan Hu, Xinqi Lin, Zhiyuan You, Jian Wang, Hongyang Li, Chao Dong, Jinjin Gu
- **Comment**: 40th Conference on Neural Information Processing Systems (NeurIPS 2026)
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent image generation models produce photo-realistic results with rich details, but transferring their powerful priors to video restoration remains difficult due to temporal inconsistency from stochastic detail synthesis. Existing video restoration methods improve temporal coherence but often sacrifice visual fidelity, leaving a gap between image-level quality and video-level stability. We propose a generative video restoration method that bridges this gap by enabling temporally consistent detail synthesis from image generation priors. Our method combines noise energy rebalancing to suppress structure-disturbing low-frequency stochasticity, motion-aligned noise warping to propagate details along object motion, a self-supervised denoising encoder to mitigate encoder-induced temporal uncertainty, and decoupled temporal attention to model cross-frame dependencies. Together, these components preserve fidelity, enhance fine-grained realism, and maintain temporal consistency. Extensive experiments show that our method substantially outperforms existing video restoration approaches in visual quality, detail richness, and temporal stability.



### SlideJev: Order-Stable and Tissue-Grounded Jev Decisions for Whole-Slide Pathology
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22879v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22879v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:53:04+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:53:04+00:00
- **Authors**: Lei Wu, Jiashuai Liu, Di Zhang, Chunze Yang, Jiusong Ge, Junbo Lu, Yongqi Liang, Wenjie Zhao, Ni Zhang, Chen Li, Zeyu Gao, Qidong Liu
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 6 figures, 3 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Whole-slide visual question answering (VQA) offers a natural-language interface to pathology, allowing diagnostic and phenotypic questions to be answered directly from tissue slides. However, current vision-language models (VLMs) rely heavily on linguistic priors: their answers are sensitive to the order of the candidate answers, and can be reached through shortcuts that bypass the tissue evidence. To address these challenges, we introduce SlideJev, a framework that separately verifies candidate answers against the slide and learns to ground these judgments in tissue evidence. To connect parallel candidate evaluation with visual evidence learning, we adopt Jev and score each candidate by the difference between support and rejection log-likelihoods from the original language-model head. We compute these scores in parallel using separate prompts with the same format and no option-position labels, then normalize them for answer selection. To ground these scores in tissue evidence, candidate-conditioned reading and comparison guide gated updates to visual tokens across decoder layers. With the backbone frozen, we train these visual modules through the same scores using question-answer and paired-image supervision. Across three whole-slide pathology VQA benchmarks, SlideJev achieves the highest mean overall accuracy and 100% answer consistency across four option orders. Image-control experiments further show that SlideJev achieves the largest average accuracy gains from correct images over mismatched and image-free inputs across the three datasets. Our code is publicly available at https://github.com/leowu-ai/SlideJev.



### Learning Agent-Centric Vision-Language-Action World Models for Autonomous Driving
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22880v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22880v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:53:49+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:53:49+00:00
- **Authors**: Guoqing Wang, Pin Tang, Xiangxuan Ren, Chao Ma
- **Comment**: Accepted by NeurIPS 2026
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Recent progress in integrating vision-language-action (VLA) models with world modeling has significantly advanced end-to-end autonomous driving by allowing systems to reason about imagined futures instead of merely reacting to current observations. However, most world VLA models generate future frames at the pixel level with plenty of dense visual tokens. Although such generated futures can be visually plausible, they preserve many driving-irrelevant appearance details, i.e., only a small portion of the tokens directly encode critical planning-relevant factors, such as agent geometry, motion dynamics, and interactions. This introduces a significant performance bottleneck. An important question thus arises: what world state should a world VLA model reason over to support planning beyond pixel-level prediction? We argue that planning-oriented world modeling should focus on a compact and structured agent-centric latent state that captures the interactions between objects in the driving scene over time. As such, we introduce AgentWorld, an agent-centric VLA world model that uses discrete agent tokens jointly for reasoning and trajectory planning. Each token is dynamically grounded to a surrounding agent associated with its object's state, such as semantic identity, 3D geometry, motion, and interaction context. AgentWorld learns these tokens through three key processes: explicit geometric grounding, reasoning-oriented supervised fine-tuning, and agent-oriented reinforcement learning. Extensive open-loop and closed-loop experiments under diverse driving scenarios show that AgentWorld enhances planning capabilities, fully exploits visual evidence and agent dynamics, and yields interpretable agent-centric world states.



### RoadAnnoter: An AI-Assisted Desktop Tool for Semantic Segmentation Annotation of Road-Scene Images
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22884v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22884v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 14:55:30+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 14:55:30+00:00
- **Authors**: Nazmul Haque, Arnob Protim Roy
- **Comment**: 33 Pages
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Accurate semantic segmentation of road-scene images is essential for road safety research; however, polygon-level annotation remains labor-intensive and limits labelled dataset development. This study presents RoadAnnoter v1.0, an open-source desktop application integrating six human-in-the-loop annotation modes: manual polygon delineation, SAM Prompt, Smart Select, Edge Fill, pretrained semantic segmentation using Cityscapes-trained SegFormer models, and task-specific segmentation using TinySegNet, a lightweight MobileNetV3-Small-based network trained with accumulated annotator-generated labels. SAM-based modes use promptable segmentation with SAM and MobileSAM, while Edge Fill combines Canny edge detection with colour-similarity-based flood filling in CIELAB colour space. SegFormer generates suggestions for common road-scene categories, whereas TinySegNet supports adaptation to project-specific features through iterative training. All six modes use a shared mask-to-polygon pipeline comprising morphological processing, Gaussian smoothing, contour extraction, and Douglas-Peucker simplification; annotations are exported in YOLO segmentation format. RoadAnnoter was used to annotate Google Street View images from recorded road accident locations in Bangladesh for a vision-language model-based investigation of free-flow speed estimation. Qualitative assessment across representative scenes demonstrated the complementary applicability of the annotation modes to diverse feature geometries. Visual comparison with Roboflow indicated similar qualitative boundary characteristics for principal road-scene regions, although no quantitative accuracy assessment was conducted. RoadAnnoter provides a locally operated framework for developing road-scene segmentation datasets for transportation engineering and road safety research.



### Wear and Go: Calibration Free Sparse Inertial Motion Capture via Online Sensor Setup Estimation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22894v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22894v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 15:02:54+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 15:02:54+00:00
- **Authors**: Yuanshuo Tan, Licheng Zhong
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Sparse inertial motion capture typically assumes that each sensor's anatomical identity and mounting orientation are known. We present MOSAIC (Motion-based Sensor Assignment and Inertial Calibration), a framework that recovers this setup from ordinary motion without a calibration pose. Our central insight is that body pose changes continuously while the sensor setup persists, allowing movements that are individually ambiguous to provide complementary evidence over time. A setup network processes unordered sensor streams with temporal and cross-sensor attention, then accumulates assignment and rotation evidence into a session-level estimate. The recovered setup canonicalizes measurements for frozen pretrained pose estimators, separating sensor configuration from motion reconstruction. For sensors worn at prescribed body sites, evaluation on real inertial recordings with randomized setups shows near-calibrated pose accuracy for bounded offsets and substantial benefits from accumulation under arbitrary orientations. Transfer across pose networks supports setup recovery as a reusable interface between interchangeable sensors and pretrained pose models.



### Impact of Pretraining Length, Model Size, and Annotation Budget on Downstream Clinical Tasks of Foundation Models in Radiotherapy
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22896v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22896v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 15:05:19+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 15:05:19+00:00
- **Authors**: Alejandro Cortina Uribe, Sidsel Højklint Poulsen, Ivan Richter Vogelius, Jens Petersen
- **Comment**: 9 pages, 5 figures, 2 tables. Supplementary Material included
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Cone-beam CT (CBCT) scans acquired during radiotherapy capture changes such as tumor shrinkage and radiation-induced tissue changes, offering great potential for foundation models that help monitor individual treatment responses. However, to our knowledge, no large-scale pretrained models for radiotherapy CBCT exist, and evaluating modeling choices is difficult since downstream tasks are not known in advance. We examine how pretraining length, model size, and finetuning strategy relate to downstream clinical performance, and how metrics tracked during pretraining correlate with it. We pretrain vision transformer encoders with masked image modeling on about 150,000 raw 3D radiotherapy CBCT scans, tracking representation quality via validation loss, effective rank, and linear probing. Downstream tasks are classification of feeding tube presence ($n=51,135$ CBCTs, $1,562$ patients) and clinical characteristics (T-, N-stage, sex, age, tumor site; $n=1,278$ patients) in head and neck cancer. We compare low-rank adaptation (LoRA) and full finetuning against no pretraining, varying annotated dataset size. Pretext validation loss was the most consistent predictor of downstream performance; effective rank and linear probing were also associated, but not consistently across model sizes and finetuning methods. LoRA performed comparably to full finetuning while training only 0.5-2.5% of the parameters. Fully finetuned models consistently outperformed models without pretraining across all sizes, with AUROC 0.91-0.97 vs. 0.58-0.85 for feeding tube classification and 0.99-1.00 vs. 0.71-0.97 for sex classification. Model size had only a small, inconsistent effect, suggesting smaller models suffice at lower computational cost. Code and pretrained models (non-commercial license): https://github.com/alexCortinaU/RT-CBCT_SSL



### You Only Encode Once: Sub-Pixel-Shifted Frames for Arbitrary-Scale Remote Sensing Super-Resolution with 2D Gaussian Splatting
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22898v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22898v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 15:05:58+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 15:05:58+00:00
- **Authors**: Canjie Liu, Liuqian Wang, Qi Ming, Wei Lu, Xiaohan Hao, Yuefei Wu, Wentao Chao, Lei Shi
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Arbitrary-scale super-resolution (ASSR) of remote sensing images remains challenging due to the limited high-frequency information in a single low-resolution (LR) observation. While multi-frame SR can exploit complementary sub-pixel information, existing approaches often require real multi-frame training sequences and repeated feature encoding, increasing data acquisition demands and computational costs. To address these limitations, we propose SPS-GS (Sub-Pixel-Shifted Gaussian Splatting), an encode-once framework that learns from multi-phase LR observations synthesized from individual high-resolution images, without requiring real multi-frame training sequences. Specifically, Phase-Coherent Aliasing Demodulation (PCAD) exploits shift-dependent aliasing through Fourier-based alignment, phase-aware residual fusion, and least-squares aliasing estimation, enabling multi-frame information to be integrated before a single deep encoding pass. Gaussian Projection Alignment (GPA) then iteratively refines Gaussian centers and colors using multi-observation back-projection to promote observation consistency. The refined Gaussians support continuous rendering at arbitrary scales. Extensive experiments on five remote sensing datasets demonstrate strong reconstruction performance against single-image and multi-frame SR baselines, while maintaining competitive inference efficiency at common scales. The code will be publicly released.



### BRepA: Graph-Based B-Rep Pretraining for Unified CAD Understanding and Generation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.22903v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.GR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.22903v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 15:08:35+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 15:08:35+00:00
- **Authors**: Dekai Zhu, Zhaiyu Chen, Stefan Gavranovic, Slobodan Ilic
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Boundary representation (B-Rep) of CAD models encodes geometric primitives and their relations as a graph structure, enabling efficient shape modeling within CAD software. Learning on B-Reps for downstream tasks, such as segmentation and novel shape generation, requires specialized neural network designs. Moreover, performance improvements and accelerated training can be achieved with effective pretraining strategies, which is particularly important for tasks with limited labeled B-Rep data.
