We therefore ask how much of the PO gain the base model can reproduce by stopping earlier. A positive end-of-sequence (EOS) logit bias lets the base model reach the recall of each PO model, and this early stopping alone reproduces 58 to 86 percent of the CHAIRi gain of three checkpoints on two disjoint COCO sets. Writing longer does not bring the lost categories back. Under greedy decoding, a negative EOS bias recovers only 2 to 9 percent of the lost CHAIR recall of these checkpoints, and 94 to 96 percent of the added object mentions repeat categories already named. On CHAIR, the base caption of the same image still names about half of the missed object categories. We therefore propose base-guided object-category restoration, which appends high-confidence base-only categories to the PO caption without training. With thresholds fixed on a development set, it brings three checkpoints within one point of the base recall on a held-out COCO set with a lower distinct-object hallucination rate than the base model. Against the recall-matched base stopping curve, its gain holds for RLAIF-V near the base recall and for RLAIF-V and CHAIR-DPO at an intermediate recall. We will release the code of the recall-controlled evaluation.



### Any2Building: Unifying Point Clouds and Images for Building Reconstruction via Shared Spatial Queries
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23187v2
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23187v2)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 18:32:04+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 08:22:49+00:00
- **Authors**: Hongye Hou, Zhuoxiao Li, Liu Xiangyu, Tengxi Wang, Jing Ou, Dongli Wu, Shuai Zhang, Tongyan Hua, Wufan Zhao
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Abstracting structured buildings from limited observations is essential for 3D urban modeling and digital twins. Partial point clouds and overhead images of the same building provide incomplete 3D surface measurements and visual cues to its footprint, roof geometry, and rooftop structures, respectively. This motivates a unified framework that aligns geometric and visual cues in 3D and reconstructs buildings from either or both modalities. Building on this connection, we present Any2Building, a unified model for Level of Detail 2 (LoD2) building reconstruction from partial point clouds, overhead images, or both, with optional coarse geometric priors. At its core is an occupancy-grounded shared spatial queries representation that organizes available observations within a common 3D grid. Supervised by complete building surface voxels, these queries connect observational evidence to target geometry, enabling a shared generator to learn structural priors across tasks and providing spatial guidance for recovering missing structure. A trained voxel conditional surface-generation and low-poly remeshing pipeline then converts the predicted structure into compact LoD2 building models. To support this framework, we introduce BuildingWorld-Image, comprising approximately one million paired samples of partial point clouds, overhead images, and LoD2 building models across 18 cities. Experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that Any2Building outperforms existing methods in geometric reconstruction accuracy across unimodal and multimodal point-cloud completion, single-image building generation, and prior-assisted generation, while maintaining detailed and robust recovery when point cloud input modality is degraded.



### Retrieval-Augmented Correction for Generalist Medical Image Segmentation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23189v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23189v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 18:33:42+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 18:33:42+00:00
- **Authors**: Vincenzo Marcianò, Xiaoming Zhang, Michela Antonelli, Sebastien Ourselin, Maria A Zuluaga
- **Comment**: Accepted in Transactions on Machine Learning Research (10/2026). Reviewed on OpenReview: https://openreview.net/forum?id=PeDqE0SQRe
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Automated segmentation is routinely used as the first step in quantitative image-based clinical workflows, where predicted masks are subsequently reviewed and corrected by human operators. Automated correction of the residual structural errors is currently addressed one anatomy at a time, so the number of trained correctors scales with the number of segmentation targets. We present GRACE, a retrieval-augmented refiner that edits an input mask under context drawn from a shared knowledge base of verified prior cases. The refiner is trained once on a multi-organ corpus spanning compact organs, cavities, branching vessels, and tumour lesions. In the held-out dataset-transfer evaluation, three held-out datasets query a fixed knowledge base built from training data only. A separate leave-one-anatomy-out experiment tests parameter-free index extension: verified cases of an unseen solid organ enter the knowledge base, and the refiner stays frozen. A light shape-aware projection head on a frozen multimodal encoder maps embedding similarity to the shape agreement of the masks, so the knowledge base returns cases that the refiner can use as a shape prior. We evaluate GRACE on 18 public datasets spanning CT, MRI and ultrasound, using nine refinement configurations and seven upstream segmentors that span weak, mid, and strong operating regimes. We report each change with the DSC of its input mask. On the weak tier, the input DSC is 0.386 and GRACE adds up to +0.35 DSC. On a strong segmentor, the input DSC is 0.932 and the mean change is small. Transfer to an imaging modality that is absent from training and from the index is partial and depends on the anatomy, and remains an open research direction. Our results suggest retrieval-conditioned refinement is a practical alternative to anatomy-specific correction models. Code: https://github.com/robustml-eurecom/grace



### Reading Scene States: Uncovering Safety Failures in Vision-Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23206v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23206v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 18:48:02+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 18:48:02+00:00
- **Authors**: Tongxi Wu, Yuyang Cheng, Jiacheng Xian, Menglin Yang, Kai Chen, Jian Zhang, Yang Gao
- **Comment**: VLM safety, multimodal jailbreaks, and scene-state reasoning
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision-language models (VLMs) can provide harmful assistance by interpreting visual events, even without an explicit request for the underlying harmful objective. We identify this \textbf{scene-state safety vulnerability} and introduce \textbf{SceneSafetyBench}, comprising 375 image--question pairs spanning Initial, Medium, and Ultimate scene states and five visual presentations. Across eight VLMs, scene-state inputs elicit harmful assistance with attack success rates (ASRs) of 45.6--72.3\%. Comparative analysis across three VLMs shows that scene-state inputs most closely resemble QR-related images in their layer-wise safety-related signal profiles, while exhibiting lower average late-layer readouts and distinct signal dynamics during generation. To turn this vulnerability into a systematic attack, we propose \textbf{S3TA}, which translates harmful objectives into scene states, queries image--question pairs independently, and assembles the responses. On seven MM-SafetyBench categories across eight open-weight and commercial VLMs, S3TA achieves a model-average ASR of \textbf{71.2\%, exceeding the strongest baseline by 6.1 percentage points}. Text-side filters and visual moderation provide limited protection. These findings highlight the need to safeguard the assistance models infer through scene-grounded reasoning, beyond screening input content.



### Distortion-Free Semantic Watermarks for Black-Box Generative Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23208v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.CR, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23208v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 18:49:17+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 18:49:17+00:00
- **Authors**: Aviv Yossef, Or Patashnik, Or Zamir
- **Comment**: Project page: https://avivyossef.github.io/distortion-free-semantic-watermark/ Code: https://github.com/avivyossef/distortion-free-semantic-watermark
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We present a provably distortion-free semantic watermarking algorithm that requires only black-box sampling access to a generative model and black-box access to a semantic embedding. A watermark is called distortion-free if, averaged over its secret detection key, it leaves the generator's output distribution unchanged; this provides a formal guarantee that watermarking does not reduce output quality. Conventional such watermarks are often brittle to transformations. This is especially problematic for images, where benign operations such as compression, cropping, or resizing change the entire pixel representation. Semantic watermarks therefore require detection to be robust to content-preserving transformations. Previous methods combining these properties rely heavily on specific white-box properties of the underlying model: either to autoregressive text generation or to diffusion-specific access, such as control of the initial noise or diffusion inversion. In contrast, our construction is fully generic: It only interacts with the generative model and the semantic embedding, a function that determines the notion of "preserving content" under which detection should be robust, via sample and query access. We then instantiate this framework for image generation. We characterize the two competing properties required of the semantic embedding: it should remain stable under transformations that preserve an image's content, but should distinguish independent generations from the same prompt. We train an image encoder directly for this tradeoff. The resulting encoder improves separation while maintaining stability over off-the-shelf alternatives and enables strong aggregate detection while remaining robust to various transformations. We demonstrate the effectiveness of our watermark scheme on both open-source and closed models, which is possible only due to restriction to black-box access.



### Learning Grounded Reasoning from Human Gaze
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23209v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23209v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 18:49:33+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 18:49:33+00:00
- **Authors**: Gabriel Sarch, Declan Campbell, Nicholas Budny, Kia Ghods, Katerina Fragkiadaki, Jonathan D. Cohen, Brenden M. Lake, Taylor W. Webb
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Human visual reasoning is driven by systematic exploration and extraction of task-relevant visual evidence. Human gaze provides a window into this process, making gaze a useful source of guidance for visually-grounded reasoning in vision-language models (VLMs). However, human gaze also includes noise and redundancies that may transfer poorly to VLMs. In this work, we investigate how human gaze data can be effectively leveraged to improve visual reasoning in VLMs, while maintaining alignment with human behavior. We collect 7,109 human gaze trials from a diverse and challenging set of visual reasoning tasks, and train grounded reasoning models with different forms of gaze guidance. Across evaluations in chart understanding, visual search, and counting, we find that using human gaze as a prior for grounded reasoning consistently outperforms supervision using the full gaze sequence. Based on these findings, we introduce GGR (Gaze-Guided Reasoner), which achieves greater human alignment and more reliable visual grounding while matching or outperforming leading grounded-reasoning baselines in accuracy. Reinforcement learning further improves task accuracy, gaze alignment, and grounding quality despite using no gaze supervision. Behavioral analyses show that gaze supervised models exhibit more visual exploration before arriving at an answer. Our results suggest that the value of human gaze lies in guiding spatial exploration and shaping reasoning behavior without requiring complete imitation of human behavior.



### Multimodal Masked Diffusion Autoencoder for Modeling Impaired Driving
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23211v2
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23211v2)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 18:50:22+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-18 14:24:55+00:00
- **Authors**: John Gideon, Anshul Gupta, Megan Applegate-Kenton, Kayli Battel, Todd J Rowell, Patricio Reyes Gomez, Laporsha Dees, Emily Sumner, Avinash Balachandran, Guy Rosman
- **Comment**: Equal contribution: John Gideon and Anshul Gupta. 8 pages, 6 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Human cognitive state (e.g., distraction, stress), gaze, and behavior interact with the driving environment in subtle ways that AI could use to assist drivers, but ground-truth labels for human cognitive state levels are costly to obtain at scale. We propose a multimodal masked-diffusion autoencoder, ID-MAE, that adds a self-supervised objective to impairment prediction: alongside predicting the impairment label as a masked token, it reconstructs masked gaze and trajectory. To probe the effects of different modalities and types of impairments on model inference, we introduce DIVERT, a driving-simulator dataset containing 103 hours of driving data (n = 109) with ten impairment inductions spanning cognitive, visual, and emotional states, recorded with gaze, vehicle state, and scene video. We demonstrate how ID-MAE affords significant improvements in impairment inference results on both the DIVERT dataset and existing datasets, and enables strong results in additional tasks, such as trajectory and gaze prediction. For location-matched pairs, our model achieves a paired AUC of 0.706 for impairment detection on DIVERT, compared to 0.590 without reconstruction loss, improving over existing baselines. We probe the design space in terms of input modalities, masking scheme, positional encoding, attention mechanism, and decoding. We also investigate the interaction between various impairments in training and inference, demonstrating that visually demanding distractions are separable from cognitive distractions. Code and data will be available upon acceptance.



### EviSlide-MIL: Multi-Query Pooling and Shared Prediction for Class-Incremental Whole-Slide Image Classification
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23212v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23212v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 18:50:42+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 18:50:42+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhouhan Qian, Sicheng Chen, Chad Wong, Tianyi Zhang, Enhui Chai, Zeyu Liu, Fei Xia
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Histopathological analysis of whole-slide images (WSIs) plays a critical role in cancer diagnosis, and multiple instance learning (MIL) is widely adopted for WSI analysis under weak supervision. As new tumor subtypes are introduced, extending existing MIL models requires learning new classes while retaining knowledge of previously learned ones. This presents a class-incremental learning challenge, where models must acquire new classes with limited access to historical training data and recognize all learned classes without task identifiers at inference. A major obstacle is forgetting, as updates to feature aggregation and prediction modules can degrade performance on previously learned classes. To address this challenge, we propose \textbf{EviSlide-MIL}, a class-incremental WSI framework that integrates multiple learnable queries per class with a shared prediction module. The queries aggregate frozen patch features into class-specific representations, while the shared prediction module limits parameter growth across incremental stages. By freezing queries associated with previously learned classes, EviSlide-MIL preserves their attention patterns for identical inputs, while experience replay and knowledge distillation further mitigate forgetting as shared parameters are updated. Experiments on eight tumor subtypes from The Cancer Genome Atlas demonstrate that EviSlide-MIL achieves improved performance and reduced forgetting compared with competing methods across most learning settings, demonstrating its potential for scalable and knowledge-preserving WSI classification. Code and pretrained models will be released.



### FastFrames: Video Generation at Interactive Latency
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23226v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23226v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 18:59:13+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 18:59:13+00:00
- **Authors**: Gene Chou, Guandao Yang, Fujun Luan, Lars Mescheder, Wei Dong, Stephan Richter, Vladlen Koltun
- **Comment**: Project page: https://apple-aiml-research.github.io/ml-fastframes/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Autoregressive video diffusion models generate frames based on new observations and inputs, making them a promising medium for interactive visual experiences. However, existing models are orders of magnitude too slow for fluid interaction. This paper introduces FastFrames, a family of fast autoregressive video diffusion models that achieves interactive latency without compromising quality. We profile the inference pipeline of state-of-the-art models and find that operations often treated as fixed implementation details, such as VAE decoding and KV cache updates, in fact account for over half of end-to-end inference latency. We challenge the assumption that these components are indispensable and replace them with lightweight alternatives. Our 1.3B variant runs at 36 ms per frame, or 28 responses per second (RPS), on a single B200 GPU while matching the generation quality of a state-of-the-art model that is nearly 8x slower. Our 14B variant runs at 45 ms per frame, or 22 RPS, on an 8x B200 GPU node and yields significantly better visual quality and realism. We show that FastFrames enables real-time interaction in applications such as camera-conditioned and physics-conditioned video generation.



### Spool: Low-Latency Streaming Inference for Hybrid Vision-Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23228v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23228v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 18:59:27+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 18:59:27+00:00
- **Authors**: Rui Ning, Om Chabra, Hari Balakrishnan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Applications such as visual accessibility agents and robotics require answering questions about recent video content promptly, over a bounded window of past frames (e.g., the last two minutes). Hybrid vision-language models (VLMs), which interleave attention with linear recurrent layers such as Gated DeltaNet (GDN), are attractive for this setting, but maintaining a sliding window is expensive. The reason is that recurrent layers combine all past frames into a shared state, so expiring old frames normally requires re-running the entire window through the model. We observe that the recurrent update is affine in the state with input-dependent coefficients, so the effect of each frame can be captured as a transformation that composes associatively. We present Spool, a training-free framework that caches per-frame state transformations and composes them efficiently as the window advances, removing expired frames from each recurrent layer's state without replaying frames through the backbone. Removal is exact within each layer; as with key-value eviction in attention, expired frames can retain residual influence through the cached inputs computed by earlier layers. We design a dedicated data structure and GPU kernels to achieve low caching and composition overhead. Across five open-source hybrid VLMs and four video reasoning benchmarks, Spool achieves the lowest p95 time-to-first-token in all 20 model-benchmark tests, reducing it by a geometric mean of $6.6\times$ over the faster of two recent vLLM baselines, while improving accuracy by 2.3 percentage points on average.



### Lossless Reversible Watermarking for AIGC with Applications in Authentication
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23395v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CR**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23395v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:00:41+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:00:41+00:00
- **Authors**: Yupeng Qiu, Han Fang, Ee-Chien Chang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: AIGC watermarking is commonly used for tracing, but it can also support applications such as authentication and tampering detection. We study lossless reversible watermarking, which enables exact recovery of the original watermarked image after permissible distortions. By exploiting the relatively low entropy of the information required for image generation, together with robust latent-space watermarking, we design a framework that preserves sufficient information for lossless recovery. We then apply the framework to AIGC authentication, which generally faces two key challenges. First, the authentication mechanism must resist adaptive adversaries. Second, the content relevant to authentication, such as visual designs or expressed messages, may depend on application requirements that are not known at generation time. To solve these difficulties, we incorporate cryptographic message authentication to provide unforgeability against adaptive attacks. Lossless recovery provides the original image as reference evidence, after which a suitable analyzer can be selected to make the authentication decision. Experiments on Stable Diffusion demonstrate exact recovery under a wide range of distortions.



### RFBench: A Simulator-Grounded Multimodal Benchmark for Passive RF Layout Design and Understanding
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23410v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23410v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:10:14+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:10:14+00:00
- **Authors**: Jiarui Cui, Boxun Yan
- **Comment**: 29 pages, 4 figures. Code: https://github.com/jerry66s6/RFBench . Data: https://huggingface.co/datasets/Jerry1030/RFBench
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: While multimodal language models can interpret diagrams and generate structured outputs, their capacity to connect passive radio-frequency (RF) design intent, layout geometry, and electromagnetic behavior remains largely untested. To address this gap, we introduce RFBench, a simulator-grounded dataset and benchmark for passive RF layout design and understanding. The dataset contains 10,000 two-port planar layouts simulated using ADS Momentum, with each layout represented by editable geometric primitives, a raster image, a 16x16 occupancy grid, complex S-parameters at 201 frequency points, a structural category, and an RF response label. Building on these aligned representations, we define seven tasks spanning specification-conditioned layout generation, representation translation, response prediction, simulator-based validation, design diagnosis and improvement, structural understanding, and response classification. Extensive baselines across seven multimodal language models reveal systematic weaknesses in generating structurally valid layouts and predicting electromagnetically faithful responses. We show that RF-specific fine-tuning increases T1a structural validity from 0.0% to 83.2% and the T3 primitive-input within-2-dB rate from 17.7% to 62.8%, demonstrating the value of domain adaptation while leaving considerable room for further progress. RFBench establishes a common testbed for evaluating multimodal RF capabilities and guiding the development of models that connect design intent and layout representations with physical behavior.



### OCLDA-GS: Object-Centric Local Deformation-Aware Gaussian Streaming
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23416v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23416v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:19:52+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:19:52+00:00
- **Authors**: Ashim Dahal, Nikesh Subedi, Rabab Abdelfattah, Nick Rahimi
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Streaming 3D Gaussian Splatting (3DGS) allows for online reconstruction of dynamic scenes from multi-view videos for Free Viewpoint Video (FVV). Current time and storage efficient methods rely on selecting a subset of anchor Gaussians and neighborhood interpolation for fast streamable 3DGS. However, these methods can overlook object-specific motion and introduce inconsistencies near object boundaries. We propose OCLDA-GS, an object-centric streaming framework that dynamically reallocates object and motion-aware Gaussian anchors to allocate anchor Gaussian support for objects undergoing large motion while at the same time, restricting the gaussian neighborhood within object groups for more precise object boundaries. Furthermore, it estimates object-wise rigid transformations (e.g. a hand moving) and leverages these estimates to learn per-anchor local deformation correction for small deformations (e.g. wrinkles on a shirt). Then, we conduct experiments on three dynamic multi-view video datasets and demonstrate improvements over the previous baselines alongside improved quality between refinement timesteps. Under matched refinement budgets, our method improves PSNR over Instant Gaussian Stream (IGS) by 0.169 dB on N3DV, 0.202 dB on Meeting Room and +0.199 on Google Immersive, while reducing the mean PSNR drop between refinement timesteps by 9.2% and 13.8% in N3DV and Meeting Room, respectively.



### Autoregressive View Model
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23417v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23417v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:20:06+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:20:06+00:00
- **Authors**:  Jensen,  Zhou, Samuel Rota Bulo, Denis Rozumny, Duncan Zauss, Peter Hedman, Nikhil Keetha, Philip Torr, Christian Rupprecht, Andrea Vedaldi, Peter Kontschieder
- **Comment**: Project Page: https://autoregressive-view-model.github.io/
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: We present the Autoregressive View Model (AVM), which generates a scene as a stream of views without constructing an explicit 3D representation. Through a unified in-context interface, AVM conditions on images or videos to generate short clips along a requested camera path. Visual context grounds reconstruction and interpolation, while a learned prior enables extrapolation into unseen regions. AVM retrieves relevant views from inputs and generated history to form a bounded active context. We fine-tune a pretrained bidirectional video diffusion transformer with flow matching in an N-in-1-out formulation. We then tune a causal model on its own rollouts to improve photorealism, reduce denoising steps, and limit long-horizon drift. We scale training to approximately 8M scenes and video clips. We evaluate generation capacity and scene persistence across benchmarks for camera control, text control, visual quality, dynamics, sparse and 4D reconstruction, and long-horizon consistency. The results demonstrate that AVM adapts to diverse input and output settings through one in-context interface.



### LIC-Audit: How Comparable Are Reported Gains in Learned Image Compression?
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23419v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **eess.IV**, cs.CV, stat.AP
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23419v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:21:03+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:21:03+00:00
- **Authors**: Haisheng Fu, Jie Liang, Runyu Yang, Feng Ding, Xin Dong, Zixiao Jin, Jingning Han
- **Comment**: 31 Pages, 4 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Learned image compression (LIC) papers often report BD-rate improvements of only a few percent. They are measured against VTM reference software whose version keeps changing: the claims we audit name 7 versions, 5 in 2025 papers alone. But how comparable are these numbers across papers, and what evidence should future papers give for such small gains? We introduce LIC-Audit, an analysis of 353 released rate-distortion curves from 36 repositories, and a controlled re-evaluation of eight public codec releases with 28,193 encode-decode records. We identify three problems. First, different VTM reference runs change BD-rates by up to 3.56 percentage points, and on CLIC Pro published numbers order 10 of 55 method pairs differently from a common computation; comparing codecs directly over a fixed quality range removes this dependence. Second, releases use different and mostly undocumented conventions for counting bits, computing PSNR, and padding images. Under consistent measurement, the GLIC method's 1.00% lead over the HPCM method on Tecnick becomes a 1.43% deficit, changing only the padding reverses the comparison of HPCM and CMIC, and most gaps narrow when MS-SSIM replaces PSNR. Third, average curves hide how much results vary from image to image, so whether a small gain is statistically significant depends on the assumed variation. An optimistic assumption calls 12.0% of comparisons between equally good codecs significant on test sets as small as Kodak, instead of 5%; a cautious one leaves 18 of 45 differences between recent codecs undecided, yet per-image results show 12 of them to be significant. Beyond auditing past results, LIC-Audit provides a versioned evaluation protocol, a public leaderboard website with a scoring tool, source-linked benchmark data, per-image measurements, and reporting guidelines, to help future LIC papers make comparable and verifiable claims of progress.



### Bits, Not Boxes: Label-Free Layer Selection for Visual Localization in VLMs
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23432v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, stat.ME
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23432v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:37:06+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:37:06+00:00
- **Authors**: Haisheng Fu, Gexin Huang, Haikai Zhao, Jie Liang, Zhenman Fang, Jingning Han, Xiaoxiao Li
- **Comment**: 35 pages, 9 figures, 56 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Attention maps cheaply show where a vision-language model (VLM) looks, but attention sinks make them unreliable for localization. Contrasting the question with a generic prompt suppresses sinks, yet only some layers localize the queried object. Existing methods find these layers with box annotations or fixed rules. We show that attention itself can identify them. A question changes how much a head attends to the image and where this attention goes. The two effects separate exactly, and the second, measured in bits on unlabeled questions, indicates which layers localize. WhereBits turns these where-to-look bits into a layer prior and combines it with per-head contrastive scores. Its three design constants were fixed once on labeled COCO data, and a new model or resolution needs only unlabeled questions. Across eight VLMs, WhereBits matches the pointing accuracy of box-tuned layers on COCO and RefCOCO and ranks target tokens better. This advantage holds on a held-out model family and at higher resolutions. Its COCO pointing accuracy is 0.72, against 0.22 for mean attention. In training-free zoom-in, its crops raise V* accuracy by 6.0 points under forced-choice evaluation.



### TransFIRA+: Transfer Learning for Biometric Recognizability Assessment
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23442v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23442v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:46:13+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:46:13+00:00
- **Authors**: Allen Tu, Kartik Narayan, Joshua Gleason, Jennifer Xu, Matthew Meyn, Tom Goldstein, Vishal M. Patel
- **Comment**: Project Page: https://transfira.github.io/plus
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Biometric recognition in surveillance, video, and web imagery must contend with extreme variation in pose, blur, illumination, occlusion, and distance, where visual quality metrics fail to predict whether inputs are recognizable to the deployed encoder. Existing face image quality assessment methods rely on visual heuristics, curated annotations, or costly generative pipelines, leaving their predictions detached from the encoder's decision geometry. We introduce TransFIRA, a lightweight framework that grounds recognizability directly in embedding space. TransFIRA derives class-center similarity (CCS) and class-center angular separation (CCAS) from a pretrained encoder's embeddings and learns to predict them from images without quality annotations. Using predicted CCS for template weighting and CCAS>0 as a natural filtering threshold yields state-of-the-art face verification performance on BRIAR, raising TAR at $10^{-6}$ FMR from 0.085 with uniform averaging to 0.598. The predicted scores also reveal how image degradations affect encoder-specific recognizability. TransFIRA+ extends this framework to cross-camera multimodal person identification in untrimmed surveillance video. In this setting, body recognizability scores saturate within a narrow range, making direct weighting effectively uniform. We introduce a post-hoc sigmoid calibration that transforms these saturated scores into informative weights without retraining. After calibration, the BRIAR-trained TransFIRA predictor improves fused face and body identification on two unseen multi-camera datasets, outperforming detector-confidence weighting and uncalibrated CCS-rank weighting. Together, TransFIRA and TransFIRA+ demonstrate that encoder-grounded recognizability generalizes across biometric modalities and recognition settings.



### Gaussian Splatoms: Direct Atomic Structure Identification in Electron Tomography
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23444v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **eess.IV**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23444v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:47:23+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:47:23+00:00
- **Authors**: Nalini Singh, Tiffany Chien, Cedric Lim, Arthur R. C. McCray, Colin Ophus, Laura Waller
- **Comment**: 26 pages, 14 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Atomic electron tomography (AET) computationally reconstructs the 3D atomic structure of materials from a series of 2D projection measurements acquired at different tilt angles. Inspired by Gaussian splatting techniques from computer vision, we introduce an atom-centric parameterization for modeling AET samples. Instead of a conventional volumetric representation with a grid of voxels, we model the sample as a list of atoms, where each atom is a 3D Gaussian characterized by learned location, scale, and amplitude. The Gaussian model is a natural fit for atoms that allows us to easily incorporate prior information grounded in physics; we impose isotropy of atoms, a minimum interatomic distance, and realistic atomic radii. We show in simulation that our method is more robust than volumetric baselines to real-world imaging challenges, including a limited number of measurements and low electron dose. We further show that our method yields a more physically plausible reconstruction of an experimental 23,000 atom iron-platinum nanoparticle than a previously published volumetric reconstruction. Beyond tomography, our representation is a promising tool for any atomic resolution imaging modality (e.g. multislice electron ptychography).



### Why Sub-4-Bit Vision CNNs Collapse and Where Mixed-Precision Recovery Stops
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23445v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23445v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:49:27+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:49:27+00:00
- **Authors**: Michael Schroeder
- **Comment**: 8 pages, 6 figures, 2 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Deep vision models operating in resource-constrained environments depend heavily on low-bit quantization, yet compressing weights below 4 bits frequently induces catastrophic accuracy collapse. While mixed-precision recovery strategies aim to rescue accuracy by selectively retaining high-precision parameters, the fundamental mechanics of where and why these models collapse remain poorly understood. In this study, we systematically analyze the anatomy of quantization collapse across standard Vision CNNs (including ResNet and EfficientNet backbones) evaluated on the full 50,000-image ImageNet-1k validation set. First, we map the exact progression of degradation, demonstrating that collapse is highly non-linear and concentrated in late-stage pointwise projections and depthwise layers. Second, we introduce a structured, multi-step recovery waterfall that evaluates selective integer precision (INT4/INT8) and selective floating-point scales. We show that keeping less than 5% of critical layer activations in 8-bit precision recovers up to 92% of the accuracy lost during aggressive sub-4-bit compression. Finally, we bridge the hardware reality gap by evaluating deployment performance on standard ARM and x86 CPU architectures. We show that while low-bit quantization reduces memory storage requirements, standard runtime software bit-unpacking introduces a 1.48x to 21.5x execution slowdown depending on batch size, highlighting a critical trade-off between model footprint and actual edge throughput. Our findings provide a practical blueprint for navigating the limits of sub-4-bit deployment without sacrificing hardware performance.



### Where Global Context and Weight Sharing Work in Compact Vision Backbones
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23446v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23446v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:49:55+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:49:55+00:00
- **Authors**: Michael Schroeder
- **Comment**: 8 pages, 5 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Compact convolutional backbones such as EfficientNet achieve strong parameter efficiency through local depthwise separable convolutions, yet two structural questions remain central to hybrid design: where does global self-attention improve recognition over pure convolution, and can repeated blocks share weights without losing representational capacity? We investigate both questions on ImageNet-1k (50,000-image validation) using controlled EfficientNet-B0 experiments. First, we show that wrapping a classification backbone in multi-scale feature pyramids (BiFPN) fails to outperform a plain MBConv trunk (69.99% - 71.81% vs. 72.86% plain B0) until paired with stage attention (73.15%). In contrast, keeping the MBConv trunk intact and inserting lightweight zero-gated residual self-attention adapters at stages P3 (28x28 windowed), P4 (14x14 global), and P5 (7x7 global) reaches 74.02% standalone and outperforms a bit-for-bit matched plain-B0 control by +0.954 percentage points (73.88% vs. 72.92%) across an 88-epoch stateless replay schedule, with learned attention amplitude rising monotonically with semantic depth (27.2% at P3 -> 45.4% at P4 -> 66.3% at P5). Second, we evaluate recurrent weight sharing across repeated MBConv blocks with pass-specific BatchNorm statistics (+1,536 parameters per pass). While post-hoc kernel averaging degrades accuracy to 17.03% and single-template stage tying (-27.6% parameters) plateaus at 66.10%, alternating two shared block templates ([B1, A, B, A]) in Stage 6 cuts total model parameters by 10.9% (582K weights) and recovers by +10.82 points in a single epoch to 70.92% (-1.84 points vs. unshared control).



### NIRJALA: Draining the Sea out of Gaussian Splatting with Three-Path Optics and Reversible Physics
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23450v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.GR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23450v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 19:53:32+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 19:53:32+00:00
- **Authors**: Latchan Chhetri, Aman Kumar
- **Comment**: 21 pages, 11 figures
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Water does not just tint an underwater photograph; it rewrites it. Each pixel mixes attenuated scene radiance, backscattered light, and scene light scattered forward on its way to the camera, and many combinations of scene and water explain the same pixels. Most Gaussian-splatting methods for underwater scenes model only the first two effects and remove the water by dividing by a transmission that decays exponentially with range, so their inversion gain grows without bound. We present NIRJALA (Sanskrit for "without water"), a 3D Gaussian Splatting framework that recovers water-free appearance and geometry together. TRIVENI, a three-path image formation model, adds a range-saturating forward-scattering term to direct transmission and backscatter; we prove that this keeps the inversion gain bounded at every range and bound how errors in the estimated backscatter and attenuation propagate into the recovered colours. PRATIBIMBA, a reversible physics anchor, ties a flexible neural water model to a ten-parameter physical model in both directions, so scene content cannot hide inside the water. SAMARASA collects the physics-informed objectives, including a range-binned colour prior. To score water removal against ground truth, we built SAJALA ("with water"), a 30-scene suite path-traced in Blender Cycles with water-free images, depth, and normals under controlled water colour and turbidity. On SAJALA, NIRJALA raises PSNR from 24.34 to 24.76 dB and lowers depth AbsRel from 0.109 to 0.091 against the strongest baseline; on real SeaThru-NeRF scenes it lowers LPIPS from 0.194 to 0.175 while rendering at 44 FPS. We also discuss where appearance metrics and geometry disagree underwater, and the limits of our evaluation.



### VeriMath: Human-in-the-Loop Generation and Verification of Curriculum-Aligned Mathematics Assessment Items
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23456v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23456v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 20:05:23+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 20:05:23+00:00
- **Authors**: Quy P. Nguyen, Duong Q. Nguyen, Dinh P. T. Nguyen, Truong-Giang T. Le, Truong A. Bui, Vi H. Vo, Quang-Thinh Bui, My-Phuong Ngo, Trong N. Do, Thao P. Le, Phuc M. Nguyen, H. Nguyen-Xuan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Automatic generation of mathematics assessment items is a growing area in educational artificial intelligence, yet classroom deployment remains challenging. Generated questions must satisfy multiple constraints: mathematical correctness, curriculum alignment, appropriate cognitive difficulty, and educator reviewability. While prior work has advanced text generation, reliable assessment authoring requires granular control over localized examination formats and expert educational judgment. To address this challenge, we present VeriMath, a generalized, human-in-the-loop framework for generating rigorous, curriculum-constrained mathematics assessment drafts. Using annotated reference items, the framework anchors generation to the requested examination section and cognitive reasoning level, then applies sequential automated verification and guided repair before submitting drafts for expert review. We evaluate VeriMath through a national curriculum case study using 4,555 mathematics items from the Vietnamese Grade 12 curriculum and 50 distinct generation requirements covering multiple-choice, true/false, and short-answer formats. Across two model backbones, the framework produces verifier-accepted drafts for 94% (47/50) of requirements per run. Blind expert evaluations confirm high mathematical accuracy and curriculum alignment, with strict teacher-readiness assessments accepting up to 56.8% of generated items. Analysis of rejected drafts reveals persistent domain-agnostic challenges involving formatting, statement validity, and solution-step completeness. These findings demonstrate that VeriMath can bridge generative AI and dependable educational applications by producing highly reviewable assessment drafts within tightly constrained national curriculum frameworks.



### mmTherm: Absolute 3D Human Pose Estimation from Low-Resolution Thermal Images and mmWave Radar
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23477v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23477v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 20:30:24+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 20:30:24+00:00
- **Authors**: Zhaoxin Chang, Badii Jouaber, Daqing Zhang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Absolute 3D human pose estimation (HPE) aims to recover both a person's body pose and their global position in 3D space. Privacy-preserving millimeter-wave (mmWave) radar provides distance and direction measurement capabilities, but its sparse point clouds make HPE under-constrained. Low-resolution thermal imaging can provide complementary observations of body direction and structure. However, our evaluation shows that a strong feature-level fusion baseline loses much of its localization and HPE advantage when generalizing to unseen subjects, with radial localization becoming less accurate than a radar-only baseline. Motivated by this finding, we introduce mmTherm, a lightweight framework that explicitly incorporates the complementary geometric properties of thermal imaging and mmWave radar. It combines body structure and joint directions estimated from thermal images with range information from radar to localize the body and reconstruct its 3D pose. Experiments on OctoNet show that mmTherm reduces cross-user absolute joint error by 33% and root localization error by 38% relative to the strongest corresponding baselines, while requiring substantially fewer parameters and less computation at inference.



### SpecLook: Speculating Where to Look for Fast and Reliable Agentic MLLMs
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23483v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CL, cs.CV, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23483v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 20:34:56+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 20:34:56+00:00
- **Authors**: Xi Ding, Jiawei Zhang
- **Comment**: Technical report
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Agentic multimodal large language models (MLLMs) that think with images interleave reasoning with zoom-in tool calls. However, this sequential loop is slow, and its answers carry no reliability score. Prior speculation of tokens, reasoning steps, or answers keeps the loop, and answer speculation also accepts confident errors. We find that the loop mostly adds one zoom-in step, with which a small model is ahead of the agent on most splits. Our key insight is to speculate the agent's next observation, which, unlike a drafted answer, can be verified, corrected, and used to test the answer. We propose SpecLook, a training-free speculate-and-verify framework with three levels. A small model drafts a glimpse and re-answers from it, and the question ends if the answer is unchanged and confident. Otherwise, the agent's large model verifies and corrects the glimpse without tools, and their answers are fused. Only questions that still lack evidence reach the agent, in one forward pass that SpecLook-Fast skips, so no question runs the loop. The trace scores every answer's reliability without labels. Extensive experiments on V*, HR-Bench, and POPE show that both are more accurate than every baseline on every split. SpecLook reaches 87.2% and 86.2% mean accuracy with 7.7x and 4.7x speedups over the DeepEyes-7B and Thyme-RL agents, and SpecLook-Fast reaches 9.9x and 6.3x.



### Reference-Guided Data Curation for 3D Medical Vision-Language Pretraining
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23484v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23484v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 20:35:32+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 20:35:32+00:00
- **Authors**: Faidra Patsatzi, Ashwin Kumar, Chong Wang, Curtis P. Langlotz, Reinhard Heckel, Akshay S. Chaudhari
- **Comment**: Accepted at WACV 2027. Code: https://github.com/StanfordMIMI/data-curation-3d-medical-vlm
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Training 3D vision-language models (VLMs) on large medical imaging datasets collected across institutions is constrained by data quality. Vast, uncurated pools of image-report pairs are characterized by redundancy, class imbalance, and cross-institutional heterogeneity. We address this challenge of data curation for improving 3D VLM pretraining by proposing an offline, training-free, reference-guided data curation recipe. Our approach leverages a small, high-quality reference set to independently extract quality signals for each modality of a large, uncurated pool and merges them through a rank-aggregation step into a single ranking that guides sample selection. We validate this method on datasets comprising 35k 3D abdominal CT volume-report pairs from two institutions and 58k 2D chest X-ray image-report pairs. On 3D CT, using our reference-guided data curation strategy, our curated model matches the zero-shot disease classification performance of a state-of-the-art baseline (Merlin) while using $3.3\times$ less training data and a reference set of 5k samples. On 2D chest X-rays, our method yields a curated model that matches or exceeds the zero-shot disease classification performance of a model trained on the full pool while using up to $10\times$ less training data and a reference set of only 74 samples. Ablations show that the selection is stable under resampling of the reference, that smaller reference sets still outperform random sampling, and that label rebalancing does not improve performance. To support reproducibility, we release our code.



### GRID-UAV: Geometry and Ray-depth Integrated Decision-making for Unmanned Aerial Vehicles
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23491v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23491v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 20:41:30+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 20:41:30+00:00
- **Authors**: Anas Anwarul Haq Khan, Amrin Kareem, Muhammad Umer Sheikh, Biplab Banerjee, Muhammad Haris Khan
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision-language-action models (VLAs) encode images with 2D encoders that capture little 3D structure. A common approach is to feed the policy estimated depth, but a UAV chooses its own altitude, must judge distances tens of meters away, where monocular depth is less reliable, and must relate its forward and downward views. On long aerial routes, adding depth features directly brings no significant gain (42.5% vs. 42.6% success), so we study how 3D information should be given to the policy. Analyzing flight failures, we find that two forms of 3D grounding fail in opposite ways: explicit 3D lifting of depth collides less on the way but more often fails to finish at the target, while implicit alignment with a 3D geometry model finishes more often but collides more. We propose GRID, which combines both: (1) it lifts monocular depth into ray-corrected 3D positions in the UAV's frame for both cameras, and (2) during training only, it aligns its visual features with a frozen multi-view geometry model, at no inference cost. GRID adding explicit lifting to implicit alignment significantly increases how often the policy reaches the target ($61.5\% \rightarrow 69.5\%$, $p<0.001$), and adding implicit alignment to explicit lifting significantly increases how often it completes the task once there ($57.7\% \rightarrow 61.4\%$, $p<0.05$). On TravelUAV (3,005 test episodes), GRID achieves the best success rate on every split, with relative gains over the state of the art of 9.5% (seen), 17.1% (unseen objects) and 25.4% (unseen map).



### MedHarness: A Unified Budgeted Runtime for Reliable Medical Imaging Agents
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23501v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23501v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 20:50:46+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 20:50:46+00:00
- **Authors**: Haofan Wu, Le Zhang
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Medical imaging agents already return answers on heterogeneous imaging surfaces, yet deployment still faces a limited verification budget where each wrong accept can harm clinical trust. When a thin check and a deeper evidence pass disagree on the same case, deciding when to stop, open more evidence, repair, or reject without a shared controller remains ill-posed. Tool-using workflows often lock a surface-specific evidence order per dataset, or a private always-open full-depth verification path; those pipelines stay costly, leave many wrong accepts, and cannot share one controller across modalities. We present MedHarness, a registered multi-surface runtime reliability layer that reformulates post-answer control as shared decision-object attachment after a proposer's initial prediction. Under Shared Decision Object, Minimum-Sufficient Verification, and Intermediate Repair, one shared policy with surface recipe adapters lets a new imaging surface join the same stop / open-evidence / repair / reject controller by recipe registration. On a 512-case live pool, MedHarness cuts wrong accepts versus a full-verification baseline by about 47% and raises accepted accuracy to 83.2% vs. 72.9% at 86.1% coverage versus full release under that baseline. On a failure-enriched multi-surface stress ranking, the same controller cuts estimated remote service calls per case by about 71% versus that baseline under a matched context-load setting. A held-out three-surface check and an alternate language-model transfer keep the same control ordering. The code is available at https://anonymous.4open.science/r/MedHarness-paper-release-A7F3/.



### BOS Summary: The First Token Matters
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23508v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.AI**, cs.CL, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23508v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 21:01:03+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 21:01:03+00:00
- **Authors**: Hui Zhu, Saleh Zare Zade, Rafi Ibn Sultan, Deng Pan, Arun Ross, Dongxiao Zhu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Large vision-language models (LVLMs) and language models (LLMs) often exhibit attention sinks, whereby particular positions, such as the beginning-of-sequence (BOS) token, receive a disproportionate share of attention. Under standard causal masking, however, the BOS query attends only to itself, so the representation that later tokens repeatedly access carries no information about the input. We introduce BOS Summary, a training-free inference-time method that repurposes this persistent pathway to carry input context. At a single transformer layer during prefill, BOS Summary relaxes the causal constraint for the BOS query row alone, letting BOS aggregate all visual and textual input tokens into a self-derived context representation while every other token retains standard causal attention. The enriched representation then remains accessible to the model throughout decoding, with no additional parameters, auxiliary modules, or decoding-time overhead. Across multiple VLM and LLM families and eight multimodal and text-only benchmarks, BOS Summary improves over direct and Chain-of-Thought prompting and outperforms recent sink-intervention methods on multi-step reasoning tasks. A mechanistic analysis shows that making BOS input-specific also weakens its sink dominance and redistributes attention toward content-bearing prompt tokens, indicating that changing what a sink carries reshapes how later layers allocate attention.



### Trust the Ray, Not the Depth: Ray-Lateral Agreement for Multi-View 3D Human Pose Fusion
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23513v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23513v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 21:05:50+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 21:05:50+00:00
- **Authors**: Farnoosh Koleini, Ahmed Helmy, Pu Wang, Xiwu Cao
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 5 figures, 1 table
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Put a monocular mesh estimator behind every camera of a calibrated rig, and the reconstructed bodies land a meter or more apart. The cause is geometric: each estimate sits at the depth implied by a focal length the network assumed, not the one the camera has. Its error therefore points almost entirely along its own viewing ray, and the calibration tells us that direction exactly. To address this, we introduce the Ray-Lateral Agreement (RLA), which follows one rule: trust the ray, not the depth. RLA first slides every estimate along its own rays, which removes the depth bias and leaves the shape untouched. It then fuses the views one point at a time, under a Gaussian error whose precision follows each viewing ray. The maximum-likelihood solution is closed-form, interpolates between averaging and midpoint triangulation, and stays well conditioned where triangulation is singular. Robustness is the harder half, because correct views also disagree along their rays. Any weight built on the full residual therefore punishes good views together with bad ones. Ray-lateral agreement instead scores every view, at every point, by its residual across that view's own ray alone. We evaluated RLA on multi-camera public benchmark datasets. Across two, three, and four cameras and three per-view pose estimators, RLA consistently achieves lower worst-case error than all triangulation baselines.



### Beyond the Sink Token: Sink-Guided Key Editing in Vision-Language Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23516v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23516v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 21:07:08+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 21:07:08+00:00
- **Authors**: Hui Zhu, Saleh Zare Zade, Rafi Ibn Sultan, Xiangyu Zhou, Dongxiao Zhu
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In vision-language models (VLMs), a few sink tokens attract a disproportionate share of attention. While previous studies have examined why these tokens attract attention, we investigate how the sink-key direction affects attention to image and question tokens. Across four probed VLMs, boosting the magnitude of positive sink-aligned components increases attention on average, whereas boosting negative components decreases it; these responses persist through later layers. This identifies the native sink key as a reference for controlling attention beyond its own position. Motivated by this finding, we propose SHRINK, a training-free method that partially reduces sink-aligned components across visible keys during generation. On nine benchmarks under sampled reasoning, it improves mean accuracy by 5.1 and 2.7 percentage points on LLaVA-OneVision-7B and Qwen2.5-VL-3B, with smaller gains on the other two main models, Qwen2.5-VL-7B and Gemma-3-4B. Beyond SHRINK, rescaling sink-aligned key components can replace the attention operations of two existing attention-steering methods, GIFT and ACG, with comparable accuracy on hallucination benchmarks.



### RageVLA: Augmentation-Trained Perception and Action for State-of-the-Art Robustness on LIBERO
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23517v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23517v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 21:07:47+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 21:07:47+00:00
- **Authors**: Amrit Lal Singh, Smarak Kanjilal
- **Comment**: 10 pages, 10 figures, 9 tables
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision-language-action (VLA) models look saturated on LIBERO, yet most of them fall apart on LIBERO-PRO as soon as an object moves or the instruction asks for something new. We argue that what is missing is not scale but decoupling.   In these policies, what to do is tied to where things were during training, to the scene around them and to the wording of the instruction. We cut these ties in two places. In the data, LIBERO demonstrations are re-arranged by general rules: every object and fixture is moved and rotated, tasks are carried into other environments and onto other surfaces, and every instruction is paraphrased. In the policy, a perception engine on frozen DINOv3 features, trained on this augmented data, hands the action model the scene in decoupled form: a list of objects with their positions, and a compact code of what the gripper is looking at, next to image and instruction embeddings from TinyCLIP. A flow-matching diffusion transformer, trained from scratch, acts on this view.   We call this combination of Robust Augmentation and Grounded Encoding RageVLA. With 0.21 billion parameters, RageVLA sets a new state of the art on LIBERO-PRO among VLA policies, scoring 0.72 against 0.53 for the best published model, and a larger variant reaches 0.73.   Two further tests on LIBERO-Object show that the policy acts on what it perceives. When the object positions it is given are shuffled while the camera images stay correct, its success falls from 0.965 to 0.215. When the target object is displaced by 28 cm, it still succeeds in 0.865 of the episodes, where $π_{0.5}$, run through the same harness, succeeds in 0.105.



### ORGANIC3D: Grounding 3D CT Vision Language Models in Organ Level Image Text Coupling
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23529v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23529v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 21:34:36+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 21:34:36+00:00
- **Authors**: Zelong Liu, Hamza Ahmed, Wynn Wingyi Lee, Mickael Torjman, Alexander Dimitrakakis, Jiahua Lu, Timothy Deyer, Bachir Taouli, Zahi A. Fayad, Xueyan Mei
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Vision language models (VLMs) have achieved strong performance in 3D computed tomography (CT) classification, but high accuracy does not reflect whether their predictions rely on the correct anatomical evidence. We introduce ORGANIC3D, a framework for evaluating and improving organ level image text alignment through controlled anatomical interventions. Our benchmark assesses 12 vision encoder checkpoints and 7 generative VLMs using matched patient comparisons, organ erasure, lesion replacement, and laterality perturbations, revealing substantial alignment failures even among top performing models. To learn organ level image text correspondence, ORGANIC3D aligns each finding sentence with the anatomy aware image features and trains on edited scans to anchor this alignment in the organ's image evidence. We further extend this framework to generative VLMs, promoting evidence dependent question answering and report generation. Trained exclusively on public data, ORGANIC3D achieves SOTA classification AUROC on CTRATE, Merlin, and the heldout RADChestCT dataset and reaches 77% laterality accuracy, compared with at most 66% for prior models. Its generative VLM matches or exceeds all evaluated publicly released VLMs on visual question answering, achieves the highest report accuracy, and retracts 96 to 99% of target organ findings after organ removal, versus a maximum of 69% for other models. Together, these results establish ORGANIC3D as a framework for testing whether 3D CT vision language models ground their predictions in anatomical evidence and for improving organ level alignment alongside predictive accuracy.



### Stability of Parallel Transport Convolution under Riemannian Metric Perturbations
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23543v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.CV, math.DG, stat.ML
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23543v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 21:50:20+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 21:50:20+00:00
- **Authors**: Hongnian Huang, Andrew Stump
- **Comment**: 78 pages. Comments welcome!
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Geometric convolutions on Riemannian manifolds depend on the geometry of the underlying domain, making stability under metric perturbations a fundamental question in geometric deep learning. This paper studies this question for parallel transport convolution through methods from differential geometry and analysis. On a fixed compact manifold, we establish that, for a continuous input signal and a continuous reference kernel supported in a sufficiently small normal neighborhood, the convolution output depends continuously in $L^2$ on the Riemannian metric in the $C^2$-topology, under the specified localization and transport-path conventions. The analysis develops perturbation estimates for geodesics, parallel transport, Jacobi fields, and exponential maps, and uses these estimates to compare the transported kernels. A central difficulty is the cut locus, which obstructs uniform comparison of the transport construction over the entire manifold. We address this difficulty by combining uniform estimates away from the cut locus with integral bounds on neighborhoods of arbitrarily small volume, while also controlling the change in the Riemannian volume measure. The resulting qualitative stability theorem connects perturbation theory for Riemannian geometric constructions with the analysis of spatial convolution operators in geometric deep learning.



### Layer-Matched Sparse Attention for Low-Redundancy 3D Reconstruction
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23554v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23554v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 22:01:43+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 22:01:43+00:00
- **Authors**: Sammam Mahdi, Fariha Binta Salim, K. M. Jubair Sami, Rakin Bin Rabbani
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: VGGT reconstructs cameras and geometry from unposed images in one pass, but its global attention grows quadratically with the number of views. Training-free accelerators thin this attention and work well on redundant video, but lose pose accuracy when views share little geometry. This paper traces these losses to two kinds of cross-view information that a uniform sparse approximation can fail to preserve together: diffuse layers aggregate distributed context, whereas matching layers depend on query-specific interactions that carry little attention mass. A per-layer statistic measures the log-mass missed by a mean token. Its row-free proxy is the logit variance, and it selects the estimator for each layer. Diffuse layers use shared, importance-sampled hub keys, with log-weights that make the softmax numerator and denominator unbiased. Matching layers stay exact: every aggressive thinning tested cost accuracy. At equal cost, the selected layers outperform the four shifted windows tested. On 13 unseen low-redundancy scenes with 32 views, LMSA stays within 1.4 AUC@5 of dense attention, compared with 10.3 for AVGGT and 4.5 for SAF3R; its difference from contemporaneous GPA-VGGT is not significant, and only its gains over AVGGT pass both scene-level tests. With 100 views, it stays within 1.9 AUC@5 while its aggregator runs $2.0\times$ faster. Used post hoc to choose the layers GPA-VGGT protects, the statistic improves it by 1.2 AUC@5 for 5% more FLOPs. Applied untuned to $π^3$, it beats AVGGT but remains further from dense accuracy.



### DOCK: Dual-Stream-Inspired Self-Supervised Object-Centric Representations Grounded in Robot Kinematics
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23578v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.RO
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23578v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 22:27:05+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 22:27:05+00:00
- **Authors**: Pengju Liu, Caiwen Ding
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Latent-space planners select actions by comparing the embedding of a predicted future image with that of a goal image. This assumes that distance in the embedding reflects how far the scene is from the goal. We profile this assumption with simulator-executed futures, which removes prediction error. For six frozen DINO and V-JEPA encoders, feature distance barely tracks the object's distance to its goal: the rank correlation is at most 0.11, against 0.87 for object-mask centroids. It is dominated by background and robot patches, and frozen-feature readouts capture the arm far better than the object. Inspired by the ventral (what) and dorsal (where/how) visual streams, we propose DOCK, which learns an object representation whose changes agree with measured physical motion. A grasped object moves with the gripper, so forward kinematics on the joint angles gives a proxy for the object's displacement without annotation. From 9,951 held-object transport trajectories in 21 simulated kitchen scenes, we mine 404K displacement pairs and train three components: an object token pooled from patch features (what), a spatial ruler that makes differences of readout coordinates equal to measured displacements (where), and a dynamics predictor over object tokens (how). Used as a planning cost, the learned representation places the object in 15 of 21 tasks, as many as an oracle that reads the true object position, while mask geometry reaches 10 and frozen feature distance 9. It still solves the most tasks when the unseen robot arm is visible, and its ranking transfers to held-out layouts with unseen object instances and categories without any adaptation.



### ExiST: Existence-Aware Self-Training for Semi-Supervised Multi-Organ Segmentation
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23581v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23581v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 22:27:46+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 22:27:46+00:00
- **Authors**: Maxx Richard Rahman, Prakhar Kumar, Wolfgang Maass
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Semi-supervised multi-organ segmentation should learn more than a dozen organs from a handful of labeled scans, and the smallest organs occupy less than one percent of the foreground. We identify a failure mode of self-training that is specific to this regime. When a teacher misses a small organ in a scan, every voxel of that organ receives the confident pseudo-label background, and the student is trained toward the organ absence. Repeated over many scans, this loop extinguishes tail organs. In a pilot study, removing the gallbladder from the pseudo-labels reduced its Dice on AMOS from 36.5 to 1.0. We argue that a missing organ should be treated as unresolved rather than absent and propose Existence-aware Self-Training (ExiST). It builds pseudo-labels by leave-one-out consensus of independently trained networks, screens them with a quality gate on validation scans, and infers, per scan and per organ, whether the organ was found. For every unresolved organ, the background voxels of that scan receive the set-valued target, trained with a marginal cross-entropy that accepts either answer and a Dice term that skips uncertain voxels. We show that this loss never pushes a candidate organ toward absence and never favors one member of the set, which leaves the decision to labeled evidence. ExiST outperforms the current state-of-the-art methods and reaches 77.1 mean Dice on Synapse with 20% labeled scans and 75.9 on AMOS with 5% labeled scans.



### GRAFT: Graph-Regularized Prototype Fields for Realistic Test-Time Adaptation of VLMs
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23591v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23591v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 22:46:15+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 22:46:15+00:00
- **Authors**: Maxx Richard Rahman, Wolfgang Maass
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Unlabeled test data can improve the zero-shot classifier of a vision-language model, yet transductive and test-time adaptation methods often fall below the zero-shot baseline when only a few vocabulary classes are present in a batch or when streams are correlated. We trace this failure to a shared design: every class owns independent parameters estimated from its own samples, so absent or rare classes are left at biased text anchors or fitted to foreign samples. We propose Graph-Regularized Anchor-displacement Field Transduction (GRAFT), which models the adapted prototypes as text anchors plus a displacement field that is partially pooled over a semantic class graph through a Gaussian Markov random field prior, together with an explicit class-presence model. Inference is closed-form and gradient-free on cached features. Anchor-only shrinkage and a single global shift are the two extreme settings of the prior; absent classes are provably harmonic extensions of the present ones, and the pooling strength can be chosen by empirical Bayes. Under the realistic batch and online protocols on eleven benchmarks and four ImageNet shifts, GRAFT improves over the zero-shot model in every setting and over anchor-based transduction where few classes are present.



### Entropy-Regularized Integrated Gradients for Stable Feature Attribution
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23600v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.LG**, cs.AI, cs.CV
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23600v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 22:53:17+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 22:53:17+00:00
- **Authors**: Rukmangadh Sai Myana, Sumit Kumar Jha, Yanzhao Wu
- **Comment**: 36 pages, 19 figures, 27 tables, including the appendix
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Path-based attribution methods like Integrated Gradients (IG) are widely used to interpret neural networks, yet imperceptible input perturbations can drastically alter their attribution maps while leaving model predictions unchanged. We introduce Entropy-Regularized Integrated Gradients (ERIG), a path-based attribution method that intrinsically attenuates gradient irregularities along the integration path using maximum-entropy softmax weighting with temperature parameter $τ$. We quantify the effect of the entropy-regularized weighting on attribution stability by using the Lipschitz properties of the model and its gradients. We decompose the attribution change under an input perturbation into a model-response term, whose bound is $τ$-independent, and a weight-variation term, whose bound decreases at rate $\mathcal{O}(1/(2τ))$. Their ratio $R(τ)$ characterizes the trade-off between gradient information and attribution stability. We further add a new dimension to attribution evaluation by adapting classical noise estimators into two metrics, LINV-C and LINV-P, that quantify attribution noise. Across eleven CNN and Transformer backbones on ImageNet and Oxford-IIIT Pet datasets, our experiments show that, relative to the next-best method, ERIG remains comparable on ranking-based fidelity metrics while improving localization by approximately 24% and attribution stability by approximately 47-fold on average, reaching up to four orders of magnitude improvement over IG, all at negligible additional computational cost.



### VIBRA: Backward-Compatible Cross-Modal Retrieval with Consensus Barycenters
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23606v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.IR
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23606v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 22:59:52+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 22:59:52+00:00
- **Authors**: Shubing Yang, Xutong Liu, Dongfang Zhao
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: Backward-compatible retrieval enables a new encoder to query the gallery that an old model has already encoded, so that a cross-modal retrieval system can be upgraded without re-encoding billions of items. Existing compatible methods either fit the old query and stay near the accuracy of the old model, or run the old and the new encoder for every query. We propose VIBRA, which matches relevance on the old gallery, corrects the errors of a single model with the barycenter of several views, which is itself a query in the old space, and trains the new encoder to inherit this consensus, so that models absent at deployment still shape every query. We show that the barycenter gains margin over the average of its views exactly when their hardest irrelevant items differ, and we characterize the share of this gain that one encoder can reach. On five benchmarks with five upgrades among six CLIP, MetaCLIP and SigLIP2 backbones, VIBRA is the best compatible method in all 50 settings, improving Recall@1 by up to 5.6 points over the strongest baseline and by up to 18.0 points over the old model, while adding less than 1 ms to the latency of the new encoder.



### Weight Space Programming for Vision Language Model Repair
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23615v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.CV**, cs.AI
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23615v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 23:07:36+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 23:07:36+00:00
- **Authors**: Avinash Goutham Aluguvelly
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: In weight-space programming, a language model edits a network's weights directly, without gradients. We ask what a successful repair of this kind relies on. We plant faults in the weights of two vision-language models, SmolVLM-256M and LFM2-VL-450M, and check each edit made by a coding agent against the original weights. Without being told where to look, the agent finds rescaled rows in the projector and noise in the vision encoder of both models, infers the unknown scale factors, restores most or all of the rescaled weights to their exact original values, and returns accuracy to near its original level; programs that it writes from such weights repair unseen checkpoints of the same model without feedback. These repairs depend on a trace of how the faults were computed. The original weights have 16-bit precision, and faults computed in 32-bit arithmetic leave low bits that only the damaged weights carry. Rounding those bits away leaves each model's answers to our test questions unchanged, and the agent, unaided, then restores no weight exactly: on SmolVLM it no longer finds the damage, and in a single session on LFM2-VL its only gain comes from rescaling answer-token embeddings, which changes how often the model answers yes. Marking the damaged weights again in their low bits brings the edits back to them. Rounded weights still hold statistical evidence of the rescaling, in the distribution of stored values and in row norms, and one sentence in the prompt that names such a statistic directs the agent's edits to the damaged rows on both models. Swapped rows or columns, which leave the set of stored values unchanged, are not found unaided on either model within our session budgets. We recommend that studies of weight repair report the number format of the weights and include a rounded control.



### Isthmus: Bridging Supervision Islands Restores Uncertainty and Stability in One-Step Flows
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23616v1

- **Authors**: Luming Yang, Siqing Li, Haoxian Liu, Zhenyu Bu, Jiazheng Zhu, Alper Yilmaz
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: One-step flow maps are increasingly used as posterior samplers, where the point of sampling is the uncertainty that the samples carry. We find that the one-step sampler loses this uncertainty and becomes less stable. Compared with multi-step samples of the same network, its samples lose variance and decorrelate. Supervising the one-step jump directly makes this worse; the samples collapse further toward the conditional mean and break down when training is extended. We trace both failures to the locations where training places its pairs of start and end times. Only pairs on the diagonal of the time triangle receive targets from data, and the pair used for one-step sampling is either left unsupervised or supervised on an island, a set of pairs that the self-consistency identity does not connect to the diagonal, where the objective neither determines the one-step map nor anchors its training. We bridge the island with an isthmus, which places 5% of the training pairs along the whole edge of the triangle that joins the one-step pair to the diagonal. The error of the one-step map is then bounded by the self-consistency residual along this edge. With the network, the loss and the inference code unchanged and at no added cost, the isthmus improves one-step sampling across several flow-map objectives, in MRI reconstruction, face inpainting, and unconditional generation on CIFAR-10 and ImageNet 32x32. On brain MRI, it restores one-step calibration to 0.96 and the correlation with multi-step samples to 0.93; both vary far less across seeds, and the agreement holds when training is extended to three times its length.



### How Uncertain Is My Action? Self-Correcting Visual Planning with Frozen World Models
- **Arxiv ID**: http://arxiv.org/abs/2611.23620v1
- **DOI**: None
- **Categories**: **cs.RO**, cs.CV, cs.LG
- **Links**: [PDF](https://arxiv.org/pdf/2611.23620v1)
- **Published**: 2026-11-17 23:23:42+00:00
- **Updated**: 2026-11-17 23:23:42+00:00
- **Authors**: Siqing Li, Luming Yang, Yijia Jiao, Junze Deng, Alper Yilmaz
- **Comment**: None
- **Journal**: None
- **Summary**: A frozen latent world model combined with sampling-based model predictive control is a visual planner. At deployment, the executed action can depart from the commanded one, and the frozen model has no way to notice. We show that the model can be run in reverse: for any hypothesized actuation shift, it predicts what it should have observed under that shift, and comparing this prediction with what was actually observed quantifies the shift and lets the planner correct itself. Unlike the residual energy, which mixes the shift with the model's own error, the score keeps only the part of the residual that a shift can produce. This shift score detects the shift with a locally optimal test, estimates it with Cramer-Rao error bars, names the dominant generator, and compensates the planner once an alarm fires. On PushT, TwoRoom and OGBench-Cube with a frozen latent world model, measured detection follows the prediction to within 0.05 AUROC. Self-correction raises success from 0.650 to 0.862 on PushT and from 0.378 to 0.432 on the harder Cube task, levels with an oracle that is told the true shift at the same block, better than five common test-time adaptation methods. On a real robotic arm, the score identifies a base-joint offset that no joint encoder registers and reduces the reach error from 36 mm to 3-5 mm, and under shifts drawn at random and sealed before the session, estimates recover 92-94% of the error reduction achieved with the true shift. The broader implication is that a frozen world model can act as a filter by predicting the future, testing action-interface uncertainty and validity, and finally correcting failures.
